Error Atlas

Every NVMe and PCIe failure you can find in a PEX trace, and the ones you can't: the errors that come with a status code, and the many that don't. Timeouts, silent data problems, commands that "succeed" without doing their job, link degradation and specification violations, each with how to detect and prove it.

PART I

How Failures Show Themselves

This atlas answers one question for every NVMe and PCIe failure: can you find it in a PEX trace (a Teledyne LeCroy PCIe analyzer capture), and if not, what else proves it? An error code is only one of four ways a failure can appear. Many of the most expensive NVMe and PCIe bugs never produce one: the command "succeeds", nothing is logged, and the trace looks normal. This part sets out the four classes, where the evidence for each lives, and how to use the rest of the atlas.

1 The Four Ways a Failure Shows Itself

2 Master Catalog →

1.1 Why error codes are not enough

The NVMe specification defines a status code for every command, and PCIe defines an Advanced Error Reporting (AER) bit for every error a link or transaction layer detects. It is tempting to think that if neither reports anything, nothing went wrong. That is false in three common ways:

  • Something took too long. A command that never completes has no status code at all, because there is no completion to carry one. Only a timer notices: the PCIe Completion Timeout in the device or root port, or the host driver's command timeout (30 seconds by default in Linux v7.2 for I/O).
  • Something "succeeded" without doing its job. A Flush that returns success while data is still only in a volatile cache, or a Deallocate (TRIM) that returns success while the blocks stay mapped. The command, the data and a clean completion are all on the wire, so a protocol analyzer shows nothing wrong. The failure happens inside the device, where no trace can see it.
  • Everything works, just worse. A Gen5 x4 link that trained at Gen3 x2, or a drive that throttles under heat. No error is raised anywhere; the only symptom is a number that is lower than it should be.

1.2 The four classes

Every card in this atlas carries one of four labels, shown in its header. The master catalog at the end of this part lists every card with its label.

LabelMeaningWho noticesIn a PEX traceExample
ReportedThe device or link raises an explicit error: a non-zero CQE status, an AER status bit, a log entry, an asynchronous event, CSTS.CFS.Software, automaticallyUsually visible: the error travels on the link (CQE status in a write, UR/CA completion status, ERR_* message)Unrecovered Read Error (SCT 2h, SC 81h); Unsupported Request in AER
Timer onlyNothing is reported; a timer expires because an expected event never arrived in time.A watchdog: device completion timer, host command timer, keep-alive timerVisible as an absence: a request with no answer, measured with timestampsDoorbell rung, command never completes; controller never sets CSTS.RDY within CAP.TO
SilentEverything reports success. The device did not do what the command asked, or returned wrong data.Only a deliberate check: read-back, checksum, utilization counters, power-cycle testUsually not visible: the trace shows a clean command and success. At best it shows a later clue, such as wrong data in a readDeallocate of the last 5 GB completes but nothing is unmapped; Flush acknowledged before data is durable
DegradedIt works, but below its capability or its specification. No error anywhere.Only a comparison against expectationOften visible: link speed and width in the training sequence, gaps and throughput in the timelineLink trained at lower speed or width; thermal throttling; p99.9 latency far above the SLA

1.2a Three verdicts for a PEX trace

Every card starts with a verdict on whether a PEX capture of the link can show the failure:

  • Visible The failure itself is on the wire: a bad status, a malformed packet, a missing completion, a timing gap, a link that retrained.
  • Partly visible The trace shows the command or a symptom, but not the proof. You need one more piece of evidence, such as a register read or a read-back.
  • Not visible Nothing on the link is wrong. The failure is inside the device, on another link, or in host memory that never crossed the probed link. The card names the evidence that does prove it.

Part I-B explains what a PEX capture records and what it fundamentally cannot record. The master catalog below lists the verdict for every failure.

1.3 The trim example in one paragraph

📘 Write 10 GB, then trim the last 5 GB: why the PEX trace shows nothing wrong

When a file is truncated, the filesystem can ask Linux to discard the freed blocks. The NVMe driver sends a Dataset Management command with the Deallocate attribute (or Write Zeroes with the Deallocate bit), listing the LBA ranges. On the wire you see the command, the range list being fetched by the device, and a successful completion. Whether the controller actually unmapped those LBAs happens inside the SSD, so no analyzer can see it, and the specification treats deallocation as something the controller may or may not carry out in full (verify the exact wording for your revision). A failure here is Silent. It can only be proven indirectly: reading back the range and comparing against the drive's advertised read behaviour (DLFEAT), enabling the Deallocated or Unwritten Logical Block error, or watching the namespace utilization counter (NUSE) before and after. Part IV works through this example step by step.

1.4 Where the evidence lives

Each tool sees one slice of the system. Knowing what a tool cannot see is as important as knowing what it can.

Evidence sourceCan showCannot show
CQE status (per command)Every error the controller chooses to report for that commandCommands that never complete; work the controller skipped but reported as success
NVMe logs: Error Information (01h), SMART / Health (02h), Firmware Slot (03h), Persistent Event, TelemetryHistory of reported errors, wear, temperature, critical warnings, media errors, unsafe shutdownsAnything the firmware does not count; exact timing of events
PCIe AER registers (lspci -vvv, kernel AER log)Link, data-link and transaction-layer errors with severity; header log for some uncorrectable errorsNVMe-level semantics; silent data errors; degraded speed
Kernel log (dmesg, journalctl -k)Host timeouts, aborts, resets, AER reports, quirks applied, link-speed warningsWhat happened on the wire between log lines; device internals
Protocol analyzer / PEX captureEvery TLP and DLLP on the link with timestamps: ordering, timing, missing completions, protocol rule violationsWhat the controller did internally (media, cache, mapping tables); anything on another link
Read-back and data verification (fio verify, checksums, PI)Wrong, stale, torn or lost data; whether a trim or erase took effectWhy it happened
Performance counters (fio, iostat, perf)Throughput, IOPS, latency percentiles, CPU costRoot cause by themselves
Register reads (lspci LnkCap/LnkSta, nvme show-regs)Current link speed and width, controller state (CC, CSTS), capabilitiesHistory: a link that dropped and recovered may look fine now

1.5 How to use this atlas

  • Debugging a symptom: find the card whose "How it shows up" matches what you see, then follow "How to detect / prove".
  • Writing a test plan: walk the master catalog and make sure every Silent and Degraded card has a test, because nothing else will catch those.
  • Reading a trace: Part VII lists the rule violations a protocol checker, or a script over an exported PEX capture, can flag.
  • Working with an AI assistant: Part IX shows what to hand over (exports, logs, register dumps) and what an assistant can and cannot conclude.
⚠️ Sources and limits

The cards cite the Linux kernel v7.2 NVMe and PCI source, QEMU's NVMe controller model and libnvme's type definitions, which were read directly, plus public research where cited. The NVMe and PCIe specifications themselves could not be downloaded from this environment, so spec wording is paraphrased and anything not confirmed in those sources is tagged uncertain or "verify in spec". Kernel behaviour described here is Linux v7.2's and can differ in other versions or operating systems.

PART II

Errors That Come With a Code

This part covers the easy failures: the drive admits something went wrong. It gives a status code in the Completion Queue Entry (CQE), raises an Asynchronous Event, sets a bit in a log page, or sets the fatal bit in a controller register. Each failure gets an error card that says what the device means by it, why it usually happens (using the places where the QEMU NVMe model returns it), and what Linux v7.2 does with it. Most of the hard bugs are near these codes: a driver that ignores the More bit, a firmware that sets DNR when it should not, a retry loop that hides a dying drive. Reading the code correctly is step one. Chapter 3 covers the status field and the Linux code path. Chapters 4–6 hold the per-code cards, Chapter 7 is the complete code table, and Chapter 8 covers failures reported through logs, events and registers instead of a CQE.

3 The Status Field: Anatomy and the Linux Path

← 2 Master Catalog4 Generic Command Status (SCT 0) →
📌 Four ways to show a failure (recap)

Part I describes them in detail. In short, an NVMe failure reaches the host in one of four ways: (1) a non-zero status in a completion (this part), (2) no completion at all, so only a host timer notices (Part on timing), (3) a successful completion with the wrong effect (silent data problems), or (4) correct results delivered slowly or over a degraded link. Category 1 also includes failures reported out of band: Asynchronous Event Request (AER) completions, log pages and the CSTS register. Chapter 8 covers those.

3.1 Why this chapter exists

A failed command returns 15 bits of status, and engineers misread them all the time. Common mistakes: decoding SC without SCT (0x02 is "Invalid Field" under SCT 0 but "Invalid Queue Size" under SCT 1), treating DNR as a severity, ignoring the More bit and never reading the Error Information log, or forgetting that the host driver invents some status codes itself (0x370, 0x371) that never appeared on the PCIe link. The cards in the following chapters assume you can decode the field without hesitating and know which branch of the Linux completion path a code takes.

  • Decode a CQE status word into Phase, SC, SCT, CRD, More and DNR, both in raw CQE layout and in the Linux shifted layout.
  • Predict whether Linux v7.2 retries, fails over, re-authenticates or completes a failed request, and which errno user space sees.
  • Find the exact dmesg line, sysfs counter and tracepoint that record each failed command.
  • Use the kernel's NVMe fault injection to test the host's handling of any status code without a misbehaving drive.

3.2 Where the status lives

A CQE is 16 bytes verified. Dword 0 (and in newer specs Dword 1) holds the command-specific result, DW2 holds SQ Head Pointer (SQHD) and SQ Identifier (SQID), and DW3 holds the Command Identifier (CID, bits 15:0), the Phase Tag (bit 16) and the 15-bit Status Field (bits 31:17). Linux's struct nvme_completion in include/linux/nvme.h declares DW3 as __u16 command_id; __le16 status;. So the 16-bit status includes the phase tag in bit 0, and the driver shifts it off.

CQE DW3 bit313029:2827:2524:171615:0
FieldDNRM (More)CRDSCTSCP (Phase)CID
bit in cqe.status (16-bit)151413:1211:98:10—
bit in nvme_req(req)->status (after >> 1)141312:1110:87:0dropped—
Linux maskNVME_STATUS_DNR 0x4000NVME_STATUS_MORE 0x2000NVME_STATUS_CRD 0x1800NVME_SCT_MASK 0x0700NVME_SC_MASK 0x00ff——

The shift happens in drivers/nvme/host/nvme.h:nvme_try_complete_req(): rq->status = le16_to_cpu(status) >> 1;. From that point on, every Linux constant (NVME_SC_*, NVME_SCT_*) is written in the shifted layout. The Generic codes are 0x000–0x0FF, Command Specific 0x100–0x1FF, Media 0x200–0x2FF and Path 0x300–0x3FF. This is why include/linux/nvme.h lists NVME_SC_WRITE_FAULT = 0x280: SCT 2, SC 0x80. libnvme's types.h instead lists the SC alone (NVME_SC_WRITE_FAULT = 0x80) and the SCT separately (NVME_SCT_MEDIA = 0x2, shift 8). Always check which convention a tool prints. nvme-cli and the Linux log line print SCT and SC as separate numbers. The QEMU trace and Linux's internal constants use the combined form.

3.3 The five sub-fields

FieldWidthMeaningWho acts on it
SC — Status Code8The specific reason. 0x00 with SCT 0 = success. Meaning depends on SCT.Everyone. Only meaningful together with SCT.
SCT — Status Code Type30 Generic Command Status, 1 Command Specific Status, 2 Media and Data Integrity Errors, 3 Path Related Status, 4–6 reserved, 7 Vendor Specific (libnvme NVME_SCT_VS = 0x7).Linux checks SCT 3 explicitly (nvme_is_path_error()) to choose multipath failover.
CRD — Command Retry Delay20 = no delay. 1–3 select CRDT1–CRDT3 from Identify Controller. Each is a time in units of 100 ms. Used only if the host enabled Advanced Command Retry Enable (ACRE) in the Host Behavior Support feature.Linux nvme_retry_req(): delay = ctrl->crdt[crd - 1] * 100 ms before kicking the requeue list.
M — More1More status information for this command is available in the Error Information log page (LID 01h).Linux only prints "MORE" in the error line. It never reads LID 01h itself (no use of NVME_LOG_ERROR in drivers/nvme/host/).
DNR — Do Not Retry1If the same command is submitted again to any controller in the NVM subsystem, it is expected to fail (libnvme wording).Linux nvme_decide_disposition(): DNR set → COMPLETE (no retry, no failover).
⚠️ DNR is a prediction, not a severity

DNR=0 does not mean "minor", and DNR=1 does not mean "fatal". An Unrecovered Read Error with DNR=0 tells the host "try again, a re-read might succeed". The data is still at risk. An Invalid Field with DNR=1 tells the host "your command is wrong". Nothing is broken on the drive. Firmware that clears DNR on deterministic errors makes Linux resend the same bad command up to max_retries (default 5) times. Firmware that sets DNR on transient errors makes Linux fail I/O that would have succeeded on retry. Both are real conformance bugs. Check DNR on every error a drive under test returns.

3.4 What Linux v7.2 does with a non-zero status

All transports (PCIe, RDMA, TCP, FC, loop) reach the same core path. The diagram below follows a completion from the CQE to the errno.

3.4.1 nvme_decide_disposition() — the one function that decides

/* drivers/nvme/host/core.c (v7.2), abridged */
static inline enum nvme_disposition nvme_decide_disposition(struct request *req)
{
	if (likely(nvme_req(req)->status == 0))
		return COMPLETE;
	if (blk_noretry_request(req) ||
	    (nvme_req(req)->status & NVME_STATUS_DNR) ||
	    nvme_req(req)->retries >= nvme_max_retries)
		return COMPLETE;
	if ((nvme_req(req)->status & NVME_SCT_SC_MASK) == NVME_SC_AUTH_REQUIRED)
		return AUTHENTICATE;
	if (req->cmd_flags & REQ_NVME_MPATH) {
		if (nvme_is_path_error(nvme_req(req)->status) ||
		    blk_queue_dying(req->q))
			return FAILOVER;
	} else {
		if (blk_queue_dying(req->q))
			return COMPLETE;
	}
	return RETRY;
}

Consequences worth remembering verified:

  • Linux does not look at the code when deciding to retry. Any non-zero status without DNR is retried up to nvme_core.max_retries (module parameter, default 5), unless the request is marked no-retry (blk_noretry_request(), i.e. a REQ_FAILFAST_* flag). So for retries, the device's DNR choice is the policy.
  • Path errors (SCT 3) fail over to another path only on a multipath namespace head (REQ_NVME_MPATH). On a single-path device they are plain retries.
  • Older kernels had a helper named nvme_req_needs_retry(). It does not exist in v7.2. Its logic is now inside nvme_decide_disposition().
  • Admin and passthrough requests use the same function, but most are submitted with flags that make them no-retry. If you see an admin error, assume it was not retried.
  • nvme_retry_req() increments nvme_req(req)->retries and the per-namespace ns->retries (sysfs command_retries_count), then calls blk_mq_delay_kick_requeue_list() with the CRD-derived delay.

3.4.2 nvme_error_status() — status to block-layer error

Only a few codes get a specific blk_status_t. Everything else becomes BLK_STS_IOERR → -EIO. The errno column comes from the blk_errors[] table in block/blk-core.c.

NVMe status (Linux constant, shifted value)blk_status_terrnoblk_print_req_error text
NVME_SC_SUCCESS 0x000BLK_STS_OK0—
NVME_SC_CAP_EXCEEDED 0x081BLK_STS_NOSPC-ENOSPCcritical space allocation
NVME_SC_LBA_RANGE 0x080, NVME_SC_CMD_INTERRUPTED 0x021, NVME_SC_NS_NOT_READY 0x082BLK_STS_TARGET-EREMOTEIOcritical target
NVME_SC_BAD_ATTRIBUTES 0x180, NVME_SC_INVALID_OPCODE 0x001, NVME_SC_INVALID_FIELD 0x002, NVME_SC_INVALID_NS 0x00BBLK_STS_NOTSUPP-EOPNOTSUPPoperation not supported
NVME_SC_WRITE_FAULT 0x280, NVME_SC_READ_ERROR 0x281, NVME_SC_UNWRITTEN_BLOCK 0x287, NVME_SC_ACCESS_DENIED 0x286, NVME_SC_READ_ONLY 0x182, NVME_SC_COMPARE_FAILED 0x285BLK_STS_MEDIUM-ENODATAcritical medium
NVME_SC_GUARD_CHECK 0x282, NVME_SC_APPTAG_CHECK 0x283, NVME_SC_REFTAG_CHECK 0x284, NVME_SC_INVALID_PI 0x181BLK_STS_PROTECTION-EILSEQprotection
NVME_SC_RESERVATION_CONFLICT 0x083BLK_STS_RESV_CONFLICT-EBADEreservation conflict
NVME_SC_HOST_PATH_ERROR 0x370BLK_STS_TRANSPORT-ENOLINKrecoverable transport
NVME_SC_ZONE_TOO_MANY_ACTIVE 0x1BDBLK_STS_ZONE_ACTIVE_RESOURCE-EOVERFLOWactive zones exceeded

everything else (Internal Error, Data Transfer Error, aborts, ANA codes, Host Aborted 0x371, all other zone codes, vendor-specific)BLK_STS_IOERR-EIOI/O

Note that the switch masks with NVME_SCT_SC_MASK, so CRD/More/DNR do not affect the mapping. Also, BLK_STS_NOTSUPP for Invalid Field means a Write Zeroes or Discard the drive rejects becomes "operation not supported". blk-lib callers use this to fall back, which can hide an Invalid Field caused by a host bug as "feature absent". derived

3.4.3 What gets printed

nvme_log_error() (rate-limited pr_err) runs for every failing non-passthrough request that does not carry RQF_QUIET:

/* I/O command on a namespace (format string from v7.2 core.c) */
"%s: %s(0x%x) @ LBA %llu, %u blocks, %s (sct 0x%x / sc 0x%x) %s%s\n"
/* admin command (no namespace) */
"%s: %s(0x%x), %s (sct 0x%x / sc 0x%x) %s%s\n"
/* passthrough (nvme_log_err_passthru) adds cdw10..cdw15 */

# illustrative, abbreviated (synthetic values):
nvme0n1: Read(0x2) @ LBA 1048576, 8 blocks, Unrecovered Read Error (sct 0x2 / sc 0x81) DNR
critical medium error, dev nvme0n1, sector 8388608 op 0x0:(READ) flags 0x0 phys_seg 1 prio class 2

The text "Unrecovered Read Error" comes from nvme_get_error_status_str() in drivers/nvme/host/constants.c, which indexes nvme_statuses[] with status & NVME_SCT_SC_MASK and returns "Unknown" for gaps. The second line comes from the block layer (block/blk-mq.c:blk_print_req_error()). Note that it reports a 512-byte sector, not an LBA. Two quirks of the string table derived:

  • 0x104 (NVME_SC_ABORT_MISSING) is printed as "Reserved" (the source carries an /* XXX */ comment).
  • Fabrics Connect codes share the numbers 0x180–0x184 with NVM command-set codes. The table only has the NVM names, so a Fabrics status decoded by this function shows the NVM text (0x182 → "Attempted Write to Read Only Range"). Connect failures are logged separately by drivers/nvme/host/fabrics.c:nvmf_log_connect_error(), which uses its own wording.

The log line appears only when the request finally completes with an error. A command that failed twice and then succeeded on the third retry prints nothing. Only command_retries_count and the tracepoint record it. This is a common way intermittent drive errors stay invisible.

3.4.4 Counters and tracepoints

WhereWhat it countsSource
/sys/block/nvmeXnY/command_retries_count (per-namespace device attribute; exact path depends on multipath layout)Every nvme_retry_req() on that namespacesysfs.c:command_retries_count_show
namespace command_error_countRequests that ended with non-zero status (logged path)sysfs.c:nvme_io_errors_show, incremented in __nvme_end_req()
controller command_error_countSame, for admin requests (no ns)sysfs.c:nvme_adm_errors_show
namespace multipath_failover_countEach nvme_failover_req()multipath.c
tracepoint nvme:nvme_complete_rqEvery completion: qid, cmdid, result, retries, flags, statusdrivers/nvme/host/trace.h
tracepoint nvme:nvme_async_eventEvery AEN result dword, decoded typetrace.h, called from nvme_complete_async_event()
# Catch every non-zero NVMe completion, including ones later retried successfully
echo 'status != 0' > /sys/kernel/tracing/events/nvme/nvme_complete_rq/filter
echo 1 > /sys/kernel/tracing/events/nvme/nvme_complete_rq/enable
echo 1 > /sys/kernel/tracing/events/nvme/nvme_async_event/enable
cat /sys/kernel/tracing/trace_pipe
# the 'status=' value printed is the shifted Linux layout (DNR=0x4000, More=0x2000)

3.4.5 Passthrough and ioctl users see the raw status

For NVME_IOCTL_ADMIN_CMD / NVME_IOCTL_IO_CMD (what nvme-cli uses), the ioctl return value is the positive NVMe status (shifted layout) when the device fails the command, and a negative errno for host-side failures (drivers/nvme/host/ioctl.c). For io_uring passthrough, nvme_uring_cmd_end_io() places nvme_req(req)->status in the CQE result and only falls back to blk_status_to_errno() when the status is zero but the block layer reported an error. This is why nvme-cli can print "NVMe status: Invalid Field in Command(0x2)" while a filesystem would only see EOPNOTSUPP.

3.5 Host-generated statuses — codes that were never on the wire

Two path-related codes are mostly synthesised by the host driver. In a protocol-analyzer capture there is no CQE carrying them.

  • NVME_SC_HOST_PATH_ERROR (0x370): set by nvme_host_path_error() when a transport cannot even submit (e.g. the controller is not live). Mapped to BLK_STS_TRANSPORT / -ENOLINK.
  • NVME_SC_HOST_ABORTED_CMD (0x371): set by nvme_cancel_request() for every in-flight request when the driver tears down queues (reset, removal, timeout recovery). Not DNR, so it is retried or failed over. If the retry budget runs out or the queue is dying, the application sees -EIO.

Likewise NVMe fault injection (drivers/nvme/host/fault_inject.c, built with CONFIG_FAULT_INJECTION_DEBUG_FS) overwrites nvme_req(req)->status inside nvme_try_complete_req(). The drive really returned success, and the trace shows a good CQE.

🧪 Lab 3.1 — Make Linux see any status code you choose CAUTION

Objective: exercise the host's error handling for a specific status (e.g. 0x281 Unrecovered Read Error) without a faulty drive.

⚠️ Safety

Injected write failures make filesystems go read-only or remount, and can corrupt a mounted filesystem's view. Use a scratch namespace or a QEMU guest. The drive's media is never touched.

Setup: kernel with CONFIG_FAULT_INJECTION_DEBUG_FS=y, debugfs mounted. Per-namespace directory: /sys/kernel/debug/nvme0n1/fault_inject/ (the parent is named after the disk or controller device).

cd /sys/kernel/debug/nvme0n1/fault_inject
echo 0x281 > status        # SCT 2 / SC 0x81, shifted Linux layout
echo 1     > dont_retry    # ORs NVME_STATUS_DNR into the injected status
echo 100   > probability
echo 1     > times
dd if=/dev/nvme0n1 of=/dev/null bs=4k count=1 iflag=direct
dmesg | tail -3            # expect the nvme_log_error line + "critical medium error"
echo 0 > dont_retry; echo 3 > times   # now observe retries in command_retries_count

Observations: with dont_retry=1, dd fails at once with "No data available" (ENODATA). With dont_retry=0 and times < 6, the read succeeds after retries, nothing is printed, and command_retries_count goes up.

Solution / explanation

The injected status passes through nvme_decide_disposition() exactly like a real one. DNR → COMPLETE → nvme_error_status() maps 0x281 to BLK_STS_MEDIUM → -ENODATA. Without DNR, the request is requeued and the next attempt succeeds because times is used up. This is the "retry hides the error" pattern from §3.4.3.

Extension: inject 0x302 (ANA Inaccessible) on one path of a multipath namespace and watch multipath_failover_count.

3.6 How the device side decides — the QEMU reference model

The QEMU NVMe controller (hw/nvme/ctrl.c) is the most readable open model of when a controller returns each status. Its checks are a useful baseline for "typical cause" impl. Real firmware has many more error sources (media, power, thermal), but argument validation usually looks very similar. Status names in QEMU's include/block/nvme.h differ from Linux's (e.g. NVME_DATA_TRAS_ERROR, NVME_INTERNAL_DEV_ERROR, NVME_DULB), and its values use the same combined SCT/SC layout as Linux. A static scan of return sites in QEMU's ctrl.c shows how lopsided real error traffic is: NVME_INVALID_FIELD | NVME_DNR is returned from about 86 places, NVME_INVALID_NSID | NVME_DNR from about 15, and everything else from a handful derived from grep, approximate.

QEMU has a few useful behaviours for testers verified in source:

  • Backend I/O failure is turned into a media status in nvme_rw_complete_cb(): reads → NVME_UNRECOVERED_READ, writes / Write Zeroes / Zone Append → NVME_WRITE_FAULT. A host-side blkdebug rule therefore gives a real SCT 2 error on the emulated wire.
  • Its Error Information log (nvme_error_info()) always returns zeroed entries, so QEMU never sets More meaningfully.
  • Its Abort (nvme_abort()) only cancels outstanding AERs, plus queued backend AIO on a best-effort basis, and otherwise completes with result bit 0 = 1 ("not aborted").
  • SMART critical warnings can be injected at run time through the QOM property smart_critical_warning (nvme_set_smart_warning()), which raises the matching SMART AEN if the host enabled it.

3.7 A synthetic trace of the retry pattern

Synthetic trace — transient read error retried by Linux, then success (no dmesg line)
#t (µs)DirEventDetail
10.0H→DMWr SQ1 tail doorbellRead, CID 0x0041, SLBA 0x100000, NLB 7
21.2D→HMRd SQE / CplDdevice fetches 64-byte SQE
3412.0D→HMWr CQE (16 B)CID 0x0041, SCT 2, SC 0x81, DNR=0, M=1, CRD=0 — no data MWr preceded it
4413.0D→HMSI-X MWrvector 1
5420.5H→DMWr CQ1 head doorbelldriver consumed CQE; nvme_decide_disposition → RETRY
6431.0H→DMWr SQ1 tail doorbellsame Read re-submitted, new CID 0x1041 (genctr bumped in CID upper bits)
7560.0D→HMWr data ×8, MWr CQEstatus 0 — success; application never sees an error

What to notice: the only host-visible evidence is the tracepoint and command_retries_count. The More bit in row 3 says the drive stored detail in LID 01h, and Linux will never fetch it. A validation harness should read the Error Information log after every test.

3.8 Common mistakes

  • Comparing a raw CQE status (phase still in bit 0) with a Linux NVME_SC_* constant: you are off by one bit and everything looks like a different code.
  • Assuming "sct 0x0 / sc 0x2" and "sct 0x1 / sc 0x2" are the same error.
  • Reading only dmesg: retried-then-successful errors never print. Read the tracepoint, the counters and the Error Information log.
  • Assuming a 0x371 in dmesg means the drive aborted a command: it means Linux cancelled it, usually during a reset that a timeout or CSTS.CFS triggered. Look for the root cause earlier in the log.
  • Expecting CRD to work without the host enabling ACRE. Linux v7.2 enables ACRE (nvme_configure_host_options()) only if crdt1 is non-zero in Identify Controller.

Quiz

  1. The raw 16-bit CQE status word is 0x8103. Decode it.

    Answer

    Bit 0 (P) = 1. Shifting right by one gives 0x4081: DNR=1 (0x4000), More=0, CRD=0, SCT=0 (bits 10:8 of 0x081 = 0), SC=0x81 → Generic "Capacity Exceeded". Linux maps it to BLK_STS_NOSPC → -ENOSPC and does not retry.

  2. A drive returns Internal Error (0x006) with DNR=0 on every attempt. How many times does Linux v7.2 send the command, and what does the application see?

    Answer

    With the default max_retries=5: the first attempt plus 5 retries = 6 submissions. Then retries >= nvme_max_retries → COMPLETE, one log line, and BLK_STS_IOERR → -EIO.

  3. Why can't you find status 0x371 in an analyzer trace that covers the failure?

    Answer

    Because the driver created it in nvme_cancel_request() while tearing down queues. No CQE carried it.

✅ Mastery gate
  • I can convert between raw CQE status, Linux shifted status and libnvme SCT/SC pairs.
  • I can predict COMPLETE / RETRY / FAILOVER / AUTHENTICATE for any status and request type.
  • I know which codes map to ENOSPC, EREMOTEIO, EOPNOTSUPP, ENODATA, EILSEQ, EBADE, ENOLINK, and that the rest map to EIO.
  • I can show a retried error that never reached dmesg.

4 Generic Command Status (SCT 0)

← 3 The Status Field: Anatomy and the Linux Path5 Command Specific Status (SCT 1) →

Generic status codes can be returned by any command. SC 0x00–0x7F apply to all commands. SC 0x80–0xBF are "generic for the I/O command set" codes such as LBA Out of Range. Linux writes them as 0x000–0x0FF. In practice, almost everything here comes from argument validation or from controller state (format, sanitize, reset). Only a few (0x04, 0x05, 0x06, 0x21, 0x22) point to a real hardware or transport problem.

How to read the cards: "Linux" values are the shifted layout from include/linux/nvme.h. "QEMU" names the function in hw/nvme/ctrl.c where the reference model returns the code. That is a good guide to typical cause, but it is implementation-specific impl. Unless a card says otherwise, Linux maps the code to BLK_STS_IOERR / -EIO (see §3.4.2) and retries it unless DNR is set.

S-01Invalid Command Opcode (SCT 0 / SC 0x01) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The controller does not implement the opcode on that queue type (admin vs I/O), or the opcode is reserved. Nothing is executed.
How it shows up
CQE with SCT 0 / SC 0x01, normally DNR=1. Linux: NVME_SC_INVALID_OPCODE 0x001 → BLK_STS_NOTSUPP → -EOPNOTSUPP. dmesg "Invalid Command Opcode".
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Generic Command Status "Invalid Command Opcode". Commands Supported and Effects log (LID 05h) CSUPP bit says which opcodes the controller claims to support.
Typical causes
Optional command not supported (Compare, Write Zeroes, Copy, Verify, Write Uncorrectable); vendor tool sending a vendor opcode to a different model; I/O opcode sent on the admin queue; mismatch between Identify ONCS/OACS bits and actual support. QEMU: nvme_admin_cmd(), nvme_io_cmd_nvm(), nvme_io_cmd_zoned() reject opcodes whose effects entry lacks NVME_CMD_EFF_CSUPP.
How to detect / prove
Compare against nvme id-ctrl (OACS, ONCS) and nvme effects-log /dev/nvme0. If the effects log says CSUPP=1 but the command returns 0x01, the controller contradicts itself (conformance bug).
In a trace
SQE fetch, then an immediate CQE (a few µs) with no data TLPs.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() (NOTSUPP); pr.c:nvme_status_to_pr_err() → -EINVAL for persistent reservations.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme io-passthru /dev/nvme0n1 --opcode=0x7f --namespace-id=1 (a reserved I/O opcode) or nvme admin-passthru /dev/nvme0 --opcode=0x7f. Check that DNR=1.
S-02Invalid Field in Command (SCT 0 / SC 0x02) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
A field in the SQE (CDW10–15, flags, PSDT, NSID-dependent fields, data length) holds a reserved or unsupported value. This is the catch-all argument error.
How it shows up
SCT 0 / SC 0x02, normally DNR=1. If the controller supports it, the Error Information log entry's Parameter Error Location names the byte and bit that was wrong. Linux 0x002 → BLK_STS_NOTSUPP → -EOPNOTSUPP.
Spec basis
Generic "Invalid Field in Command". Error Information log field Parameter Error Location (libnvme parm_error_location).
Typical causes
Transfer larger than MDTS (QEMU nvme_check_mdts()); unsupported Feature ID or log page offset (QEMU nvme_get_log(), nvme_set_feature()); Identify CNS not supported; bad Abort SQID (nvme_abort()); reserved bits set; SGL requested when unsupported (nvme_map_dptr()); Flush to a bad NSID; DSM range count too large. QEMU returns this from about 86 sites, more than any other code.
How to detect / prove
nvme error-log /dev/nvme0 -e 16: match SQID/CID, then read parm_error_location. Re-check the command against Identify limits (MDTS, ONCS, SGLS, DMRL/DMRSL).
In a trace
Immediate CQE after the SQE fetch. For PRP-based commands the device may first fetch the PRP list before rejecting.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); core.c:nvme_scan_work() falls back to sequential namespace scanning when Identify NS List fails with DNR.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: Read with NLB larger than MDTS through nvme io-passthru; nvme get-feature -f 0x7e on an unsupported FID; nvme get-log -i 0x02 -l 4096 -o 0x10000 (offset beyond the log).
S-03Command ID Conflict (SCT 0 / SC 0x03) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The host submitted a command whose CID is already outstanding on the same SQ. The controller may reject the new command.
How it shows up
SC 0x03. Many controllers do not check this and instead complete two commands with the same CID, which the host then confuses. Linux does not handle the code specially (→ -EIO).
Spec basis
Generic "Command ID Conflict". CIDs must be unique per SQ among outstanding commands.
Typical causes
Host driver bug (tag reuse before completion), a user-space driver (SPDK-style) with a broken tag allocator, or a duplicate completion making the host believe a tag is free. QEMU does not return it.
How to detect / prove
In an analyzer trace, list outstanding CIDs per SQ and flag a second SQE with the same CID before its CQE. Linux v7.2 adds a 4-bit generation counter to CID bits 15:12 (nvme_cid_install_genctr()) and warns on mismatching completions, which catches the host-side variant.
In a trace
Two SQEs on one SQ with the same CID and no CQE between them.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme.h:nvme_cid(), nvme_genctr_from_cid(); quirk NVME_QUIRK_SKIP_CID_GEN disables the generation counter for devices that mishandle it.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: only from a user-space driver or a protocol exerciser that can submit duplicate CIDs.
S-04Data Transfer Error (SCT 0 / SC 0x04) ReportedNVMe command / PCIe DMA
What happens
Moving data or metadata between host memory and the controller failed: a DMA read returned an error completion, a write could not be posted, or a transport failed in the middle of a transfer.
How it shows up
SC 0x04, DNR varies (QEMU uses both). Often there is a companion PCIe-level error: Unsupported Request or Completer Abort completion for the device's MRd, or an IOMMU fault on the host. Linux → -EIO.
Spec basis
Generic "Data Transfer Error". PCIe completion status UR/CA for the device's memory reads.
Typical causes
IOMMU blocks a PRP/SGL address (stale mapping, use-after-unmap in the host driver); a bad PRP entry pointing to non-existent memory; host memory poisoned (ECC) during the DMA read; link errors that corrupt the transfer. QEMU: nvme_map_addr(), nvme_map_prp(), nvme_map_sgl(), nvme_tx() return it when pci_dma_rw() fails or the address cannot be mapped.
How to detect / prove
dmesg for IOMMU/DMAR faults with the device's BDF at the same time; AER logs; analyzer: the device's MRd to the PRP address gets a UR/CA completion. Check lspci -vvv DevSta "UnsupReq+" / "AuxPwr".
In a trace
MRd from device → Cpl with status UR or CA (or no completion → device completion timeout), followed by a CQE with SC 0x04.
Linux v7.2 source
No special handling. Mapped to BLK_STS_IOERR in core.c:nvme_error_status().
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: in a QEMU guest with a vIOMMU, unmap a buffer while a passthrough command is outstanding; or use a protocol exerciser to return UR to the device's data MRd.
S-05Commands Aborted due to Power Loss Notification (SCT 0 / SC 0x05) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The controller saw a power-loss notification (e.g. a shutdown notification by CC.SHN, or a platform power-fail signal) and aborted outstanding commands instead of finishing them.
How it shows up
SC 0x05 on the commands that were outstanding. Retryable after power returns. Linux → -EIO if the request ends there; usually the controller is being shut down anyway.
Spec basis
Generic "Commands Aborted due to Power Loss Notification". Shutdown via CC.SHN / CSTS.SHST.
Typical causes
Host issued a shutdown while I/O was still queued (driver ordering bug); surprise power loss with a hold-up circuit giving the firmware time to post completions; hot-unplug.
How to detect / prove
Correlate with SMART "Unsafe Shutdowns" increasing, the Persistent Event log "Power-on or Reset" event, and CC.SHN writes in the trace. The data from writes aborted this way is not guaranteed. Verify it with read-back.
In a trace
MWr to CC (offset 14h) setting SHN, then a burst of CQEs with SC 0x05, then CSTS.SHST = 10b (shutdown complete).
Linux v7.2 source
Linux quiesces and drains queues before shutdown (pci.c:nvme_dev_disable()). Seeing 0x05 under Linux suggests an unexpected power event. No special case in nvme_error_status().
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: write CC.SHN through a user-space driver while I/O is queued (not with the kernel driver bound). EXPERT: a power-fail test fixture.
S-06Internal Error (SCT 0 / SC 0x06) ReportedNVMe command / device
What happens
The command failed because of something inside the controller that is not a media error and not the host's fault: a firmware exception, resource exhaustion, an internal bus or SRAM error.
How it shows up
SC 0x06; DNR=0 for transient problems, DNR=1 if the firmware knows it will repeat. Often comes with an Error-type AEN (Transient Internal Error 04h or Persistent Internal Error 03h) and a Persistent Event log "NVM Subsystem Hardware Error" event. Linux → -EIO after retries.
Spec basis
Generic "Internal Error". Asynchronous Event Information – Error Status values 03h/04h (see S-79).
Typical causes
Firmware bugs (assertion, corner-case state), DRAM ECC errors inside the SSD, thermal throttling edge cases, internal queue overflow. QEMU: returned when the backend block-status query fails (nvme_check_dulbe()), in zone-state checks for impossible states, and in nvme_map_addr() when too many SG entries are needed.
How to detect / prove
Read nvme error-log, nvme persistent-event-log and the vendor telemetry (nvme telemetry-log --output-file=...) right after the event. Repeat the same command: if it fails deterministically, record it as a firmware defect with the exact SQE.
In a trace
Normal SQE fetch, sometimes partial data transfer, then a CQE with SC 0x06. Latency is often abnormal (long internal retry before giving up).
Linux v7.2 source
Default branch of core.c:nvme_error_status(); persistent internal error AEN → core.c:nvme_handle_aer_persistent_error() resets the controller.
Test to provoke it
Not reliably possible from the host. Use vendor error-injection commands, or QEMU with a backend whose block-status call fails while DULBE is enabled.
S-07Command Abort Requested (SCT 0 / SC 0x07) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The command was aborted because the host sent an Abort (admin opcode 08h) naming its SQID and CID.
How it shows up
The aborted command completes with SC 0x07. The Abort command itself completes with Dword 0 bit 0 = 0 if the command was aborted, 1 if not. Linux prints "Abort status: 0x%x" from pci.c:abort_endio().
Spec basis
Admin Abort command; Identify Controller ACL (Abort Command Limit, 0's based); Generic "Command Abort Requested".
Typical causes
Almost always a host timeout: Linux pci.c:nvme_timeout() sends Abort for an I/O command that exceeded nvme_core.io_timeout (default 30 s) the first time it times out. The real problem is whatever made the command slow or stuck.
How to detect / prove
dmesg "I/O tag N (cid) opcode ... QID q timeout, aborting" followed by "Abort status". If the command is not aborted and times out again, Linux resets the controller ("timeout, reset controller").
In a trace
Original SQE → long silence → admin SQE opcode 08h with CDW10 = SQID | CID<<16 → CQE for the target (SC 0x07) and a CQE for the Abort.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout(), pci.c:abort_endio(); the limit ctrl->abort_limit is set to ACL+1 in core.c.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: in QEMU, cause a stalled backend I/O (for example a blkdebug rule with a delay); lower io_timeout (writable module parameter) to see the abort sooner. QEMU's nvme_abort() cancels the backend AIO, which completes it with 0x07.
S-08Command Aborted due to SQ Deletion (SCT 0 / SC 0x08) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The host deleted an I/O SQ (admin opcode 00h) while commands were still outstanding on it. The controller aborts them.
How it shows up
SC 0x08 on each outstanding command, posted to the associated CQ before the Delete SQ completes. If the CQ has already been reaped, these completions can be lost.
Spec basis
Delete I/O Submission Queue; Generic "Command Aborted due to SQ Deletion".
Typical causes
Controller reset path, CPU hot-unplug changing queue count, driver unload. QEMU: nvme_del_sq() and nvme_ctrl_reset() set this status on in-flight requests.
How to detect / prove
Analyzer: Delete SQ SQE followed by completions with SC 0x08. Linux normally cancels requests itself (0x371) before deleting queues, so seeing many 0x08 under Linux suggests a driver bypass or a non-Linux host.
In a trace
Admin SQE opcode 00h → CQEs on the I/O CQ with SC 0x08 → CQE for Delete SQ.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:adapter_delete_sq(); mapped to -EIO by default.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver deleting a busy SQ.
S-09Fused command aborted: Failed Fused (0x09) / Missing Fused (0x0A) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
A fused pair (e.g. Compare + Write = atomic compare-and-write) did not run as a pair. 0x09: the other half failed, so this half was aborted. 0x0A: the adjacent SQE was not the expected fused partner.
How it shows up
One half gets the real error (e.g. Compare Failure 0x285), and the other gets 0x09. A mis-built pair gets 0x0A.
Spec basis
Fused Operation (FUSE field in CDW0: first/second command); Identify FUSES. Generic codes 0x09, 0x0A.
Typical causes
Compare miscompare (expected for CAS semantics); host submitting the two halves non-adjacently (another thread rang the doorbell in between); controller not supporting fused operations.
How to detect / prove
Trace: both SQEs must be consecutive slots in the same SQ, and the tail doorbell must cover both in one write.
In a trace
SQE[n] FUSE=01b, SQE[n+1] FUSE=10b. On failure: CQE(n) Compare Failure, CQE(n+1) SC 0x09.
Linux v7.2 source
The kernel block path does not issue fused commands. They come only through passthrough (ioctl.c). Status → -EIO.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space (SPDK-style) compare-and-write with deliberately wrong compare data → 0x285 + 0x09.
S-10Invalid Namespace or Format (SCT 0 / SC 0x0B) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The NSID is not valid for this command (not allocated, not attached, 0 where not allowed, or FFFFFFFFh where broadcast is not allowed), or the namespace's current format does not support the command.
How it shows up
SC 0x0B, normally DNR=1. Linux 0x00B → BLK_STS_NOTSUPP → -EOPNOTSUPP. During a namespace rescan, Linux treats an Identify Namespace failure with DNR as "namespace gone" and removes the block device.
Spec basis
Generic "Invalid Namespace or Format". Identify NN (number of namespaces), Active Namespace ID list (CNS 02h).
Typical causes
Namespace deleted or detached by another host or a management tool; stale NSID after reformatting; tools using NSID 1 on a drive where it does not exist. QEMU: nvme_io_cmd(), nvme_identify_ns(), nvme_get_feature(), nvme_smart_info() (per-namespace SMART not supported) and about 15 other sites.
How to detect / prove
nvme list-ns /dev/nvme0 --all versus nvme list-ns /dev/nvme0 (allocated vs attached); look for a Namespace Attribute Changed AEN and the Changed Namespace List log (LID 04h).
In a trace
Immediate CQE after the SQE fetch.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); core.c:nvme_validate_ns() starts with ret = NVME_SC_INVALID_NS | NVME_STATUS_DNR and calls nvme_ns_remove() on fatal (DNR) errors.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme id-ns /dev/nvme0 -n 0xfffe (an unused NSID). CAUTION: detach a namespace (nvme detach-ns) with I/O running and watch the block device disappear.
S-11Command Sequence Error (SCT 0 / SC 0x0C) ReportedNVMe admin
What happens
The command is valid on its own but not allowed in the current state of a multi-command sequence or controller state.
How it shows up
SC 0x0C, usually DNR=1.
Spec basis
Generic "Command Sequence Error". Examples in the spec: Firmware Image Download sequencing, Set Features Number of Queues after queues exist, security protocol sequences.
Typical causes
Changing Number of Queues (FID 07h) after I/O queues were created (QEMU nvme_set_feature(): if (n->qs_created) return NVME_CMD_SEQ_ERROR); changing FDP mode while namespaces exist in the endurance group (QEMU); Firmware Commit without a complete downloaded image; TCG/OPAL protocol out of order.
How to detect / prove
Reconstruct the admin command order from a trace or from the nvme_setup_cmd/nvme_complete_rq tracepoints; check which preceding command changed the state.
In a trace
Immediate CQE. The interesting part is the earlier commands.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux sets Number of Queues once per reset (core.c:nvme_set_queue_count()) before creating queues, so it avoids this. A tool that sends the feature at runtime gets 0x0C.
Test to provoke it
SAFE on QEMU / CAUTION on hardware: nvme set-feature /dev/nvme0 -f 7 -v 0x00030003 while the driver has queues.
S-12Data pointer descriptor errors: SGL 0x0D–0x11, 0x16, 0x1E; PRP Offset 0x13 ReportedNVMe command / data pointer
What happens
The controller rejects the data pointer: an invalid SGL Segment/Last Segment descriptor (0x0D), a bad number of descriptors (0x0E), Data SGL length too short or too long (0x0F), Metadata SGL length invalid (0x10), unsupported SGL descriptor type (0x11), SGL offset invalid (0x16, mainly fabrics in-capsule data), SGL data block granularity invalid (0x1E), or PRP entry offset invalid (0x13).
How it shows up
One of these SCs, with DNR=1 in QEMU. Linux → -EIO. These almost always point to a host driver or IOMMU bug, not a drive problem.
Spec basis
PRP rules (PRP1 offset; PRP list entries must be page-aligned, Dword-aligned offsets); SGL descriptor types; Identify Controller SGLS field.
Typical causes
QEMU: nvme_map_sgl() → 0x0D for a misplaced segment descriptor; nvme_map_sgl_data() → 0x0E / 0x0F; nvme_map_mptr() → 0x10; 0x11 for unknown types (ctrl.c line ~1013); nvme_map_prp(), nvme_create_cq()/nvme_create_sq() → 0x13 for a non-page-aligned PRP. In real systems: a new driver (user-space or in-kernel) building PRP lists across page boundaries incorrectly, or MPS (CC.MPS) disagreeing with the host's assumed page size.
How to detect / prove
Decode the SQE's PSDT and DPTR in the trace and walk the PRP list or SGL by hand. Check that CC.MPS matches the host page size the driver uses.
In a trace
MRd of the PRP list or SGL segment (sometimes), then a CQE. No data TLPs.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux builds PRPs and SGLs in pci.c (e.g. nvme_pci_setup_data_prp() and the SGL counterpart). Errors are mapped to -EIO by nvme_error_status().
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver with a deliberately misaligned PRP2; QEMU gives deterministic results.
S-13Controller Memory Buffer misuse: Invalid Use of CMB (0x12), Command Not Supported for Queue in CMB (0x1F) ReportedNVMe command / CMB
What happens
0x12: a data pointer mixes CMB (or PMR) addresses and host-memory addresses in a way the controller does not support, or uses the CMB for something not enabled in CMBSZ. 0x1F: the command is not allowed when its SQ/CQ lives in the CMB.
How it shows up
SC 0x12 / 0x1F, DNR=1 in QEMU.
Spec basis
CMBLOC (38h), CMBSZ (3Ch) register capabilities (SQS, CQS, WDS, RDS); CMBMSC in newer revisions.
Typical causes
QEMU nvme_map_addr(): a scatter list that has started with DMA addresses then hits a CMB/PMR address (or the reverse). Peer-to-peer DMA setups with partly mapped buffers.
How to detect / prove
Compare every PRP/SGL address with the CMB BAR range from lspci -vvv and the CMBLOC offset.
In a trace
Addresses inside the device's own BAR in the DPTR. There is no MRd to host memory for those parts.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c CMB support (nvme_map_cmb(); SQs in CMB with the use_cmb_sqes module parameter). The status itself is mapped to -EIO.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: QEMU -device nvme,cmb_size_mb=64 plus a user-space driver mixing CMB and host buffers.
S-14Atomic Write Unit Exceeded (SCT 0 / SC 0x14) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The write is longer than the atomic write unit, and the controller is configured to reject such writes instead of executing them non-atomically.
How it shows up
SC 0x14. Linux → -EIO. Note that Linux's newer atomic-write support (RWF_ATOMIC) checks sizes before submission, so a drive returning this to the kernel path points to a mismatch between Identify limits and behaviour.
Spec basis
Identify Controller AWUN/AWUPF; Identify Namespace NAWUN/NAWUPF/NABSN; Write Atomicity Normal feature (FID 0Ah); QEMU nvme_set_feature_write_atomicity().
Typical causes
Database or filesystem relying on atomic writes larger than the advertised unit; writes crossing an atomic boundary (NABO/NABSN).
How to detect / prove
Compare the write's SLBA/NLB with NAWUPF/NABSN from nvme id-ns -H. QEMU's nvme_atomic_write_check() shows how a controller decides whether a write is atomic.
In a trace
SQE for Write with NLB > limit → CQE 0x14, no data MRd.
Linux v7.2 source
Atomic limits come from Identify (NAWUPF/AWUPF) into queue limits in core.c:nvme_configure_atomic_write(); core.c:nvme_valid_atomic_write() refuses, on the host side, REQ_ATOMIC writes that cross an atomic boundary before they reach the drive. The status itself → -EIO.
Test to provoke it
SAFE (data-destructive only for the target LBA range): enable the Write Atomicity Normal feature on QEMU and issue an oversized write with nvme write.
S-15Operation Denied (SCT 0 / SC 0x15) ReportedNVMe command / security
What happens
The command was denied for lack of access rights, as defined by a security specification (e.g. TCG Storage), or by a locking or lockdown rule.
How it shows up
SC 0x15. A read to a locked TCG range usually returns this or Access Denied 0x286, depending on the implementation impl.
Spec basis
Generic "Operation Denied"; Security Send/Receive; related Generic 0x23 "Command Prohibited by Command and Feature Lockdown" (libnvme NVME_SC_PROHIBITED_BY_CMD_AND_FEAT).
Typical causes
Self-encrypting drive locked after power cycle (no unlock in initramfs); management commands blocked by lockdown; vendor-unique commands needing a key.
How to detect / prove
sedutil-cli --query or nvme security-recv to check locking state; repeat after unlock.
In a trace
Immediate CQE; for SEDs, there are Security Send/Receive (81h/82h) exchanges earlier.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c OPAL hook (nvme_sec_submit()); the status itself → -EIO.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: lock a TCG Opal range on a test drive and read it.
S-16Host Identifier Inconsistent Format (0x18), Keep Alive Timer Expired (0x19), Keep Alive Timeout Invalid (0x1A) ReportedNVMe admin / NVMe-oF
What happens
0x18: the subsystem saw both 64-bit and 128-bit Host Identifiers used across controllers. 0x19: the Keep Alive Timer expired and the controller tears down the association; outstanding commands may complete with this status. 0x1A: Set Features Keep Alive Timer (FID 0Fh) with an unacceptable value.
How it shows up
On fabrics: 0x19 together with the controller disappearing; the host sees the connection reset and reconnect attempts. On PCIe keep-alive is optional and rarely enabled.
Spec basis
Host Identifier feature (FID 81h); Keep Alive Timer feature (FID 0Fh), KAS in Identify Controller.
Typical causes
0x19: host stalled (CPU soft lockup, network partition), or a target too strict about KATO. 0x18: mixed host tooling on a shared subsystem. 0x1A: KATO outside the controller's range.
How to detect / prove
On the host: dmesg keep-alive messages from core.c:nvme_keep_alive_end_io(); on the target, its log; on the network, a gap in Keep Alive capsules longer than KATO.
In a trace
Missing Keep Alive (admin opcode 18h) commands for > KATO, then the association closes.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_keep_alive_work(), nvme_keep_alive_end_io(); pr.c handles 0x18 (NVME_SC_HOST_ID_INCONSIST) for Reservation Report by retrying without the Extended Data Structure (128-bit Host ID) format.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: NVMe/TCP loopback target, then tc qdisc add ... netem loss 100% for longer than KATO.
S-17Command Aborted due to Preempt and Abort (SCT 0 / SC 0x1B) ReportedNVMe command / reservations
What happens
Another host ran Reservation Acquire with the Preempt and Abort action. Commands from the preempted host that were in progress on the namespace are aborted.
How it shows up
SC 0x1B on in-flight commands of the preempted host. Later commands from that host get Reservation Conflict (0x83). A Reservation Notification log page (LID 80h) and an I/O Command Set–specific AEN (Reservation Log Page Available) may follow.
Spec basis
Reservations (Register/Acquire/Release/Report); Reservation Notification log.
Typical causes
Cluster fencing (pacemaker/STONITH with persistent reservations) evicting a node.
How to detect / prove
nvme resv-report /dev/nvme0n1 before and after; cluster logs; nvme get-log -i 0x80.
In a trace
On the other host's path: Reservation Acquire (opcode 11h) with RACQA=Preempt and Abort; on this host's path: CQEs with 0x1B.
Linux v7.2 source
pr.c implements the block pr_ops; status → -EIO in the I/O path.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: two hosts (or two controllers) on a shared namespace; register both keys, acquire on A, then preempt-and-abort from B while A runs fio.
S-18Sanitize Failed (SCT 0 / SC 0x1C) ReportedNVMe admin / sanitize
What happens
The most recent sanitize operation failed and no recovery has completed yet. Until then, the controller rejects commands that would expose possibly un-sanitized data.
How it shows up
Media-access commands complete with SC 0x1C. Sanitize Status log (LID 81h) SSTAT status = 3h "completed with failure" (libnvme NVME_SANITIZE_SSTAT_STATUS_COMPLETED_FAILED). See S-89.
Spec basis
Sanitize command (84h), Sanitize Status log, "Exit Failure Mode" sanitize action.
Typical causes
Block erase failed on some dies; power lost during sanitize with an implementation that cannot resume; crypto erase key-store failure.
How to detect / prove
nvme sanitize-log /dev/nvme0: SSTAT and SPROG; Persistent Event log Sanitize Start/Completion events (types 09h/0Ah).
In a trace
Sanitize SQE earlier; reads now return 0x1C immediately.
Linux v7.2 source
No special handling (→ -EIO). The kernel does not issue Sanitize itself.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT/destructive: power-cycle during a sanitize on a sacrificial drive. Recovery: nvme sanitize -a 1 (Exit Failure Mode) or retry sanitize.
S-19Sanitize In Progress (SCT 0 / SC 0x1D) ReportedNVMe command / sanitize
What happens
A sanitize is running. Commands that are prohibited during sanitize (I/O, most admin commands that touch media) are rejected.
How it shows up
SC 0x1D, typically DNR=0 (it will work once sanitize is done). Linux retries up to max_retries, which is far shorter than a sanitize, so the I/O ends with -EIO. Boot-time partition scans fail and the disk may look empty.
Spec basis
Sanitize; Sanitize Status log SPROG (progress /65536) and SSTAT = 2h "in progress"; sanitize persists across resets and power cycles.
Typical causes
A sanitize started by someone else (or by a previous boot, because sanitize resumes after power cycle); estimated times (ETBE/ETCE/ETO fields in LID 81h) of hours on large drives.
How to detect / prove
nvme sanitize-log /dev/nvme0 shows SSTAT=2 and SPROG increasing. Get Log Page for LID 81h is allowed during sanitize.
In a trace
Every I/O SQE gets an immediate CQE 0x1D.
Linux v7.2 source
Generic retry in core.c:nvme_decide_disposition(); the delay depends only on CRD.
Test to provoke it
DESTRUCTIVE / EXPERT: nvme sanitize /dev/nvme0 -a 2 (block erase) on a scratch drive, then dd from it.
S-20Namespace is Write Protected (SCT 0 / SC 0x20) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The namespace is in a write-protected state (Namespace Write Protection Config feature: Write Protect, Write Protect Until Power Cycle, Permanent Write Protect), so writes and other modifying commands are refused.
How it shows up
SC 0x20 on writes. Linux → -EIO for that write. Linux also reads the namespace write-protect state at scan time and marks the disk read-only if Identify NS NSATTR has the write-protected bit set.
Spec basis
Namespace Write Protection Config feature (FID 84h); NSATTR in Identify Namespace.
Typical causes
Admin set write protection (forensics, golden images); permanent protection after end-of-life policies; feature set without the host noticing (no AEN for it).
How to detect / prove
nvme get-feature /dev/nvme0 -n 1 -f 0x84; nvme id-ns -H NSATTR; blockdev --getro.
In a trace
Writes get immediate CQEs. Reads succeed.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c namespace update path sets the disk read-only from NSATTR (NVME_NS_ATTR_RO). The status itself → -EIO.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: nvme set-feature /dev/nvme0 -n 1 -f 0x84 -v 1 (Write Protect, reversible), write, then set it back to 0. Never use the permanent value on a drive you want to keep.
S-21Command Interrupted (SCT 0 / SC 0x21) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The controller stopped processing the command (internal event, ANA-related change, resource rebalance) and asks the host to retry, often with a Command Retry Delay.
How it shows up
SC 0x21 with DNR=0 by definition. If the retries run out, Linux maps it to BLK_STS_TARGET → -EREMOTEIO ("critical target error").
Spec basis
Generic "Command Interrupted": "the host should retry the command" (libnvme). CRD field + CRDT1–3 + ACRE.
Typical causes
Firmware busy with a background operation; controller-level events in dual-port arrays; QEMU returns it as an internal status for interrupted zone/atomic operations (defined as NVME_COMMAND_INTERRUPTED).
How to detect / prove
Trace with nvme:nvme_complete_rq (status 0x21 with retries > 0) and command_retries_count. Check CRD values against nvme id-ctrl crdt1..3.
In a trace
CQE 0x21 → host waits the CRD time → same command re-submitted.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() → BLK_STS_TARGET; nvme_retry_req() CRD delay; nvme_configure_host_options() ACRE.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: fault-inject status 0x21 with dont_retry=0 (§3.5) to see the retry and errno behaviour.
S-22Transient Transport Error (SCT 0 / SC 0x22) ReportedNVMe-oF transport
What happens
A short-lived transport problem made the command fail. Retrying on the same controller should work. libnvme quotes the spec: after four or more failures with this status, treat it as a persistent transport error.
How it shows up
SC 0x22, DNR=0. Linux retries (up to 5) and then gives -EIO. It is not in SCT 3, so Linux multipath does not fail over on it directly.
Spec basis
Generic "Transient Transport Error"; transport bindings (TCP, RDMA, FC).
Typical causes
Packet loss or digest errors (NVMe/TCP HDGST/DDGST), RDMA retry exhaustion, FC link resets.
How to detect / prove
Network counters (ethtool -S, RDMA counters), NVMe/TCP digest error messages in dmesg; correlation with retries in the tracepoint.
In a trace
On the network capture: retransmissions or digest mismatches just before the response capsule.
Linux v7.2 source
Default retry/COMPLETE path in core.c; transport drivers in tcp.c/rdma.c.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: NVMe/TCP with tc netem corrupt plus data digest enabled.
S-23Admin Command Media Not Ready (SCT 0 / SC 0x24) ReportedNVMe admin
What happens
The admin command needs the media, but the media is not ready yet. This can happen when the controller became ready early (CSTS.RDY) because the host set CC.CRIME (Controller Ready Independent of Media Enable).
How it shows up
SC 0x24 on admin commands such as Identify Namespace, Get Log Page of media-backed logs, Format.
Spec basis
CAP.CRMS (CRWMS/CRIMS), CC.CRIME, CRTO register (Controller Ready Timeouts).
Typical causes
Host using CRIME mode and sending media-dependent commands before media ready; large drives whose internal metadata load takes seconds.
How to detect / prove
Check CC.CRIME in a register dump or trace; compare time since CC.EN with CRTO.CRIMT.
In a trace
CC write with CRIME=1 → CSTS.RDY=1 quickly → admin command → CQE 0x24.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_enable_ctrl() clears CRIME on purpose: "Setting CRIME results in CSTS.RDY before the media is ready. This makes it possible for media related commands to return the error NVME_SC_ADMIN_COMMAND_MEDIA_NOT_READY. Until the driver is restructured to handle retries, disable CC.CRIME." So Linux should never see this code.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver enabling with CRIME=1 on a CAP.CRMS-capable drive and sending Identify NS at once.
S-24I/O Command Set errors: Invalid I/O Command Set (0x2C) and the 0x29–0x2B group ReportedNVMe admin
What happens
A command references a Command Set Identifier (CSI) that the controller does not support, has not enabled, or cannot combine with the others.
How it shows up
Linux defines NVME_SC_INVALID_IO_CMD_SET = 0x2C in the Generic range. QEMU (NVME_INVALID_IOCS = 0x012c) and libnvme (NVME_SC_INVALID_IOCS = 0x2c under "Command Set Specific – Namespace Types") place it in SCT 1, along with 0x29 I/O Command Set Not Supported, 0x2A Not Enabled, 0x2B Combination Rejected. uncertain Verify the SCT in the spec. A Linux string lookup of a real SCT 1 / 0x2C status prints "Unknown" because 0x12C has no entry.
Spec basis
I/O Command Set Profile feature (FID 19h), Identify I/O Command Set data structure (CNS 1Ch), CC.CSS.
Typical causes
QEMU: nvme_identify_ns() returns INVALID_IOCS for a namespace whose CSI is not enabled; nvme_set_feature() returns IOCS_NOT_SUPPORTED / COMBINATION_REJECTED. Real: tools asking for ZNS/KV identify data on an NVM-only controller.
How to detect / prove
nvme id-iocs /dev/nvme0; nvme get-feature -f 0x19.
In a trace
Identify with CNS 05h/06h and a CSI value → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux sets CC.CSS to NVME_CC_CSS_CSI when CAP.CSS allows it (core.c:nvme_enable_ctrl()) and ignores namespaces with unsupported CSI during scan.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme admin-passthru /dev/nvme0 --opcode=0x06 --cdw10=0x05 --cdw11=0x05000000 -r -l 4096 --namespace-id=1 (Identify CNS 05h with an unsupported CSI value).
S-25LBA Out of Range (SCT 0 / SC 0x80) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
SLBA + NLB goes past the namespace size (NSZE), or the arithmetic overflows. Nothing is transferred.
How it shows up
SC 0x80, DNR=1 in QEMU. Linux maps it to BLK_STS_TARGET → -EREMOTEIO ("critical target error"), not EIO, which surprises people searching logs.
Spec basis
Generic (I/O command set) "LBA Out of Range"; Identify Namespace NSZE.
Typical causes
Namespace shrunk/recreated while the host kept the old size (no rescan); partition table from a larger drive copied to a smaller one; block-layer sector-to-LBA conversion bugs with mixed 512/4096 formats. QEMU: nvme_check_bounds(): if (UINT64_MAX - slba < nlb || slba + nlb > nsze).
How to detect / prove
dmesg error line shows "@ LBA x, n blocks". Compare with nvme id-ns NSZE and blockdev --getsize64. A mismatch means a stale capacity.
In a trace
SQE with SLBA near the end of the namespace → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() (BLK_STS_TARGET); capacity updates in nvme_update_ns_info() after rescans.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme read /dev/nvme0n1 -s <NSZE> -c 0 -z 4096 (read the block just past the end).
S-26Capacity Exceeded (SCT 0 / SC 0x81) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
Running the command would make the namespace use more than its capacity (NCAP). This only matters for thin-provisioned namespaces, where NCAP < NSZE.
How it shows up
SC 0x81. Linux maps it to BLK_STS_NOSPC → -ENOSPC: the filesystem gets "no space" even though df shows free space.
Spec basis
Identify Namespace NSZE, NCAP, NUSE; thin provisioning (NSFEAT bit 0).
Typical causes
Over-subscribed thin namespace; host not sending Deallocate (discard), so NUSE never shrinks; deallocations the drive accepts but does not apply (see silent-failure part).
How to detect / prove
Watch NUSE vs NCAP with nvme id-ns over time; compare with filesystem usage. If NUSE stays high after fstrim, deallocation is not taking effect.
In a trace
Write SQE → CQE 0x81, possibly after data transfer.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() → BLK_STS_NOSPC.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: thin namespace (QEMU or array) filled past NCAP with fio.
S-27Namespace Not Ready (SCT 0 / SC 0x82) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The namespace cannot be accessed right now, for a reason other than an ANA state (libnvme wording). Examples: still coming up after reset, a background operation, or a namespace that is being created.
How it shows up
SC 0x82. DNR=0 means "try again later", DNR=1 means "will not become ready". Linux → BLK_STS_TARGET → -EREMOTEIO once retries are used up.
Spec basis
Generic "Namespace Not Ready"; Identify Namespace / I/O Command Set Independent NS data "Namespace Ready" indications in newer revisions (verify in spec).
Typical causes
Command sent right after reset or namespace attach on a drive that rebuilds mapping tables; a drive in a vendor "failure" mode returning 0x82 to everything.
How to detect / prove
Time since CSTS.RDY; check whether it clears by itself (poll with nvme read each second). Persistent 0x82 with DNR=1 is a dead namespace.
In a trace
Immediate CQE 0x82 to I/O shortly after controller enable.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() → BLK_STS_TARGET.
Test to provoke it
SAFE via fault injection (status 0x082).
S-28Reservation Conflict (SCT 0 / SC 0x83) ReportedNVMe command / reservations
What happens
Another host holds a reservation on the namespace that does not allow this host's access (for example, Write Exclusive held by another host, and this host writes).
How it shows up
SC 0x83, normally DNR=1. Linux maps it to BLK_STS_RESV_CONFLICT → -EBADE; the persistent-reservation API returns PR_STS_RESERVATION_CONFLICT.
Spec basis
Reservation types (Write Exclusive, Exclusive Access, Registrants Only, All Registrants); Reservation Report.
Typical causes
Cluster software fenced this node; stale reservation left by a crashed node; a host that has not registered its key after reboot.
How to detect / prove
nvme resv-report /dev/nvme0n1 -c 1 shows holder, type and registrants; compare with this host's Host ID (nvme get-feature -f 0x81).
In a trace
I/O SQE → immediate CQE 0x83.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); pr.c:nvme_status_to_pr_err().
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: nvme resv-register + resv-acquire -t 1 (Write Exclusive) from controller A, write from controller B.
S-29Format In Progress (SCT 0 / SC 0x84) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
A Format NVM is running on the namespace; I/O to it is rejected until it completes.
How it shows up
SC 0x84 on I/O, DNR typically 0. Linux retries a few times, then -EIO.
Spec basis
Format NVM (80h); Identify Controller FNA (Format NVM Attributes, e.g. whether a format applies to all namespaces). Some drives report progress in a vendor log.
Typical causes
Format issued by another tool or host on a shared namespace, or on a "format all namespaces" drive (FNA bit) where formatting one namespace blocks the others. QEMU: nvme_do_format() sets ns->status = NVME_FORMAT_IN_PROGRESS and I/O to that namespace returns it until done.
How to detect / prove
Look for an earlier Format SQE (opcode 80h) in the trace or in the admin nvme_setup_cmd tracepoint; Persistent Event log Format NVM Start/Completion events (07h/08h).
In a trace
Format SQE without its CQE yet; I/O CQEs with 0x84.
Linux v7.2 source
Default handling; the kernel itself never formats.
Test to provoke it
DESTRUCTIVE / CAUTION: on a scratch namespace in QEMU, nvme format /dev/nvme0n1 -s 1 (user-data erase; slower) and read from another shell.
💡 Senior insight

When a new drive fails qualification with "-EOPNOTSUPP" or "-EREMOTEIO" in an application, translate back with the table in §3.4.2 before blaming the application. EREMOTEIO always means LBA Out of Range, Namespace Not Ready or Command Interrupted. Those three have very different root causes, and the dmesg nvme_log_error line (if it printed) tells you which one. If it did not print, the request was RQF_QUIET or the error came from somewhere other than the NVMe driver.

5 Command Specific Status (SCT 1)

← 4 Generic Command Status (SCT 0)3.1 Time Is an Error Signal →

SCT 1 codes only make sense together with the opcode that got them. SC 0x00–0x7F are defined per admin command (queue management, firmware, namespace management, virtualization). SC 0x80–0xBF are defined per I/O command set: for the NVM command set (0x80–0x8x), for Fabrics commands (the same numbers, different meanings), and for Zoned Namespaces (0xB6–0xBF). In Linux's shifted layout they are 0x100–0x1FF. Always decode an SCT 1 status with the opcode next to it. 0x180 is "Conflicting Attributes" for a Dataset Management command but "Incompatible Format" for a Fabrics Connect.

💡 Linux ignores some of these on purpose

drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_create_io_queues() says: "Ignore failing Create SQ/CQ commands, we can continue with less than the desired amount of queues, and even a controller without I/O queues can still be used to issue admin commands." A drive that rejects queue creation with an SCT 1 code (0x100–0x102, 0x108) therefore does not fail probe. The result is a working but slower device with fewer queues. The error is reported, but in practice it shows up as a performance anomaly. Check ls /sys/block/nvme0n1/mq | wc -l against the CPU count.

S-30Completion Queue Invalid (SCT 1 / SC 0x00) ReportedNVMe admin / queues
What happens
Create I/O SQ named a CQID that does not exist (or is 0), or Delete I/O CQ named an invalid CQ.
How it shows up
Linux 0x100 "Completion Queue Invalid" on the admin command; queue setup continues with fewer queues (see callout above).
Spec basis
Create I/O Submission Queue (01h) CDW11 CQID; Delete I/O Completion Queue (04h).
Typical causes
Host creating the SQ before its CQ, or after a failed CQ creation; QEMU nvme_create_sq() and nvme_del_cq(): if (!cqid || nvme_check_cqid(n, cqid)) return NVME_INVALID_CQID | NVME_DNR.
How to detect / prove
Admin command order in the trace: Create CQ (05h) must complete successfully before Create SQ (01h) uses its ID.
In a trace
Create SQ with CQID=n where no earlier successful Create CQ n exists.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_create_queue() → adapter_alloc_cq() then adapter_alloc_sq().
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver creating SQ 1 bound to CQ 7 that was never created.
S-31Invalid Queue Identifier (SCT 1 / SC 0x01) ReportedNVMe admin / queues
What happens
Create I/O SQ/CQ used a QID that is 0, already in use, or above the number of queues granted by Set Features Number of Queues. Also used by Delete I/O SQ for a bad SQID.
How it shows up
Linux 0x101; queue creation stops at that queue.
Spec basis
Number of Queues feature (FID 07h) result NSQA/NCQA (0's based); Create/Delete queue commands.
Typical causes
Host asked for N queues, got fewer in the Set Features result, and created N anyway; QID reuse without deleting. QEMU: nvme_create_sq()/nvme_create_cq() check qid > n->conf_ioqpairs || n->sq[qid] != NULL.
How to detect / prove
Decode the Number of Queues completion Dword 0 in the trace and compare with the highest QID created.
In a trace
Set Features FID 07h CQE DW0 = e.g. 0x00070007 (8 SQ + 8 CQ), then Create CQ with QID 9.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_set_queue_count() uses the granted count. pci.c:nvme_setup_io_queues() sizes the queues from it.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: start with -device nvme,max_ioqpairs=4 and create queue 5 from a user-space driver.
S-32Invalid Queue Size (SCT 1 / SC 0x02) ReportedNVMe admin / queues
What happens
The queue size in Create I/O SQ/CQ is 0 or bigger than CAP.MQES+1, or (QEMU) the CC.IOSQES/IOCQES entry sizes are not 64/16 bytes.
How it shows up
Linux 0x102 "Invalid Queue Size".
Spec basis
CAP.MQES (0's based maximum queue entries); CAP.CQR (contiguous queues required); CC.IOSQES/IOCQES.
Typical causes
Host ignoring MQES; a device that reports MQES wrongly (Linux has quirks for this). QEMU: nvme_create_cq()/nvme_create_sq() return NVME_MAX_QSIZE_EXCEEDED | NVME_DNR.
How to detect / prove
Read CAP (nvme show-regs /dev/nvme0 -H) and the QSIZE field (CDW10 bits 31:16) of the Create command.
In a trace
Create CQ CDW10 = (QSIZE<<16)|QID with QSIZE > MQES.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c: queue depth is limited by NVME_CAP_MQES(cap) + 1 and the io_queue_depth module parameter (default 1024).
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver creating a queue of MQES+2 entries.
S-33Abort Command Limit Exceeded (0x03) / 0x04 "Abort Command Is Missing" (reserved) ReportedNVMe admin
What happens
The host has more Abort commands outstanding than ACL+1 (Identify Controller ACL is 0's based). The extra Abort is rejected. 0x04 is listed by libnvme as "Abort command is missing". Linux names it NVME_SC_ABORT_MISSING but prints "Reserved" uncertain in current specs.
How it shows up
The Abort itself completes with 0x103. Linux logs "Abort status: 0x103" from pci.c:abort_endio().
Spec basis
Abort command (08h); ACL in Identify Controller (spec recommends at least 4, i.e. ACL ≥ 3).
Typical causes
Many simultaneous timeouts from a non-Linux host. Linux sets ctrl->abort_limit = id->acl + 1 and, in nvme_timeout(), does not send more aborts than that (it re-arms the timer instead). Linux therefore should not trigger this code unless ACL is reported wrongly.
How to detect / prove
Count outstanding Abort SQEs (opcode 08h) in the trace vs ACL from nvme id-ctrl.
In a trace
ACL+2 Abort SQEs without completions between them; the last one gets 0x103.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout() (atomic_dec_return(&dev->ctrl.abort_limit) < 0 → BLK_EH_RESET_TIMER).
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver issuing ACL+2 aborts for commands stuck in a slow backend (QEMU).
S-34Asynchronous Event Request Limit Exceeded (SCT 1 / SC 0x05) ReportedNVMe admin / AEN
What happens
More AER commands are outstanding than the controller supports (Identify Controller AERL, 0's based). The extra AER completes at once with this status.
How it shows up
0x105 on an AER (opcode 0Ch). Linux's nvme_complete_async_event() returns early for any non-success AER status (if (le16_to_cpu(status) >> 1 != NVME_SC_SUCCESS) return;) and does not resubmit, so the error is not logged.
Spec basis
Asynchronous Event Request command; AERL (spec recommends at least 4 supported).
Typical causes
A host or tool submitting AERs in a loop; a controller with AERL=0 meeting a host that posts several. Linux posts only one (NVME_NR_AEN_COMMANDS = 1). QEMU: nvme_aer(): if (n->outstanding_aers > n->params.aerl) return NVME_AER_LIMIT_EXCEEDED (no DNR).
How to detect / prove
Count outstanding opcode 0Ch commands in the trace; compare with AERL.
In a trace
AER SQEs normally stay outstanding for hours. One that completes within microseconds with 0x105 is this error.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_complete_async_event(), pci.c:nvme_pci_submit_async_event() (fixed CID NVME_AQ_BLK_MQ_DEPTH).
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: -device nvme,aerl=0 plus a second AER sent with nvme admin-passthru --opcode=0x0c (it blocks until an event if accepted).
S-35Invalid Firmware Slot (SCT 1 / SC 0x06) ReportedNVMe admin / firmware
What happens
Firmware Commit named a slot that does not exist, or a read-only slot (slot 1 when FRMW.S1RO is set) for a replace action.
How it shows up
0x106 on Firmware Commit (opcode 10h). nvme-cli prints the status name.
Spec basis
Identify Controller FRMW: bit 0 slot 1 read-only, bits 3:1 number of slots, bit 4 activation without reset; Firmware Commit CDW10 FS (slot) and CA (commit action).
Typical causes
Update scripts written for a model with more slots; writing to slot 1 on drives where it holds the read-only factory image; slot 0 used with a CA that requires an explicit slot.
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ctrl -H /dev/nvme0 | grep -A5 frmw; nvme fw-log /dev/nvme0 shows populated slots.
In a trace
Firmware Commit SQE CDW10[2:0] slot value → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
The kernel does not issue Firmware Commit. Only passthrough (status returned raw to nvme-cli).
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme fw-commit /dev/nvme0 -s 7 -a 2 on a drive with fewer than 7 slots (CA 2 = activate existing image; no image is replaced).
S-36Invalid Firmware Image (0x07) and Overlapping Range (0x14) ReportedNVMe admin / firmware
What happens
0x07: the downloaded image failed validation (signature, CRC, wrong model, incomplete) at commit time. 0x14: Firmware Image Download pieces overlapped (the same offset range sent twice in an incompatible way).
How it shows up
0x107 on Firmware Commit, or 0x114 on Firmware Image Download/Commit. The running firmware is unchanged. Some drives also raise an Error AEN "Firmware Image Load Error" (05h) at a later activation.
Spec basis
Firmware Image Download (11h) NUMD/OFST in Dwords; FWUG (Firmware Update Granularity) in Identify Controller; Firmware Commit.
Typical causes
Wrong image file for the SKU; chunk size not a multiple of FWUG; tool retrying a chunk with a different offset; image signed for a different security version (anti-rollback).
How to detect / prove
Log every Firmware Image Download (offset, length) and check contiguity; compare nvme id-ctrl FWUG with the transfer size (nvme fw-download --xfer).
In a trace
A series of opcode 11h SQEs with OFST increasing by NUMD+1; a gap or overlap is visible directly.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: download a truncated or foreign image, then commit with CA=0 (replace, no activation). The drive must reject it without bricking. Run this as a robustness test.
S-37Invalid Interrupt Vector (SCT 1 / SC 0x08) ReportedNVMe admin / queues
What happens
Create I/O CQ named an interrupt vector the controller cannot use: beyond the MSI-X table size, or non-zero when MSI-X is disabled.
How it shows up
0x108; Linux continues with fewer queues.
Spec basis
Create I/O CQ CDW11 IV and IEN; PCIe MSI-X capability Table Size.
Typical causes
MSI-X table smaller than the advertised queue count; host fell back to MSI/INTx but still assigns per-queue vectors. QEMU nvme_create_cq(): if (!msix_enabled(...) && vector) and if (vector >= n->conf_msix_qsize).
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv MSI-X "Count=" vs number of I/O queues; /proc/interrupts nvme entries.
In a trace
Create CQ CDW11[31:16] = vector ≥ table size.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:adapter_alloc_cq(); vector allocation in nvme_setup_irqs().
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: -device nvme,msix_qsize=2 with more queues requested; boot with pci=nomsi to see the non-MSI-X case.
S-38Invalid Log Page (SCT 1 / SC 0x09) ReportedNVMe admin / logs
What happens
Get Log Page asked for an unsupported or reserved Log Identifier.
How it shows up
0x109. QEMU, however, returns Invalid Field (0x002) from nvme_get_log()'s default case. Implementations differ on which code they use impl.
Spec basis
Get Log Page (02h) LID; Supported Log Pages log (LID 00h, Linux NVME_LOG_SUPPORTED); LPA field in Identify Controller.
Typical causes
Tools asking for Persistent Event (0Dh), Telemetry (07h/08h) or vendor logs on drives without them.
How to detect / prove
nvme supported-log-pages /dev/nvme0 (if LID 00h is supported) and LPA bits in nvme id-ctrl -H.
In a trace
Get Log Page with CDW10[7:0]=LID → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_get_log(); the driver checks LPA before reading optional logs (e.g. command effects via NVME_CTRL_LPA_CMD_EFFECTS_LOG).
