Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme get-log /dev/nvme0 -i 0x30 -l 512.
S-39Invalid Format (SCT 1 / SC 0x0A) ReportedNVMe admin / format
What happens
Format NVM asked for an LBA format index that does not exist, or for a PI/metadata combination the namespace cannot support.
How it shows up
0x10A on Format NVM. The namespace is unchanged.
Spec basis
Identify Namespace NLBAF and LBAF[] table; Format NVM CDW10 LBAF, MSET, PI, PIL, SES.
Typical causes
LBAF index beyond NLBAF; PI type with a format whose metadata size is 0; extended LBA format index (above 15) without LBAFEE. QEMU: nvme_format_check() returns NVME_INVALID_FORMAT | NVME_DNR for lbaf > nlbaf and for PI on formats with metadata smaller than the PI size. nvme_copy() also uses it for formats it cannot copy between.
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ns -H /dev/nvme0n1 lists the LBA formats with "in use".
In a trace
Format SQE (80h) → fast CQE. A real format takes seconds to hours.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only; Linux enables LBAFEE in Host Behavior Support when CTRATT.ELBAS is set (core.c:nvme_configure_host_options()).
Test to provoke it
SAFE (rejected before any erase): nvme format /dev/nvme0n1 -l 15 on a namespace with fewer formats.
S-40Firmware Activation Requires Reset: Conventional (0x0B), NVM Subsystem (0x10), Controller Level (0x11) ReportedNVMe admin / firmware
What happens
These are successes reported as errors. The commit worked and the image is in the slot, but it will only run after the given type of reset. Until then the old firmware keeps running.
How it shows up
0x10B / 0x110 / 0x111 on Firmware Commit. Firmware Slot log (LID 03h) AFI: the "next active" slot (bits 6:4) differs from the current slot (bits 2:0). Linux reports this at AEN time as "Firmware is activated after next Controller Level Reset".
Spec basis
Firmware Commit CA values (libnvme: 0 replace, 1 replace+activate at reset, 2 activate at reset, 3 replace+activate immediately, 6/7 boot partition); Firmware Slot Information log AFI; NSSR register (20h) for subsystem reset.
Typical causes
Image needs a full reset to load; CA=1/2 used (activation at next reset by design). Scripts that treat any non-zero status as failure roll back a successful update. Scripts that ignore it think the new firmware is running when it is not.
How to detect / prove
nvme fw-log /dev/nvme0 AFI before and after; cat /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/firmware_rev stays old until reset; after a reset, check the revision again.
In a trace
Firmware Commit CQE with SCT 1 SC 0x0B/0x10/0x11; later CC.EN 1→0→1 (controller reset), PCIe hot/conventional reset, or a write of "NVMe" (4E564D65h) to NSSR.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_get_fw_slot_info(): if (!cur_fw_slot || (next_fw_slot && (cur_fw_slot != next_fw_slot))) → "Firmware is activated after next Controller Level Reset"; reset via nvme reset /dev/nvme0 (NVME_IOCTL_RESET) or subsystem reset (NVME_IOCTL_SUBSYS_RESET, pci.c:nvme_pci_subsystem_reset()).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: nvme fw-download a valid image, nvme fw-commit -s 2 -a 1, read status, then nvme reset /dev/nvme0 and verify firmware_rev. Use a supported vendor image only.
S-41Firmware Activation Requires Maximum Time Violation (0x12) / Activation Prohibited (0x13) ReportedNVMe admin / firmware
What happens
0x12: activating now (CA=3, immediate) would take longer than MTFA (Maximum Time for Firmware Activation, Identify Controller, 100 ms units), so the controller refuses. 0x13: the controller refuses this image for vendor-specific reasons (e.g. anti-rollback, a downgrade not allowed).
How it shows up
0x112 / 0x113 on Firmware Commit; the running firmware is unchanged.
Spec basis
MTFA; FRMW bit 4 (activation without reset supported); Firmware Commit CA=3.
Typical causes
Immediate activation of an image that changes the media layout; downgrade blocked by security policy.
How to detect / prove
Retry with CA=1/2 and a reset. If it works, the immediate path was the problem. For 0x13, check the vendor's release notes for minimum versions.
In a trace
Commit CDW10[5:3]=3 → CQE 0x12.
Linux v7.2 source
MTFA is stored in ctrl->mtfa and used by core.c:nvme_fw_act_work() to bound the wait while CSTS.PP is set after a "Firmware Activation Starting" AEN (S-81).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: commit with CA=3 an image the vendor says needs a reset.
S-42Invalid Queue Deletion (SCT 1 / SC 0x0C) ReportedNVMe admin / queues
What happens
Delete I/O CQ was sent for a CQ that still has SQs attached (they must be deleted first), or for the admin CQ.
How it shows up
0x10C on Delete I/O CQ (opcode 04h). QEMU returns it without DNR, so retrying after deleting the SQ is expected.
Spec basis
Delete I/O Completion Queue; queue deletion order (SQs before their CQ).
Typical causes
Teardown order bug in a host driver. QEMU nvme_del_cq(): if (!QTAILQ_EMPTY(&cq->sq_list)) return NVME_INVALID_QUEUE_DEL.
How to detect / prove
Trace order: Delete CQ (04h) issued before Delete SQ (00h) for an SQ mapped to that CQ.
In a trace
04h before 00h.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_delete_io_queues() deletes SQs first, then CQs (passes of nvme_admin_delete_sq then nvme_admin_delete_cq).
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: user-space driver deleting the CQ first.
S-43Feature errors: Not Saveable (0x0D), Not Changeable (0x0E), Not Namespace Specific (0x0F) ReportedNVMe admin / features
What happens
Set Features with SV=1 for a feature that cannot be saved (0x0D); a write to a read-only feature (0x0E); a namespace-specific NSID used with a controller-wide feature (0x0F).
How it shows up
0x10D/0x10E/0x10F with DNR=1 (QEMU). The current value is unchanged.
Spec basis
Set/Get Features, SV bit, Get Features SEL=11b "supported capabilities" (saveable, namespace-specific, changeable). ONCS bit "Save field in Set/Get Features".
Typical causes
Tools blindly setting --save. QEMU: nvme_set_feature() returns NVME_FID_NOT_SAVEABLE when save is set on any feature, NVME_FEAT_NOT_NS_SPEC for NSID ≠ 0/FFFFFFFFh on controller-scope features, NVME_FEAT_NOT_CHANGEABLE for fixed features.
How to detect / prove
nvme get-feature /dev/nvme0 -f <fid> -s 3 (capabilities) before setting.
In a trace
Set Features CDW10 bit 31 (SV) set → CQE 0x10D.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_set_features() never sets SV. Errors come only from passthrough tools.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme set-feature /dev/nvme0 -f 0x08 -v 0 --save (Interrupt Coalescing, saved) on a drive without saveable support.
S-44Namespace management/attachment errors: 0x15, 0x16, 0x18, 0x19, 0x1A, 0x1B, 0x1C, 0x24, 0x25 ReportedNVMe admin / namespaces
What happens
Namespace Management (0Dh) or Namespace Attachment (15h) refused: Namespace Insufficient Capacity (0x15), Namespace Identifier Unavailable (0x16, no free NSIDs), Namespace Already Attached (0x18), Namespace Is Private (0x19, already attached to another controller), Namespace Not Attached (0x1A, detach of a non-attached namespace), Thin Provisioning Not Supported (0x1B, NCAP < NSZE requested), Controller List Invalid (0x1C), ANA Group Identifier Invalid (0x24), ANA Attach Failed (0x25).
How it shows up
The matching 0x1xx status. Nothing changes.
Spec basis
Namespace Management create/delete; Namespace Attachment attach/detach with a Controller List; Identify TNVMCAP/UNVMCAP, NN, MNAN, ANAGRPMAX.
Typical causes
Provisioning scripts not checking UNVMCAP; wrong controller IDs (CNTLID) in the list; creating private namespaces on dual-port drives then attaching to both ports. QEMU nvme_ns_attachment() returns NS_ALREADY_ATTACHED, NS_PRIVATE, NS_NOT_ATTACHED, NS_CTRL_LIST_INVALID.
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ctrl (tnvmcap, unvmcap, nn, cntlid), nvme list-ctrl /dev/nvme0 -n <nsid>, nvme list-ns --all.
In a trace
Admin 0Dh/15h with a 4 KiB data buffer (namespace data or controller list) → CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only. After a successful change Linux rescans on the Namespace Attribute Changed AEN (core.c:nvme_handle_aen_notice() → nvme_queue_scan()).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION (the creates and deletes are destructive): on QEMU with -device nvme-subsys and nvme-ns,detached=true, attach the same namespace twice → 0x118.
S-45Device Self-test In Progress (SCT 1 / SC 0x1D) ReportedNVMe admin / self-test
What happens
A Device Self-test (opcode 14h) was started while another self-test is running.
How it shows up
0x11D. Device Self-test log (LID 06h) byte 0 shows the current operation (1h short, 2h extended) and byte 1 the percent complete.
Spec basis
Device Self-test command STC (1h short, 2h extended, Fh abort); Device Self-test log; EDSTT (extended test time, minutes).
Typical causes
Monitoring agents (smartd, vendor tools) starting tests on a schedule while a manual test runs.
How to detect / prove
nvme self-test-log /dev/nvme0; abort with nvme device-self-test -s 0xf.
In a trace
Two opcode 14h commands; the second gets 0x11D.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: nvme device-self-test /dev/nvme0 -s 2 twice in a row.
S-46Boot Partition Write Prohibited (SCT 1 / SC 0x1E) ReportedNVMe admin / boot partition
What happens
An attempt to change a locked Boot Partition (Firmware Image Download/Commit with boot-partition commit actions 6/7).
How it shows up
0x11E.
Spec basis
Boot Partitions (CAP.BPS, BPINFO/BPRSEL/BPMBL registers), Boot Partition Write Protection Config feature in newer revisions.
Typical causes
Platform locked the boot partition; tool writing the wrong partition.
How to detect / prove
nvme show-regs -H BPINFO; Boot Partition log (LID 15h).
In a trace
Firmware Commit CA=6 → CQE 0x11E.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: platform with boot-partition lock.
S-47Virtualization management errors: 0x1F Invalid Controller Identifier, 0x20 Invalid Secondary Controller State, 0x21 Invalid Number of Controller Resources, 0x22 Invalid Resource Identifier ReportedNVMe admin / SR-IOV
What happens
Virtualization Management (1Ch) refused: unknown secondary controller, secondary controller in the wrong state (online vs offline) for the action, resources (VQ/VI) over the flexible limit, or bad resource type.
How it shows up
0x11F–0x122 with DNR=1 (QEMU).
Spec basis
Virtualization Enhancements; Primary Controller Capabilities (CNS 14h), Secondary Controller List (CNS 15h).
Typical causes
Assigning resources to a VF that is online; asking for more queues than VQFRSM. QEMU: nvme_assign_virt_res_to_sec(), nvme_virt_set_state(), nvme_virt_mngmt().
How to detect / prove
nvme primary-ctrl-caps, nvme list-secondary.
In a trace
Admin 1Ch → CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only.
Test to provoke it
SAFE on QEMU with sriov_max_vfs: assign resources to a secondary controller that is online.
S-48Sanitize Prohibited While Persistent Memory Region is Enabled (SCT 1 / SC 0x23) ReportedNVMe admin / sanitize
What happens
Sanitize was requested while the PMR is enabled (PMRCTL.EN=1).
How it shows up
0x123. Linux prints "Sanitize Prohibited".
Spec basis
Persistent Memory Region (CAP.PMRS, PMRCAP/PMRCTL registers); Sanitize.
Typical causes
Decommissioning scripts not disabling the PMR first.
How to detect / prove
nvme show-regs -H PMRCTL.
In a trace
Sanitize SQE → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Passthrough only.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: a PMR-capable controller (QEMU pmrdev=) with PMR enabled.

5.1 NVM command set specific (SCT 1, SC 0x80–0x8F)

S-49Conflicting Attributes (SCT 1 / SC 0x80) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
Dataset Management (or another NVM command) carries attribute bits that contradict each other or that the controller cannot honour together.
How it shows up
0x180 → Linux BLK_STS_NOTSUPP → -EOPNOTSUPP (a discard that fails this way looks like "discard not supported"). Persistent reservations map it to -EINVAL.
Spec basis
NVM Command Set: Dataset Management attributes (AD deallocate, IDR, IDW) and context attributes.
Typical causes
DSM with a combination the firmware does not implement; reservation commands with conflicting options.
How to detect / prove
Decode CDW11 of the DSM in the trace; repeat with only AD set.
In a trace
DSM (09h) SQE, MRd of the range list, CQE 0x180.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); pr.c:nvme_status_to_pr_err().
Test to provoke it
SAFE via fault injection; on hardware, nvme dsm /dev/nvme0n1 -a 1 -d -w -r combinations (illustrative; check the nvme-cli syntax for your version).
S-50Invalid Protection Information (SCT 1 / SC 0x81) ReportedNVMe command / PI
What happens
The PRINFO field (PRACT, PRCHK bits) is not valid for the namespace's PI type, or PI was requested on a namespace without PI.
How it shows up
0x181 → Linux BLK_STS_PROTECTION → -EILSEQ.
Spec basis
End-to-end Data Protection: Identify Namespace DPS/DPC, PRINFO in Read/Write/Compare/Verify CDW12.
Typical causes
Host sends PRCHK for Type 3 reference tag; PRACT with metadata size larger than the PI size in a mode the controller rejects. QEMU: nvme_do_write(), nvme_compare(), nvme_verify() return NVME_INVALID_PROT_INFO | NVME_DNR (e.g. Compare with PRACT set).
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ns -H DPS/DPC; decode CDW12[29:26] in the trace.
In a trace
Immediate CQE, no data.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); PRINFO built in core.c:nvme_setup_rw() from the integrity profile.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU (PI namespace -device nvme-ns,pi=1,ms=8): nvme compare with PRACT set.
S-51Attempted Write to Read Only Range (SCT 1 / SC 0x82) ReportedNVMe command
What happens
A write touched an LBA range that the host previously marked read-only through the LBA Range Type feature (FID 03h).
How it shows up
0x182 → Linux BLK_STS_MEDIUM → -ENODATA ("critical medium error"). This is misleading: the media is fine and the cause is configuration.
Spec basis
LBA Range Type feature (attributes: read-only / hidden ranges). QEMU defines NVME_WRITE_TO_RO but never returns it.
Typical causes
OEM BIOS/firmware marked ranges read-only; old tools.
How to detect / prove
nvme get-feature /dev/nvme0 -n 1 -f 3 -H.
In a trace
Write → CQE 0x182 (may follow data transfer).
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status() (BLK_STS_MEDIUM).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: set an LBA Range Type entry with the read-only attribute on a test drive that supports FID 03h.
S-52Command Size Limits Exceeded (0x83) and Copy-related 0x85/0x86/0x87/0x89 ReportedNVMe command / Copy
What happens
A Copy (19h) or similar command exceeds MSSRL (max single source range length), MCL (max copy length) or MSRC (max source range count): 0x83. libnvme and QEMU add 0x85 Incompatible Namespace or Format, 0x86 Fast Copy Not Possible, 0x87 Overlapping I/O Range, 0x89 Insufficient Resources. These are not in Linux's nvme.h, so Linux prints them as "Unknown".
How it shows up
0x183 (Linux text "Command Size Limits Exceeded"); 0x185/0x187 from QEMU's nvme_do_copy().
Spec basis
NVM Command Set Copy command; Identify Namespace MSSRL/MCL/MSRC; ONCS Copy bit.
Typical causes
Copy offload with ranges built from filesystem extents larger than MSSRL; cross-namespace copy between different LBA formats (0x85); source overlapping destination (0x87). QEMU: nvme_check_copy_mcl(), nvme_copy().
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ns -H mssrl/mcl/msrc, then the Copy range list contents.
In a trace
Copy SQE → MRd of the source range descriptors → CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Default mapping (-EIO); Linux v7.2 does not issue Copy from the block layer in the paths examined here uncertain, so Copy errors come from passthrough.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: nvme copy with a range longer than MSSRL.
S-53Fabrics Connect/Discovery statuses: 0x80–0x84, 0x90 Discovery Restart, 0x91 Authentication Required ReportedNVMe-oF
What happens
Fabrics Connect rejected: Incompatible Format (0x180), Controller Busy (0x181), Connect Invalid Parameters (0x182), Connect Restart Discovery (0x183), Connect Invalid Host (0x184). Also Discovery Restart (0x190) and Authentication Required (0x191). libnvme also lists 0x85 Invalid Queue Type for Disconnect.
How it shows up
Connect fails; nvme connect reports the error. dmesg from fabrics.c:nvmf_log_connect_error(). For 0x182 it decodes which parameter failed using the Connect response's IATTR/IPO fields. 0x191 on I/O → Linux disposition AUTHENTICATE → re-authentication work, then retry.
Spec basis
NVMe over Fabrics: Connect command and response, Discovery log, DH-HMAC-CHAP in-band authentication.
Typical causes
Host NQN not on the target's allow list (0x184); wrong subsystem NQN or CNTLID (0x182); dynamic controller already associated (0x181); keys not configured (0x191).
How to detect / prove
dmesg "Connect Invalid Host"/"Connect command failed ..." lines; target-side logs; nvme discover output.
In a trace
Network capture: Fabrics command capsule (opcode 7Fh, fctype Connect) → response capsule with the status.
Linux v7.2 source
fabrics.c:nvmf_log_connect_error(), nvmf_should_reconnect() path stops reconnecting when status & NVME_STATUS_DNR; core.c:nvme_decide_disposition() AUTHENTICATE branch. Note the string-table collision with NVM codes (§3.4.3).
Test to provoke it
SAFE: Linux nvmet loopback target without the host NQN in allowed_hosts → 0x184.

5.2 Zoned Namespace command set (SCT 1, SC 0xB6–0xBF)

ZNS errors are mostly the host breaking the zone state machine (write pointer, open and active limits). QEMU's ZNS model (nvme_check_zone_write(), nvme_check_zone_state_for_write(), nvme_zns_check_resources(), nvme_zrm_*()) returns all of them, so it is an excellent test device. Linux zoned users (f2fs, btrfs, dm-zoned, zonefs) get -EIO for most of them, but -EOVERFLOW / -ETOOMANYREFS for the active and open limits. libnvme also lists 0xB6 Invalid Zone Operation Request and 0xB7 ZRWA Resources Unavailable, which Linux's nvme.h lacks.

S-54Zone Boundary Error (SCT 1 / SC 0xB8) ReportedNVMe command / ZNS
What happens
A command's LBA range spans more than one zone. For reads this is only an error if the namespace forbids cross-zone reads (Read Across Zone Boundaries, OZCS.RAZB=0).
How it shows up
0x1B8 → -EIO.
Spec basis
ZNS Command Set: zone size (ZSZE), OZCS RAZB.
Typical causes
Host splitting logic ignoring zone size (non-power-of-2 zone sizes are a classic trap). QEMU: nvme_check_zone_write(), nvme_check_zone_read().
How to detect / prove
nvme zns id-ns (zsze); compute slba / zsze and (slba+nlb-1) / zsze.
In a trace
Write SQE near a zone end → CQE 0x1B8.
Linux v7.2 source
Block layer splits at chunk_sectors (zone size) so in-kernel I/O should not cross; default mapping in nvme_error_status().
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU ZNS: nvme read across a zone boundary.
S-55Zone Is Full (0xB9) / Zone Is Read Only (0xBA) / Zone Is Offline (0xBB) ReportedNVMe command / ZNS
What happens
A write to a zone in ZSF:Full (0xB9) or ZSRO:Read Only (0xBA), or any access to a zone in ZSO:Offline (0xBB). Read Only and Offline are device-initiated states: the drive moved the zone there because of media problems. That makes them media-health events disguised as state errors.
How it shows up
0x1B9/0x1BA/0x1BB → -EIO. A zone report shows the zone state. The drive may also raise a Zone Descriptor Changed notice (ZNS-specific AEN) verify in spec.
Spec basis
ZNS zone states ZSE/ZSIO/ZSEO/ZSC/ZSF/ZSRO/ZSO; Zone Management Receive (report zones).
Typical causes
Host lost track of the write pointer after a crash (Full); worn or failed dies (Read Only, Offline). QEMU: nvme_check_zone_state_for_write(), nvme_check_zone_state_for_read().
How to detect / prove
nvme zns report-zones /dev/nvme0n1 -d 10; blkzone report; count zones in RO/Offline over time as a health metric.
In a trace
Write SQE → immediate CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
zns.c (nvme_ns_report_zones()) converts zone states for the block layer; status → -EIO.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: fill a zone, then write again (0xB9). QEMU's Offline send action works only from Read Only (nvme_offline_zone()). Read Only and Offline must be entered by the device, so test 0xBA/0xBB with vendor injection or fault injection on the host.
S-56Zone Invalid Write (SCT 1 / SC 0xBC) ReportedNVMe command / ZNS
What happens
A Write to a sequential-write-required zone did not start at the zone's write pointer.
How it shows up
0x1BC → -EIO. The most common ZNS error in the field.
Spec basis
ZNS sequential write requirement; Zone Append (opcode 7Dh) avoids it by letting the device choose the LBA.
Typical causes
Reordered writes (multiple queues, no zone write locking or plugging); retry of a write that actually succeeded (write pointer already advanced); host write-pointer cache stale after power loss. QEMU nvme_check_zone_write(): if (slba != zone->w_ptr) return NVME_ZONE_INVALID_WRITE.
How to detect / prove
Trace per-zone SLBA order and compare with the reported write pointer. A write that is reported failed but whose data is present means the earlier attempt succeeded.
In a trace
Two Writes to the same zone completing out of order, or a re-submission after a host timeout.
Linux v7.2 source
Block layer zone write plugging keeps order. The NVMe driver maps the status to -EIO. Note the danger of nvme_decide_disposition() retries for zoned writes when DNR is clear derived.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: nvme write to zslba + 8 on an empty zone.
S-57Too Many Active Zones (0xBD) / Too Many Open Zones (0xBE) ReportedNVMe command / ZNS
What happens
Opening or writing a zone would exceed MAR (Maximum Active Resources) or MOR (Maximum Open Resources), both 0's based.
How it shows up
0x1BD → BLK_STS_ZONE_ACTIVE_RESOURCE → -EOVERFLOW; 0x1BE → BLK_STS_ZONE_OPEN_RESOURCE → -ETOOMANYREFS. These are the only zone codes with dedicated Linux errnos, so the filesystem can tell them apart and close zones.
Spec basis
ZNS Identify Namespace MAR/MOR; implicit vs explicit open.
Typical causes
Too many concurrent writers or log streams; not finishing zones. QEMU: nvme_zns_check_resources() (with DNR).
How to detect / prove
cat /sys/block/nvme0n1/queue/max_open_zones and max_active_zones; count zones in open/closed states from a zone report.
In a trace
A write to an empty zone → CQE 0x1BE.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_error_status(); limits set in zns.c from MAR/MOR.
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: -device nvme-ns,zoned=true,zoned.max_open=2, then write to 3 empty zones.
S-58Invalid Zone State Transition (0xBF) and Invalid Zone Operation Request (0xB6) / ZRWA Resources Unavailable (0xB7) ReportedNVMe command / ZNS
What happens
Zone Management Send asked for a transition the state machine does not allow (e.g. Open a Full zone, Finish an Offline zone) → 0xBF. 0xB6: invalid zone operation (e.g. allocating a ZRWA on a non-empty zone). 0xB7: no Zone Random Write Area available.
How it shows up
0x1BF → -EIO (Linux has no constant for 0xB6/0xB7 and prints "Unknown").
Spec basis
ZNS Zone Send Actions (Close, Finish, Open, Reset, Offline, Set Zone Descriptor Extension, Flush Explicit ZRWA Range).
Typical causes
Host state cache out of sync; tools sending Open to Full zones. QEMU: nvme_zrm_open_flags(), nvme_zrm_finish(), nvme_zrm_close(), nvme_zrm_reset(), nvme_offline_zone() return NVME_ZONE_INVAL_TRANSITION (no DNR); nvme_open_zone() returns NVME_INVALID_ZONE_OP / NVME_NOZRWA.
How to detect / prove
Zone report before the command; ZNS state diagram.
In a trace
Zone Management Send (79h) → CQE.
Linux v7.2 source
Zone ops (REQ_OP_ZONE_*) built in zns.c:nvme_setup_zone_mgmt_send().
Test to provoke it
SAFE in QEMU: nvme zns open-zone on a full zone.
PART III

Errors of Time: When Something Takes Too Long

A command that never finishes has no status code, because there is no completion to carry one. A link that never trains raises no AER bit, because there is no link to send it on. In NVMe, PCIe and the Linux host these failures are caught by one thing only: a timer. This part lists every timer and time limit across the three layers — who sets it, in what units, what happens when it expires, how to see it, and what Linux v7.2 does — and treats each expiry as an error in its own right, with its own card.

3.1 Time Is an Error Signal

← 5 Command Specific Status (SCT 1)3.2 NVMe Time Limits: Ready, Shutdown, Keep Alive, Abort, Activation and Friends →

3.1.1 Why "too slow" is a failure, not a nuisance

Every protocol in the storage path is built on a promise: if I ask, you will answer. The NVMe specification never says how long a Read may take, but it does say how long the controller may take to become ready (CAP.TO), to shut down (reported through RTD3E), to activate firmware (MTFA), to finish a transition between Asymmetric Namespace Access (ANA) states (ANATT), and how often the host must prove it is alive (KATO). PCI Express (PCIe) bounds how long a requester waits for a completion (Completion Timeout), how long a transmitter waits for an acknowledgement (replay timer), and how long each Link Training and Status State Machine (LTSSM) state may last. The Linux host adds its own limits on top: 30 seconds for an I/O command, 60 seconds for an admin command, 5 seconds by default for a shutdown.

When one of these limits passes, the same thing has happened as when a device returns an error status: the system can no longer trust that the request did what it asked. Often it is worse than an error status, for three reasons:

  • No information. An error status says what went wrong. An expired timer only says that something did not arrive. The cause could be the device firmware, the link, a missed interrupt, the host's own scheduling, or a power state that never woke up.
  • Unknown state. After a timeout the host does not know whether the command ran, ran partly, or is still running. A Write that "timed out" may have reached the media; a Write that was aborted may still be in flight inside the drive. Recovery has to assume the worst, which is why Linux escalates to a controller reset.
  • Large blast radius. One stuck command can cost a reset of the whole controller, and every other in-flight command on every queue is cancelled and retried with it.
📌 Remember

A timer expiry is an error even when no status code exists. In this atlas every such failure is labelled Timer only. If the cause is a slowdown that never trips any timer — a p99.9 latency miss, a throttled drive — it is labelled Degraded.

3.1.2 Three kinds of time limit

KindWho measuresExamplesWhat expiry means
Advertised limit (a promise the device makes)The device states it; the host measures against itCAP.TO, CRTO, RTD3E, MTFA, EDSTT, ANATT, ENLAT/EXLAT, Sanitize estimated times, CRDTThe device broke its own promise — a spec or firmware defect, or the device is failing
Protocol watchdog (part of the protocol)Hardware state machines in both link partners, or the controllerPCIe Completion Timeout, replay timer, REPLAY_NUM, LTSSM state timeouts, Keep Alive TimerAn expected packet or command did not arrive; the hardware takes a defined action (replay, retrain, error log, association teardown)
Host policy (chosen by the operating system)Driver softwareLinux io_timeout, admin_timeout, shutdown_timeout, ctrl_loss_tmo, pci_dev_wait() 60 s, ps_max_latency_usThe host decided it waited long enough; it starts recovery (abort, reset, failover, removal)

The distinction matters when you write a bug report. If the device exceeded an advertised limit, it is the device's defect even if the host timeout was generous. If the device stayed within all advertised limits and the host still timed out, the host policy is too tight for that device (or the host itself stalled).

3.1.3 Nested timers for one I/O: which fires first

Consider one 4 KiB Read submitted on an NVMe drive behind a Root Port. Several independent timers start running at almost the same moment. They are nested by orders of magnitude, and the order in which they fire decides what you see in the logs.

PCIe hardware timerHost / Linux timerNVMe protocol timer (fabrics)Recovery action

Read the figure from left to right:

  1. Replay timer (microseconds). If a Transaction Layer Packet (TLP) is corrupted on the wire, the Data Link Layer retransmits it long before anyone else notices. The I/O is merely a few microseconds late. AER may log a correctable error.
  2. Completion Timeout (default 50 µs to 50 ms). Either side can be the requester. The drive is the requester when it fetches the Submission Queue Entry (SQE) and reads write data from host memory; the host is the requester when the CPU reads a register such as CSTS. If a completion does not come back in time, the requester's Completion Timeout fires and is logged as an uncorrectable AER error. For an MMIO read, the CPU then gets all-ones data. For a device DMA read, the device firmware decides what to do: ideally it completes the NVMe command with an error status; a poor implementation simply never completes it.
  3. Linux command timeout (30 s). If no Completion Queue Entry (CQE) arrives, the block layer timer fires and nvme_timeout() in drivers/nvme/host/pci.c runs: it first checks for a missed interrupt by polling the queue, then sends an NVMe Abort and re-arms the timer.
  4. Second expiry (about 60 s). If the aborted command still has not returned, Linux v7.2 disables the controller and schedules a reset. Every command in flight is cancelled and requeued.

The gap between the levels is the point: the lower layer should always fire first, so that the upper layer sees a clean error rather than silence. When a lower timer is configured longer than an upper one (a Completion Timeout in Range D, for example), the order flips: the NVMe timer fires while the PCIe transaction is still officially outstanding, and the reset races with a link-level error that has not yet been reported.

💡 Senior insight

When you see I/O tag N ... timeout, aborting in dmesg, first look at the AER log of the drive and of its Root Port for the same few seconds. If there is a Completion Timeout, Replay Timer Timeout or Surprise Down there, the NVMe timeout is a symptom; the root cause is on the link. If AER is clean, the drive accepted the command and failed to complete it: a firmware or media problem.

3.1.4 The escalation ladder in Linux v7.2

Linux turns a silent stall into a series of increasingly heavy actions. The ladder for an NVMe over PCIe I/O, as implemented in drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_timeout() and block/blk-mq.c:blk_mq_timeout_work():

t = 0          submit; blk_add_timer() arms deadline = now + io_timeout (30 s)
t = 30 s       blk_mq_timeout_work() finds request past deadline
               -> blk_mq_rq_timed_out() -> nvme_timeout(req)
                  if PCI device disconnected      -> state DELETING, disable, done
                  if pci_channel_offline()        -> BLK_EH_RESET_TIMER (let AER/DPC recover)
                  if CSTS.CFS=1 or NSSRO          -> "controller is down; will reset", reset
                  poll the CQ                     -> found? "completion polled", done
                  if controller CONNECTING        -> disable controller
                  if admin queue or already aborted -> "reset controller", reset
                  if no Abort credits (ACL+1 used) -> BLK_EH_RESET_TIMER (wait again)
                  else send Abort(SQID, CID), mark IOD_ABORTED, BLK_EH_RESET_TIMER
t = 60 s       second expiry of the same request -> IOD_ABORTED set -> reset controller
reset          nvme_dev_disable(): CC.EN=0, wait CSTS.RDY=0 for up to CAP.TO;
               cancel all requests; re-enable: CC.EN=1, wait CSTS.RDY=1 for CAP.TO / CRTO
retry          each cancelled request is retried up to max_retries (5) unless DNR

Every step is a separate failure card in chapter 3.3.

3.1.5 Master table of every timer

One row per timer or time limit. "Card" points to the failure card that describes what happens when the limit is broken. Values marked verify are widely quoted but were not confirmed against a primary source for this atlas; check them in the current PCI Express Base Specification or NVM Express Base Specification before relying on them.

Service level (no protocol timer)

T-01Command submitted but never completedTimer onlyNVMe command
What happens
The host writes an SQE and rings the doorbell; the controller fetches it (or not) but never posts a CQE. No status code exists because there is no completion.
How it shows up
Nothing until the host timer fires. Linux v7.2: nvme nvme0: I/O tag 17 (0011) opcode 0x2 (Read) QID 3 timeout, aborting req_op:READ(0) size:4096 after 30 s; if the abort also fails, ... timeout, reset controller.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: every command submitted shall be completed with a CQE (unless the controller is reset or the queue deleted). The spec gives no general per-command time limit; host timeouts are policy.
Typical causes
Firmware hang or deadlock on one command type; command lost in the controller after a malformed SQE; device DMA read of the SQE hit a Completion Timeout and firmware dropped the command; MSI-X interrupt lost (command completed but host never noticed); queue stuck behind a long-running background operation.
How to detect / prove
dmesg timeout lines carry tag, CID, opcode, QID. Compare with an analyzer trace: was the SQE fetched (device MRd to the SQ address)? Was a CQE written (device MWr to the CQ address)? Was the MSI-X MWr sent? The first missing packet locates the fault. nvme error-log may show an entry for the CID if firmware noticed.
In a trace
Normal: doorbell MWr → device MRd of SQE → CplD → data MWr/MRd → CQE MWr → MSI-X MWr. Failure: the sequence stops; often the only later packets for that queue are the Abort SQE fetch 30 s later and then config/MMIO traffic of the reset.
Linux v7.2 source
block/blk-mq.c:blk_mq_timeout_work(), blk_mq_rq_timed_out(); drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_timeout().
Test to provoke it
Use the kernel NVMe fault-injection (drivers/nvme/host/fault_inject.c, CONFIG_FAULT_INJECTION_DEBUG_FS) or a QEMU NVMe model with a patched delay; on real hardware, a vendor "hang command" debug hook. synthetic Lower /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/io_timeout (ms) to make the ladder fast. SAFE on a scratch device.

3.1.6 Common mistakes when reasoning about time

  • Treating the timeout value as the latency of the failure. A "30 s timeout" means the command was missing for at least 30 s; it may have been lost at t=1 ms.
  • Raising the timeout to "fix" errors. It hides a stall rather than removing it, and every stalled command now blocks its queue slot for longer.
  • Forgetting that the host can be the slow party. A CPU stuck with interrupts off, a thread starved by the scheduler, or a hypervisor that paused the VM produces exactly the same symptom as a slow drive. The "completion polled" message (T-21) is the classic sign.
  • Ignoring nesting. If an upper timer is shorter than a lower one, the upper layer always "wins" and the true cause is never logged.

3.2 NVMe Time Limits: Ready, Shutdown, Keep Alive, Abort, Activation and Friends

← 3.1 Time Is an Error Signal3.3 Host Timers in Linux v7.2: Timeouts, Retries, Resets and Reconnects →

The NVM Express (NVMe) specification bounds time in two ways. Some limits are registers or Identify fields the controller advertises (CAP.TO, CRTO, RTD3E, MTFA, EDSTT, ANATT, CRDT, power state latencies); the host is expected to wait at least that long and may declare the controller failed afterwards. Others are timers the controller runs on the host (the Keep Alive Timer). The cards below cover each one. For the controller-state cards it helps to keep the register handshake in mind:

  host writes CC.EN=1  ---------->  controller initialises  ----> CSTS.RDY=1     (bounded by CAP.TO or CRTO)
  host writes CC.EN=0  ---------->  controller resets       ----> CSTS.RDY=0     (bounded by CAP.TO)
  host writes CC.SHN=01b -------->  controller flushes      ----> CSTS.SHST=10b  (typical time RTD3E; host picks the limit)
  host stops sending Keep Alive -->  KATO expires           ----> controller treats host as gone
📘 Units cheat-sheet

CAP.TO and CRTO: 500 ms units. MTFA and CRDT: 100 ms units. KAS (keep-alive granularity): 100 ms units. KATO value: milliseconds. RTD3E, RTD3R, ENLAT, EXLAT: microseconds. EDSTT: minutes. ANATT and the Sanitize estimates: seconds. Timestamp: milliseconds since 1 January 1970 (48 bits). Getting the unit wrong is itself a common bug: a CAP.TO of 20 means 10 s, not 20 s.

T-02CSTS.RDY does not become 1 within CAP.TO after CC.EN=1Timer onlyNVMe controller register
What happens
The host enables the controller (CC.EN 0→1) and polls CSTS.RDY. The controller stays not-ready for longer than the worst-case time it advertised in CAP.TO.
How it shows up
No status at all; only a host timer. Linux v7.2 prints Device not ready; aborting initialisation, CSTS=0x0 and the probe or reset fails with -ENODEV. If CSTS reads 0xffffffff the loop exits immediately with -ENODEV (device gone from the bus).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CAP.TO (bits 31:24) is the worst-case time, in 500 ms units, the host should wait for CSTS.RDY to change after CC.EN changes. Maximum 0xFF = 127.5 s.
Typical causes
Firmware boot slower than advertised (e.g. large mapping-table rebuild after unsafe shutdown); media or NAND not responding; CAP.TO set too small by firmware; controller waiting on a dependency (power, clocks, PERST# timing); host started polling before the device was out of reset.
How to detect / prove
Measure: dmesg -T timestamps of driver bind vs failure; with an analyzer, find the MWr to CC (offset 14h) and the last MRd of CSTS (offset 1Ch) and compare the gap with CAP.TO×500 ms. Read CAP with nvme show-regs /dev/nvme0 (read-only) and decode bits 31:24.
In a trace
A long string of MRd to BAR0+1Ch, each CplD returning RDY=0, starting just after the MWr to BAR0+14h with EN=1. Nothing else from the device. Gaps of 1–2 ms between polls (Linux sleeps 1–2 ms per loop).
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/core.c:nvme_enable_ctrl() computes the limit as (CAP.TO + 1) / 2 seconds and calls nvme_wait_ready(), which polls every 1–2 ms (usleep_range(1000, 2000)), aborts on fatal signal, and fails with -ENODEV on timeout or CSTS = ~0.
Test to provoke it
In QEMU, CAP.TO is 0Fh (7.5 s) in hw/nvme/ctrl.c; patch the model to delay setting RDY beyond that. On hardware, force an unsafe power cut during heavy writes (CAUTION: data-loss risk on that drive) and time the next initialisation against CAP.TO.
T-03CRTO inconsistent with CAP.TO, or media not ready in timeTimer onlyNVMe controller register
What happens
Controllers that support Controller Ready Modes (CAP.CRMS) report two timeouts in the CRTO register: CRWMT (ready with media) and CRIMT (ready independent of media). If CRWMT is smaller than CAP.TO, or the media is not ready within CRWMT, the host either waits too little or gives up.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2 logs once bad crto:<value> cap:<value> when CRWMT < CAP.TO and falls back to CAP.TO. If the controller then still misses the deadline, the T-02 message appears. When CC.CRIME=1 is used by a host, media commands issued too early complete with Admin Command Media Not Ready (generic 24h) or Namespace Not Ready (82h).
Spec basis
NVMe Base 2.x: CAP.CRMS (CRWMS bit 59, CRIMS bit 60), CC.CRIME (bit 24), CRTO register at offset 68h with CRWMT (bits 15:0) and CRIMT (bits 31:16), both in 500 ms units.
Typical causes
Firmware fills CRTO but forgets that CAP.TO must stay consistent; large-capacity drives whose media becomes ready much later than the controller.
How to detect / prove
nvme show-regs to read CAP and CRTO; compare CRWMT with CAP.TO. In dmesg, look for bad crto. Time the CC.EN→RDY gap on an analyzer.
In a trace
Same as T-02. With CRIME=1, an early Identify or Read completes quickly but with SC 24h or 82h; a correct device sets RDY late instead.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_enable_ctrl() reads NVME_REG_CRTO if NVME_CAP_CRMS_CRWMS, uses the larger of CAP.TO and CRWMT, and always clears NVME_CC_CRIME ("Until the driver is restructured to handle retries, disable CC.CRIME").
Test to provoke it
QEMU or a device simulator: set CAP.CRMS and an inconsistent CRTO; check for the bad crto warning. SAFE.
T-04CSTS.RDY does not return to 0 after CC.EN=0 (reset hangs)Timer onlyNVMe controller register
What happens
To reset, the host clears CC.EN and waits for CSTS.RDY to go to 0. The controller keeps RDY=1 beyond CAP.TO, so the reset cannot proceed and the controller cannot be re-enabled.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2: Device not ready; aborting reset, CSTS=0x1; the controller is then marked dead and its namespaces are removed. Every outstanding I/O fails.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: after CC.EN 1→0 the controller shall bring CSTS.RDY to 0 within CAP.TO. Some devices need an extra delay before the host starts checking RDY (see NVME_QUIRK_DELAY_BEFORE_CHK_RDY).
Typical causes
Firmware stuck in the very operation that caused the reset (a hung command, a media operation that cannot be interrupted); internal deadlock; PCIe link lost (CSTS reads all-ones instead, a different failure).
How to detect / prove
dmesg shows the "aborting reset" line after a timeout or nvme reset. Analyzer: MWr CC with EN=0, then MRd CSTS returning RDY=1 for longer than CAP.TO×500 ms. Check whether the device sent any TLPs in between (DMA still running after EN=0 is itself a bug).
In a trace
MWr BAR0+14h (EN=0) → repeated MRd BAR0+1Ch, CplD RDY=1. A correct device may keep DMA quiet and drops RDY within milliseconds.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_disable_ctrl(ctrl, false): optional msleep(NVME_QUIRK_DELAY_AMOUNT) (2300 ms), then nvme_wait_ready(ctrl, NVME_CSTS_RDY, 0, (NVME_CAP_TIMEOUT(cap)+1)/2, "reset"). Note: v7.2 uses CAP.TO, not CRTO, for the disable direction.
Test to provoke it
Issue nvme reset /dev/nvme0 while a vendor debug hook keeps the firmware busy, or during a long Format. CAUTION: may leave the drive needing a power cycle.
T-05Shutdown not complete (CSTS.SHST ≠ 10b) before power is removedTimer onlyNVMe controller register
What happens
At power-off, suspend or driver unload the host writes CC.SHN=01b (normal shutdown) and waits for CSTS.SHST=10b (shutdown processing complete). If the controller takes longer than the host waits, power may be removed while the write cache and mapping tables are still being saved.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2: Device not ready; aborting shutdown, CSTS=0x.... At the next boot the drive's SMART / Health log Unsafe Shutdowns counter has increased, and initialisation may be slower (rebuild), possibly exceeding CAP.TO (T-02).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CC.SHN (01b normal, 10b abrupt), CSTS.SHST (00b normal operation, 01b processing, 10b complete). Identify Controller RTD3E gives the typical time in microseconds to enter Runtime D3 (shutdown); RTD3R the expected resume time.
Typical causes
Large volatile cache to flush; RTD3E under-reported; host value too small (Linux default 5 s); platform cuts power on a fixed timer regardless of SHST.
How to detect / prove
nvme smart-log /dev/nvme0 before and after a clean reboot: unsafe_shutdowns must not increase. nvme id-ctrl /dev/nvme0 | grep -i rtd3 for RTD3E. dmesg for D3 entry latency set to N seconds (Linux applied RTD3E) and for the aborting-shutdown line.
In a trace
MWr CC with SHN=01b; MRd CSTS polls with SHST=01b; the series ends (power removed or host gives up) without a CplD showing SHST=10b.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_init_identify() sets ctrl->shutdown_timeout = clamp(RTD3E/1e6, shutdown_timeout, 60) seconds; module parameter nvme_core.shutdown_timeout (default 5). nvme_disable_ctrl(ctrl, true) sets NVME_CC_SHN_NORMAL and waits for NVME_CSTS_SHST_CMPLT.
Test to provoke it
Fill the write cache (sustained writes with volatile write cache on), then reboot and compare unsafe_shutdowns. Try nvme_core.shutdown_timeout=1 on a test machine to see how a too-short host limit shows up. CAUTION: risk of losing cached data on that drive.
T-06Resume from runtime D3 slower than RTD3RDegradedNVMe power management
What happens
After a runtime-D3 or system suspend, the controller takes much longer to resume than the RTD3R value it advertises. Nothing fails, but the first I/O after resume stalls.
How it shows up
No error. Applications see a multi-second stall after resume; if it exceeds CAP.TO the re-enable fails (T-02). dmesg timestamps between resume start and the controller becoming live show the gap.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Identify Controller RTD3R (Runtime D3 Resume Latency, µs, expected) and RTD3E (entry latency). These are informational: the host uses them to decide whether runtime D3 is worth it.
Typical causes
Mapping table reload from NAND; firmware re-training of internal components; platform delays on the PCIe side (D3cold power ramp, link training, see T-40 and T-41).
How to detect / prove
Measure with dmesg -T or ftrace on the PM callbacks (pci_pm_resume, nvme_resume) and compare with nvme id-ctrl RTD3R. Run fio with latency logging across a suspend cycle.
In a trace
Power-up and link training, config reads (possibly RRS), then MWr CC EN=1 and a long CSTS polling series.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux v7.2 does not use RTD3R in the NVMe host driver (only RTD3E, for the shutdown timeout). Resume paths: pci.c:nvme_resume(), core.c:nvme_enable_ctrl().
Test to provoke it
rtcwake -m mem -s 10 on a laptop with a fio latency log running. SAFE.
T-07Keep Alive Timer expires in the controllerTimer onlyNVMe over Fabrics
What happens
The host sets a Keep Alive Timeout (KATO) at connect time or via Set Features (FID 0Fh). If the controller does not receive a Keep Alive command (or, with Traffic Based Keep Alive, any command) within KATO, it concludes the host is gone.
How it shows up
On the controller: the association is torn down, outstanding commands may complete with Keep Alive Timer Expired (generic status 19h) and the host's resources (reservations held by the host, queues) are released. On the host: the transport connection drops, the controller goes to RESETTING/CONNECTING and reconnects (T-28).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Keep Alive command (admin opcode 18h); Keep Alive Timer feature (FID 0Fh), KATO in milliseconds; Identify Controller KAS = timer granularity in 100 ms units; CTRATT.TBKAS = Traffic Based Keep Alive support. The host should send Keep Alive at a rate that allows for round-trip time (Linux comment quotes "at half of the Keep Alive Timeout").
Typical causes
Host CPU starvation or a stalled admin queue (the Keep Alive itself is queued behind blocked admin commands); network congestion or packet loss; a target that processes admin commands slowly; KATO set close to the network RTT.
How to detect / prove
Host dmesg: reconnect messages, long keepalive RTT (N ms). Target-side logs (e.g. Linux nvmet prints keep-alive expiry). Packet capture: interval between Keep Alive capsules (admin opcode 18h) versus KATO.
In a trace
TCP/RDMA capture: Keep Alive command capsules every KATO/2 (or KATO/4 with TBKAS in Linux); before the failure the gap grows to ≥ KATO, then the target closes the connection (TCP FIN/RST or RDMA disconnect).
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_keep_alive_work_period() = KATO/2 (halved again if TBKAS); nvme_keep_alive_work() sends the command with rq->timeout = kato * HZ; fabrics.c:nvmf_parse_options() defaults KATO to NVME_DEFAULT_KATO (5 s) for I/O controllers; fabrics.c:nvmf_connect_cmd_prep() puts KATO×1000 into the Connect command.
Test to provoke it
nvme-tcp to a Linux nvmet target; add delay with tc qdisc add dev eth0 root netem delay 3000ms on the host (CAUTION: affects all traffic on that interface). Watch the target drop the association after KATO.
T-08Keep Alive command itself slow or failing (host side)Timer onlyNVMe over Fabrics
What happens
The host's Keep Alive command takes most of the keep-alive period to complete, or fails. The host cannot keep the controller's timer refreshed in time.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2 warns long keepalive RTT (N ms) when the round trip exceeds the work period, and failed nvme_keep_alive_end_io error=N on failure. If the request cannot even be allocated: keep-alive failed: N followed by a controller reset.
Spec basis
NVMe Base Keep Alive; the host-side scheduling is implementation policy.
Typical causes
Reserved admin tags exhausted; admin queue blocked by long commands (Format, Sanitize start, Firmware Commit); network latency; target overloaded.
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -i keepalive; trace with nvme_setup_cmd/nvme_complete_rq tracepoints (/sys/kernel/tracing/events/nvme) filtering admin opcode 0x18.
In a trace
Keep Alive capsule sent on time; the response capsule arrives late.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_keep_alive_end_io() subtracts the measured RTT from the next delay and logs the warning; nvme_keep_alive_work() resets the controller if blk_mq_alloc_request() fails. A Set Features KATO passed through user space is applied by nvme_update_keep_alive() ("keep alive interval updated from ... ms to ... ms").
Test to provoke it
Same netem setup as T-07, with delays between KATO/2 and KATO. SAFE on a lab network.
T-09Abort does not abort (or cannot be sent)Timer onlyNVMe admin command
What happens
After an I/O timeout the host sends Abort (admin 08h) naming the SQID and CID. Abort is best-effort: the controller may complete it without aborting anything, or the host may have no Abort credits left.
How it shows up
Abort CQE Dword 0 bit 0 = 1 means "command not aborted". Linux v7.2 logs only Abort status: 0x0 and does not inspect Dword 0; it relies on the second timeout to reset. If more Aborts are outstanding than ACL+1, the controller returns Abort Command Limit Exceeded (103h); Linux avoids that by keeping its own budget and simply re-arming the timer.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Abort command; Identify Controller ACL (0's based; the specification recommends 3, i.e. four concurrent Aborts); command-specific status 103h; an aborted command completes with Command Abort Requested (generic 07h).
Typical causes
Firmware cannot locate the command (already inside the media pipeline); controller ignores Abort entirely; Abort itself stuck behind the hung command on the admin queue.
How to detect / prove
Analyzer: Abort SQE fetched from the admin SQ, its CQE written with DW0 bit 0 = 1, and no CQE for the target CID. dmesg: "aborting" followed ~io_timeout later by "reset controller" for the same tag.
In a trace
CQE for Abort (SQID 0) with DW0=00000001h; no completion with SC 07h for the victim CID.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout(): atomic_dec_return(&dev->ctrl.abort_limit) (initialised to ACL+1 in core.c:nvme_init_identify()), sets IOD_ABORTED, sends Abort with abort_endio(); a second timeout on an IOD_ABORTED request resets. QEMU hw/nvme/ctrl.c:nvme_abort() sets result=1 by default and only reports 0 when it aborts an AER.
Test to provoke it
QEMU with a stalled backend (e.g. a blkdebug or throttled image) and a low io_timeout; watch the Abort CQE result. SAFE.
T-10Firmware activation takes longer than MTFATimer onlyNVMe admin / firmware
What happens
Firmware Commit with activation without reset pauses command processing (CSTS.PP=1) while the new image starts. If the pause lasts longer than MTFA, every I/O on the host stalls for that time and the host may reset the controller.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2: Fw activation timeout, reset controller. Applications see an I/O pause of up to MTFA (or up to admin_timeout when MTFA=0). If the controller knows activation would exceed MTFA it should refuse up front with Firmware Activation Requires Maximum Time Violation (command-specific 112h).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Identify Controller MTFA (100 ms units); CSTS.PP (Processing Paused, bit 5); Firmware Activation Notice asynchronous event; status 112h.
Typical causes
New firmware image with longer init; image upgrade that migrates internal data structures; MTFA left at an optimistic value.
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ctrl | grep -i mtfa; run fio with --write_lat_log during nvme fw-commit -a 3 and measure the pause; analyzer: CSTS MRd showing PP=1 and its duration.
In a trace
AER completion (Notice: Firmware Activation Starting), then CSTS reads with PP=1; device sends no CQEs until PP clears.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_fw_act_work(): quiesces I/O queues, polls nvme_ctrl_pp_status() every 100 ms until mtfa * 100 ms (or admin_timeout seconds if MTFA=0), then nvme_try_sched_reset().
Test to provoke it
Activate a firmware image with commit action 3 (activate without reset) under load. EXPERT: firmware updates can brick a drive; use vendor-approved images only.
T-11Format NVM outlasts the host admin timeoutTimer onlyNVMe admin command
What happens
Format NVM (80h) runs synchronously: the CQE is posted when the format is done. On a large drive or with Secure Erase Settings = user data erase, that can take minutes. The host's admin timeout (60 s in Linux) fires first.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2 times out an admin command and goes straight to reset: I/O tag N (....) opcode 0x80 (Format NVM) QID 0 timeout, reset controller. The reset interrupts the format; afterwards the namespace may report Format In Progress (84h) or be unusable until a new format.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Format NVM has no advertised duration. Commands to the affected namespace during the format complete with Format In Progress (84h). FNA in Identify Controller describes the scope of a format/secure erase.
Typical causes
Using the default admin timeout for a long operation; tools that do not pass a longer timeout.
How to detect / prove
dmesg line with opcode 0x80 on QID 0. Pass an explicit timeout: nvme format /dev/nvme0n1 --timeout=600000 (milliseconds; check your nvme-cli version's default).
In a trace
Format SQE fetched from the admin SQ; no CQE; 60 s later a CC.EN=0 write (reset).
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout(): if (!nvmeq->qid || ...) → reset for any admin-queue timeout, no Abort. Passthrough timeout: ioctl.c uses cmd.timeout_ms when non-zero.
Test to provoke it
Format a large namespace with SES=1 with --timeout=60000 on a lab drive. EXPERT/DESTRUCTIVE: erases all data on the namespace.
T-12Sanitize runs far longer than its estimate (or never finishes)DegradedNVMe admin / background
What happens
Sanitize (84h) completes immediately and the operation continues in the background. The controller publishes estimated durations in the Sanitize Status log. If the operation takes much longer than estimated, or SPROG stops moving, the drive is unusable for that time and no timer fires.
How it shows up
No error. Most I/O and many admin commands fail with Sanitize In Progress (generic 1Dh) for the whole duration. Sanitize Status log: SSTAT = in progress, SPROG (numerator over 65536) stuck. On failure SSTAT reports "failed" and later commands get Sanitize Failed (1Ch).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Sanitize Status log (LID 81h) fields SPROG, SSTAT, SCDW10, ETO, ETBE, ETCE and the No-Deallocate variants ETOND, ETBEND, ETCEND (seconds; 0 = completes in the background when the command completes; FFFFFFFFh = not reported). A sanitize continues across resets and power cycles.
Typical causes
Block erase on a drive with many bad blocks; overwrite passes; firmware estimate based on a different capacity; firmware hang.
How to detect / prove
Poll nvme sanitize-log /dev/nvme0 and record SPROG versus time; compare the elapsed time with the relevant estimate. A tool should flag "elapsed > k × estimate" (pick k, e.g. 2).
In a trace
Sanitize SQE and an immediate success CQE; afterwards periodic Get Log Page (02h, LID 81h) from the host; I/O CQEs with SC 1Dh.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux v7.2 has no Sanitize timer; the CQE arrives quickly. The driver records failures in passthrough logs only.
Test to provoke it
nvme sanitize /dev/nvme0 -a 2 (block erase) and poll the log. EXPERT/DESTRUCTIVE: erases the whole NVM subsystem's user data.
T-13Device self-test exceeds EDSTT (or short test exceeds its limit)DegradedNVMe admin / background
What happens
Device Self-test (admin 14h) runs in the background. The extended test should finish in about EDSTT minutes; the short test has a fixed upper bound in the specification. A test that keeps running hides a slow or failing internal component.
How it shows up
No error while it runs; a second self-test request gets Device Self-test In Progress (command-specific 11Dh). The Device Self-test log shows Current Operation and Current Completion percentage. The final result entry records abort reasons or a failing segment.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Identify Controller EDSTT (minutes, extended test in power state 0); short self-test time limit (two minutes in NVMe Base; verify in your spec revision); Device Self-test log (LID 06h).
Typical causes
Media with many weak blocks; self-test throttled by host I/O (allowed); thermal throttling (T-49).
How to detect / prove
Start nvme device-self-test /dev/nvme0 -s 2; poll nvme self-test-log /dev/nvme0 and compare elapsed time with nvme id-ctrl | grep edstt.
In a trace
Self-test SQE and fast CQE; periodic Get Log Page LID 06h.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux v7.2 does not time self-tests; the command completes immediately.
Test to provoke it
Run the extended test idle and again under full fio load; compare durations. SAFE (self-test is non-destructive by definition; still avoid on production drives).
T-14Power-state exit slower than EXLAT (latency spike after idle)DegradedNVMe power management
What happens
With Autonomous Power State Transition (APST) the controller drops into non-operational power states after an idle period. The first command after idle waits for the exit. If the real exit latency exceeds the advertised EXLAT, the host's latency budget is broken without any error.
How it shows up
No error. A periodic latency outlier on the first I/O after idle (often tens of milliseconds). Linux excludes states whose EXLAT > ps_max_latency_us, so a device that under-reports EXLAT defeats that protection.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Power State Descriptor ENLAT/EXLAT (µs, 0 = not reported), NON-OP flag; Autonomous Power State Transition feature (FID 0Ch) entries with Idle Time Prior to Transition (ms) and Idle Transition Power State; Identify Controller APSTA, NPSS.
Typical causes
Firmware under-reports EXLAT; platform adds PCIe L1/L1.2 exit on top (T-45, T-46); host idle timeout too short (thrashing between states).
How to detect / prove
nvme id-ctrl -H /dev/nvme0 for power state table; nvme get-feature -f 0x0c -H /dev/nvme0 for the APST table Linux programmed; fio with --thinktime larger than the idle time, recording clat; compare outlier size with EXLAT. Disable APST (nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us=0) to confirm the outliers disappear.
In a trace
Idle period on the link (probably in L1), then doorbell MWr; the device's first SQE fetch MRd arrives late (exit time). An analyzer that shows link power states will show L1 exit first.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_configure_apst() walks states from deepest up, skips states with exit_lat > ps_max_latency_us or quirked deepest, uses apst_primary_timeout_ms (100 ms) for states with total latency ≤ 15 ms and apst_secondary_timeout_ms (2000 ms) for ≤ 100 ms; without those parameters it uses (ENLAT+EXLAT)/20 ms ("50 × (enlat + exlat) microseconds"). Per-device limit via PM QoS (/sys/class/nvme/nvme0/power/pm_qos_latency_tolerance_us, nvme_set_latency_tolerance()).
Test to provoke it
Sweep fio --thinktime across the APST idle times and plot first-I/O latency. SAFE.
T-15Controller never wakes from a deep power state (falls off the bus)Timer onlyNVMe power / PCIe
What happens
After entering a deep non-operational state (often combined with PCIe ASPM L1 substates), the device fails to come back: config and MMIO reads return all-ones.
How it shows up
The next I/O times out (30 s). Linux v7.2 then reads CSTS = 0xffffffff and prints controller is down; will reset: CSTS=0xffffffff, PCI_STATUS=0x... followed by Does your device have a faulty power saving mode enabled? and Try "nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us=0 pcie_aspm=off pcie_port_pm=off" and report a bug. The reset usually fails and the namespace disappears.
Spec basis
NVMe Base APST and power states; PCIe ASPM and L1 PM substates. No timer in either spec catches this; only the host I/O timer does.
Typical causes
Firmware bugs in the deepest power state; platform BIOS/ACPI problems; L1.2 timing parameters wrong (T-46). Linux carries DMI-based quirks for specific laptop/board and SSD combinations (Samsung SM951/PM951 on Dell XPS 15 9550, Samsung 960 EVO on some ASUS boards, a Lenovo N60z board).
How to detect / prove
The all-ones CSTS line in dmesg. Reproduce with APST disabled (default_ps_max_latency_us=0) and with ASPM off; if the problem disappears, it is a power-state exit failure. lspci -vvv after the event: "Unknown header type 7f" or all-ones config space means the device is gone.
In a trace
Link enters L1/L1.2 during idle; on the next doorbell, the root port starts exit but the link never returns to L0 (LTSSM goes to Detect) or the endpoint does not respond; root port logs a Completion Timeout or Surprise Down.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_warn_reset() prints the hints; pci.c:check_vendor_combination_bug() returns NVME_QUIRK_NO_DEEPEST_PS or NVME_QUIRK_NO_APST by DMI; core.c quirk table entries for specific models.
Test to provoke it
Idle the system for long periods (hours) with APST and ASPM enabled, polling with a cron job that issues one read every N minutes. SAFE, but may require a power cycle to recover.
T-16Host Memory Buffer missing or disabled: DRAM-less drive becomes slowDegradedNVMe feature
What happens
DRAM-less SSDs ask the host for a Host Memory Buffer (HMB) to cache mapping tables. If the host gives less than requested, or disables the HMB (for example across suspend), the drive keeps working but random-access latency rises.
How it shows up
No error. Linux may log min host memory (N MiB) above limit (128 MiB). and run without HMB, or failed to allocate host memory buffer. ("controller must work without HMB"). Random 4 KiB reads get markedly slower; the effect is device-specific.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Host Memory Buffer feature (FID 0Dh); Identify Controller HMPRE (preferred size) and HMMIN (minimum), both in 4 KiB units, HMMINDS, HMMAXD.
Typical causes
Host limit lower than HMMIN; memory fragmentation; host disables HMB on suspend and the drive has to rebuild its cache on resume.
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -i "host memory"; nvme get-feature -f 0x0d -H /dev/nvme0 shows whether HMB is enabled; sysfs /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/hmb (present when HMPRE ≠ 0). Compare fio random-read latency with and without HMB.
In a trace
With HMB the device issues MRd/MWr to host memory addresses inside the HMB descriptor list during I/O; without it those accesses disappear and NAND reads per I/O increase (not visible on PCIe).
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_setup_host_mem() caps the size at max_host_mem_size_mb (module parameter, default 128); pci.c:nvme_suspend() calls nvme_set_host_mem(ndev, 0) before suspend ("Host memory access may not be successful in a system suspend state").
Test to provoke it
Load nvme with max_host_mem_size_mb=0 on a DRAM-less drive and compare 4 KiB random-read latency. SAFE.
T-17Command Retry Delay ignored, missing or too longReportedNVMe command status
What happens
A controller can ask the host to wait before retrying: it sets the CRD field in the CQE status (1–3) and the host waits CRDT1/2/3 × 100 ms. If CRDT is zero while CRD is used, the host retries instantly and burns its retry budget; if CRDT is very large, every retry adds seconds of latency.
How it shows up
Completions with DNR=0 and a non-zero CRD (e.g. Namespace Not Ready 82h, Command Interrupted 21h). With Linux, each retry increments command_retries_count in sysfs; after max_retries (5) the error reaches the application.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CQE Status field CRD (bits 12:11 of the status word as Linux stores it, mask 0x1800); Identify Controller CRDT1–CRDT3 (100 ms units); Host Behavior Support feature (FID 16h) ACRE bit enables the mechanism.
Typical causes
Firmware returning CRD before the host enabled ACRE; CRDT left at 0; CRDT set to many seconds for a transient condition.
How to detect / prove
Tracepoint nvme:nvme_complete_rq shows status and retries; nvme id-ctrl | grep -i crdt; nvme get-feature -f 0x16 -H for ACRE.
In a trace
CQE with status CRD≠0, then (after the delay) the same command resubmitted with a new CID.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_retry_req(): delay = ctrl->crdt[crd - 1] * 100 ms, then blk_mq_delay_kick_requeue_list(); nvme_configure_host_options() sets ACRE only when CRDT1 ≠ 0; nvme_decide_disposition() stops retrying on DNR or retries >= nvme_max_retries.
Test to provoke it
QEMU or a target with a hook returning 82h with CRD=1; vary CRDT1 and measure added latency. SAFE.
T-18Namespace stays "not ready" longer than the host retriesReportedNVMe command status
What happens
After a reset, resume or capacity change, the namespace answers with Namespace Not Ready (82h) or Admin Command Media Not Ready (24h) for longer than the host is willing to retry.
How it shows up
First a few retried commands, then I/O errors reported to the application (blk_update_request: I/O error-style messages, the NVMe status in nvme_log_error() output).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: generic status 82h (Namespace Not Ready, retry allowed when DNR=0) and 24h (Admin Command Media Not Ready, used with CC.CRIME=1).
Typical causes
Media initialisation slower than the controller ready time; controller sets RDY too early; host retry budget (5 × CRDT) shorter than the real wait.
How to detect / prove
dmesg NVMe error lines with status 0x82; /sys/block/nvme0n1/device/.../command_retries_count; correlate with time since controller enable.
In a trace
Repeated I/O CQEs with SC 82h shortly after CSTS.RDY=1, each resubmitted, last one returned to the host as error.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_decide_disposition(), nvme_retry_req(), nvme_error_status() maps the status to a block error. CRIME is kept off in nvme_enable_ctrl(), which avoids most 24h cases.
Test to provoke it
Power-cycle a large drive after an unsafe shutdown and start I/O immediately when the block device appears. CAUTION.
T-19ANA transition exceeds ANATTTimer onlyNVMe multipath (ANA)
What happens
In Asymmetric Namespace Access (ANA), a group can report the "change" state while it moves between optimized, non-optimized and inaccessible. ANATT is the maximum time the controller promises to stay in that state. If it stays longer, the host cannot use the path.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2 arms a timer at 2 × ANATT when any group is in change state; on expiry: ANATT timeout, resetting controller. I/O on that path completes with ANA Transition (path-related status 303h) and is failed over.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Identify Controller ANATT (seconds), ANACAP; ANA log page; path-related statuses ANA Persistent Loss 301h, ANA Inaccessible 302h, ANA Transition 303h.
Typical causes
Target failover taking longer than declared (storage array controller takeover); target bug that never leaves change state.
How to detect / prove
nvme ana-log /dev/nvme0 repeatedly; sysfs ana_state per namespace path; dmesg for the ANATT line; multipath_failover_count in sysfs.
In a trace
Fabrics capture: AEN for ANA change, Get Log Page ANA, groups in change state; I/O completions with 303h.
Linux v7.2 source
multipath.c:nvme_read_ana_log() (and nvme_mpath_update()): mod_timer(&ctrl->anatt_timer, ctrl->anatt * HZ * 2 + jiffies) ("double the ANATT value to provide some slack"); nvme_anatt_timeout() calls nvme_reset_ctrl().
Test to provoke it
Linux nvmet: set a port's ANA group to change (configfs ana_groups/N/ana_state) and leave it there. SAFE in a lab.
T-20Controller timestamp drifts or stopsSilentNVMe feature
What happens
The host sets the controller clock with the Timestamp feature; the controller keeps counting. Over long uptimes the device clock can drift, and in some power states it may stop. Device-side logs (Persistent Event Log, telemetry) then carry wrong times.
How it shows up
Nothing. Log entries cannot be correlated with host logs; a device event appears minutes away from the host symptom it caused.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Timestamp feature (FID 0Eh): 48-bit milliseconds since midnight 1 January 1970 UTC, plus attribute bits giving the timestamp origin and a "Synch" indication that the count may have stopped (exact semantics: verify). ONCS bit indicates support.
Typical causes
Oscillator tolerance; clock not counting in non-operational power states; host sets it only at controller initialisation.
How to detect / prove
nvme get-feature -f 0x0e -H /dev/nvme0 and compare with date +%s%3N; repeat a day later to measure drift.
In a trace
Set Features 0Eh at init (host to device data 8 bytes); later Get Features 0Eh returns the controller's value.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_configure_timestamp() is called from nvme_init_ctrl_finish(), i.e. on every controller (re)initialisation, with ktime_get_real() in ms; failure logs could not set timestamp. QEMU computes the value from its virtual clock (nvme_get_timestamp()).
Test to provoke it
Read the timestamp periodically over a week of uptime; plot device minus host. SAFE.
🐞 Debugging connection

When a controller-state timeout (T-02, T-04, T-05) happens, always read CSTS one more time by hand: nvme show-regs /dev/nvme0. CSTS = 0xffffffff means the problem is below NVMe (link or power, see chapter 3.4). CSTS.CFS = 1 means the controller declared a fatal error itself. RDY stuck with CFS = 0 means the firmware is alive enough to answer register reads but not progressing — collect a telemetry log (nvme telemetry-log) before resetting it.

3.3 Host Timers in Linux v7.2: Timeouts, Retries, Resets and Reconnects

← 3.2 NVMe Time Limits: Ready, Shutdown, Keep Alive, Abort, Activation and FriendsIV.1 The deallocate that didn't: a full worked case →

The NVMe and PCIe specifications say what the device promises. The Linux host decides how long it will actually wait, and what to do afterwards. Every value in this chapter is impl Linux v7.2 behaviour, read from drivers/nvme/host/*.c, block/blk-mq.c and drivers/pci/pci.c; other operating systems and other kernel versions differ.

3.3.1 Where the knobs are

KnobDefaultScopeHow to read / change
nvme_core.io_timeout30 sAll NVMe I/O queues created afterwards/sys/module/nvme_core/parameters/io_timeout; per controller (ms): /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/io_timeout; per disk (ms): /sys/block/nvme0n1/queue/io_timeout
nvme_core.admin_timeout60 sAdmin queue (and fabrics queue)Module parameter; per controller (ms): /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/admin_timeout
nvme_core.max_retries5All commandsModule parameter
nvme_core.shutdown_timeout5 sLower bound; RTD3E may raise it up to 60 sModule parameter; dmesg "D3 entry latency set to N seconds"
nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us100000APST deepest allowed exit latencyModule parameter; per device PM QoS
apst_primary_timeout_ms / apst_secondary_timeout_ms100 / 2000APST idle timesModule parameters
apst_primary_latency_tol_us / apst_secondary_latency_tol_us15000 / 100000Which states use which idle timeModule parameters
nvme.max_host_mem_size_mb128HMB capModule parameter (read-only at runtime)
ctrl_loss_tmo, reconnect_delay, fast_io_fail_tmo, keep_alive_tmo600 s, 10 s, -1 (off), 5 sPer fabrics controllernvme connect ... --ctrl-loss-tmo= --reconnect-delay= --fast_io_fail_tmo= --keep-alive-tmo=; sysfs /sys/class/nvme/nvmeX/ctrl_loss_tmo etc.
nvme_tcp.tls_handshake_timeout10 sNVMe/TCP with TLSModule parameter
delayed_removal_secs0Per multipath headsysfs on the head disk
⚠️ Changing timeouts

Writing to these files is safe in the sense that it cannot damage the drive, but on a production system a shorter timeout turns slow-but-successful commands into resets, and a longer one turns a hung device into a hung application. Change them on test machines; record the original values first.

3.3.2 What a timeout looks like in the log

Synthetic kernel log — one Read that never completes (format follows the v7.2 dev_warn strings; values invented)
TimeMessageMeaning
t+0 s(nothing)Read submitted on QID 3, tag 17
t+30 snvme nvme0: I/O tag 17 (0011) opcode 0x2 (Read) QID 3 timeout, aborting req_op:READ(0) size:4096First expiry; CQ polled, nothing found; Abort sent
t+30 snvme nvme0: Abort status: 0x0Abort completed successfully — which does not mean the Read was aborted
t+60 snvme nvme0: I/O tag 17 (0011) opcode 0x2 (Read) QID 3 timeout, reset controllerSecond expiry; IOD_ABORTED already set
t+60 s(reset; requests cancelled with host-aborted status and retried)nvme_dev_disable(), nvme_reset_work()
t+61 snvme nvme0: N/0/0 default/read/poll queuesController back; retried I/O completes
T-21Missed interrupt: completion found only by pollingDegradedLinux host / interrupts
What happens
The controller posted the CQE and (maybe) sent the MSI-X message, but the host never ran the completion handler. The command sits "in flight" until the timeout handler polls the CQ and finds it.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2: I/O tag N (CID) QID Q timeout, completion polled. The command succeeds, but 30 s late. No reset.
Spec basis
NVMe over PCIe: MSI-X vector per CQ, interrupt coalescing (Feature 08h), INTMS/INTMC registers for pin/MSI. PCIe: MSI-X is a posted MWr to the address in the MSI-X table.
Typical causes
MSI-X table or address programmed wrong (e.g. after a VM migration or IOMMU remap); interrupt remapping fault; CPU hot-unplug with vector affinity; device bug that writes the CQE but skips the MSI-X write; interrupt coalescing that never fires because the threshold is not reached and the timer is broken.
How to detect / prove
Count "completion polled" lines in dmesg; check /proc/interrupts for the nvme queue vector not incrementing; IOMMU fault messages (DMAR: ... fault). On an analyzer, find the CQE MWr and check for the following MSI-X MWr to the APIC address range.
In a trace
CQE MWr present, phase bit correct; MSI-X MWr missing or addressed wrongly. 30 s later the host reads the CQ directly from memory (not visible on PCIe) and rings the CQ head doorbell.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout(): nvme_poll() for polled queues or nvme_poll_irqdisable(); if blk_mq_rq_state(req) != MQ_RQ_IN_FLIGHT afterwards, logs "completion polled" and returns BLK_EH_DONE.
Test to provoke it
Mask the queue's MSI-X vector through a debug interface, or use QEMU and drop interrupts in the model; with a short io_timeout the message appears. SAFE in a VM.
T-22I/O command timeout: poll, Abort, then resetTimer onlyLinux host / nvme-pci
What happens
An I/O command passes its block-layer deadline (io_timeout, 30 s). Linux escalates: check for a dead or offline device, poll the CQ, send an NVMe Abort, and on the second expiry reset the controller.
How it shows up
I/O tag N (CID) opcode 0x.. (Name) QID Q timeout, aborting req_op:... size:..., then possibly ... timeout, reset controller. If PCI error recovery is in progress (pci_channel_offline()), the timer is simply re-armed. If CSTS.CFS=1 or NSSRO is set, the driver resets at once with controller is down; will reset: CSTS=0x..., PCI_STATUS=0x....
Spec basis
Host policy; NVMe Abort and controller reset are spec mechanisms used by the policy.
Typical causes
Any device stall (see T-01); a firmware bug affecting a specific opcode or LBA; Completion Timeouts on the device's DMA (T-33); a device that is in a long internal operation (GC, T-48) exceeding 30 s.
How to detect / prove
dmesg timeout lines (tag, CID, opcode, QID, size); nvme error-log; AER status of the device and Root Port for the same time window; tracepoints nvme:nvme_setup_cmd and nvme:nvme_complete_rq to measure per-command latency before the failure.
In a trace
SQE fetched; no CQE; 30 s later Abort SQE on the admin queue; 30 s later the reset sequence (CC.EN=0, CSTS polling, admin queue re-creation).
Linux v7.2 source
block/blk-mq.c:blk_mq_timeout_work() → blk_mq_rq_timed_out() → pci.c:nvme_timeout(); helpers nvme_should_reset(), nvme_warn_reset(), abort_endio(), nvme_dev_disable(), nvme_try_sched_reset(). Timeout value set by core.c:nvme_alloc_io_tag_set() (NVME_IO_TIMEOUT).
Test to provoke it
echo 2000 > /sys/class/nvme/nvme0/io_timeout on a test box, then generate long commands (e.g. very large Write Zeroes or Verify ranges on a slow device) to see the ladder quickly. CAUTION: resets interrupt other I/O.
T-23Admin command timeout: immediate controller resetTimer onlyLinux host / nvme-pci
What happens
Any command on the admin queue (QID 0) that exceeds admin_timeout (60 s) causes a controller reset straight away; Linux never sends an Abort for an admin command.
How it shows up
I/O tag N (CID) opcode 0x.. (Name) QID 0 timeout, reset controller. Typical opcodes: Format NVM (0x80), Sanitize (0x84), Firmware Commit (0x10), Get Log Page of a large telemetry log (0x02), vendor-specific commands.
Spec basis
Host policy.
Typical causes
Long synchronous admin commands; controller stuck in an internal operation; a firmware path that blocks the admin queue while background work runs.
How to detect / prove
QID 0 in the timeout message. For tools: pass an explicit timeout (nvme ... --timeout=<ms>) for long operations.
In a trace
Admin SQE fetched, no CQE; then CC.EN=0.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout(): if (!nvmeq->qid || (iod->flags & IOD_ABORTED)) → reset. Admin timeout configured in core.c:nvme_alloc_admin_tag_set() (NVME_ADMIN_TIMEOUT); changeable through sysfs admin_timeout (sysfs.c:nvme_admin_timeout_store()).
Test to provoke it
See T-11. EXPERT when the command is destructive.
T-24Retry budget exhausted (max_retries)ReportedLinux host / nvme-core
What happens
Retryable errors (DNR=0) and cancelled commands after a reset are retried. After max_retries (5) attempts, or immediately when DNR=1, the error reaches the block layer and the application.
How it shows up
An application I/O error; kernel NVMe error log line with the final status; sysfs command_retries_count and command_error_count per namespace increase.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: DNR bit, CRD field. Retry count is host policy.
Typical causes
Repeated resets (each reset cancels and retries all in-flight commands); a namespace that stays not-ready (T-18); transient transport errors on fabrics.
How to detect / prove
cat /sys/block/nvme0n1/device/../nvme0n1/command_retries_count (path varies; find with find /sys -name command_retries_count); tracepoint nvme:nvme_complete_rq prints retries.
In a trace
The same LBA range resubmitted with new CIDs, separated by resets or by CRDT delays.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_decide_disposition() (retries >= nvme_max_retries), nvme_retry_req(), nvme_cancel_request() (sets NVME_SC_HOST_ABORTED_CMD on reset).
Test to provoke it
Inject errors with drivers/nvme/host/fault_inject.c (debugfs directory per disk with status and dont_retry files plus the standard fault-attr controls); clear dont_retry to watch retries. SAFE.
T-25Timeout while the controller is resetting or connectingTimer onlyLinux host / nvme-pci
What happens
A command (often an admin command issued during re-initialisation) times out while the controller is already in RESETTING or CONNECTING. The host cannot reset again, so it gives up on the controller.
How it shows up
I/O tag N (CID) QID Q timeout, disable controller (CONNECTING or DELETING); the controller is marked for deletion and the namespaces disappear. During RESETTING the timer is just re-armed.
Spec basis
Host policy.
Typical causes
Device fails during its own recovery: Identify or Create I/O Queue never completes after a reset; flaky link that drops again during re-init.
How to detect / prove
dmesg sequence: a reset, then "timeout, disable controller", then the device node vanishes (nvme list).
In a trace
Reset sequence starts; admin SQEs (Identify 06h, Set Features 09h with Number of Queues FID 07h, Create I/O CQ 05h / SQ 01h) fetched but not completed.
Linux v7.2 source
pci.c:nvme_timeout() state switch: NVME_CTRL_CONNECTING → change to DELETING, nvme_dev_disable(dev, true); NVME_CTRL_RESETTING → BLK_EH_RESET_TIMER. For fabrics, tcp.c:nvme_tcp_timeout() and rdma.c:nvme_rdma_timeout() complete the request immediately when not LIVE.
Test to provoke it
