Make the device fail a second time during reset (vendor hook or QEMU model). CAUTION.
T-26Multipath failover delayed by the full timeoutDegradedLinux host / multipath
What happens
With native NVMe multipath, a path-related status (SCT 3h) fails over immediately. A path that simply stops answering gives no status, so failover waits for the command timeout (and for fabrics, the transport's error recovery).
How it shows up
I/O pauses for about io_timeout (30 s) — or KATO / transport detection time on fabrics — then continues on the other path. multipath_failover_count in sysfs increases. No application error.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: path-related status codes (Host Path Error 370h, Host Aborted Command 371h, ANA statuses 301h–303h).
Typical causes
Hung path rather than failed path; long io_timeout chosen for single-path use left in place on multipath systems.
How to detect / prove
Latency log shows a gap equal to the timeout when one path is pulled; dmesg timeout on the failed path followed by successful I/O. Compare iopolicy (numa, round-robin, queue-depth) behaviour.
In a trace
Requests on path A never complete; after the timeout the same LBAs appear on path B.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_decide_disposition() returns FAILOVER for REQ_NVME_MPATH with nvme_is_path_error(); multipath.c:nvme_failover_req() moves the bios to the head's requeue list and kicks nvme_requeue_work(); cancelled requests carry NVME_SC_HOST_ABORTED_CMD (a path error) so they fail over after a reset.
Test to provoke it
Two-port NVMe-oF setup; block one path with iptables -j DROP (not REJECT) and measure the I/O gap. SAFE in a lab.
T-27All paths gone: I/O queued, then failed (or held forever)Timer onlyLinux host / multipath
What happens
When no path is usable, the multipath head either fails I/O immediately or queues it. Queued I/O waits for a path to return, for delayed_removal_secs, or for every controller to be deleted (ctrl_loss_tmo).
How it shows up
Applications block in D state; after the hold time, I/O errors. sysfs io_fail_no_available_path_count increases.
Spec basis
Host policy.
Typical causes
Network outage longer than expected; all controllers in CONNECTING with failfast not expired; misconfigured ctrl_loss_tmo=-1 (reconnect forever).
How to detect / prove
nvme list-subsys shows all paths connecting or missing; hung-task warnings in dmesg; sysfs counters above.
In a trace
No traffic to the target; host reconnect attempts every reconnect_delay.
Linux v7.2 source
multipath.c:nvme_available_path() treats LIVE, RESETTING and CONNECTING controllers as available unless NVME_CTRL_FAILFAST_EXPIRED is set; otherwise nvme_mpath_queue_if_no_path() (driven by delayed_removal_secs) decides.
Test to provoke it
Disconnect all target ports and watch blocked tasks; vary fast_io_fail_tmo. SAFE in a lab.
T-28Fabrics controller lost: reconnect loop ends (ctrl_loss_tmo) or fails fastTimer onlyLinux host / NVMe-oF
What happens
After a transport error the host reconnects every reconnect_delay seconds. After ctrl_loss_tmo it gives up and deletes the controller. If fast_io_fail_tmo is set, queued I/O is failed after that time even while reconnects continue.
How it shows up
Reconnecting in 10 seconds... repeated; finally Removing controller (N).... With fast_io_fail_tmo: failfast expired and queued I/O fails with a host path error.
Spec basis
NVMe over Fabrics: association, Connect command. Timer values are host policy.
Typical causes
Target down longer than ctrl_loss_tmo (default 600 s); authentication failure (no retry); Connect rejected with DNR.
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -E "Reconnecting|Removing controller|failfast"; sysfs ctrl_loss_tmo, reconnect_delay, fast_io_fail_tmo for the controller.
In a trace
Repeated TCP SYN (or RDMA CM requests) every reconnect_delay; possibly ICReq/ICResp then a failing Connect.
Linux v7.2 source
fabrics.c:nvmf_parse_options(): max_reconnects = DIV_ROUND_UP(ctrl_loss_tmo, reconnect_delay), warns failfast tmo (N) larger than controller loss tmo (M); fabrics.c:nvmf_should_reconnect() (no reconnect on DNR or key rejection); tcp.c:nvme_tcp_reconnect_or_remove(); core.c:nvme_failfast_work(), nvme_fail_nonready_command(). Defaults in fabrics.h: NVMF_DEF_RECONNECT_DELAY 10, NVMF_DEF_CTRL_LOSS_TMO 600, NVMF_DEF_FAIL_FAST_TMO -1.
Test to provoke it
nvme connect -t tcp ... --ctrl-loss-tmo=30 --reconnect-delay=5 --fast_io_fail_tmo=10, then stop the target for 60 s. SAFE in a lab.
T-29NVMe/TCP or RDMA request timeout triggers error recoveryTimer onlyLinux host / NVMe-oF
What happens
On fabrics there is no Abort escalation: a request timeout in LIVE state starts transport error recovery, which tears down all queues and reconnects.
How it shows up
I/O tag N (CID) type T opcode 0x.. (Name) QID Q timeout (TCP) followed by reconnect messages; all in-flight I/O is retried or failed over.
Spec basis
NVMe over Fabrics / NVMe/TCP transport; host policy.
Typical causes
Target stall; lost PDU; network partition shorter than KATO but longer than io_timeout.
How to detect / prove
dmesg; packet capture showing a command capsule without a response capsule.
In a trace
Command capsule (CapsuleCmd PDU) sent; no CapsuleResp/C2HData; then TCP connection reset by host.
Linux v7.2 source
tcp.c:nvme_tcp_timeout() → nvme_tcp_error_recovery() → nvme_tcp_error_recovery_work(); when not LIVE, nvme_tcp_complete_timed_out(). RDMA: rdma.c:nvme_rdma_timeout(), nvme_rdma_error_recovery(). FC: fc.c:nvme_fc_timeout().
Test to provoke it
Drop only response packets for one queue with a targeted firewall rule. SAFE in a lab.
T-30TLS handshake does not complete in time (NVMe/TCP)Timer onlyLinux host / NVMe-oF
What happens
With TLS-secured NVMe/TCP, the in-kernel handshake upcall must finish within tls_handshake_timeout. If not, the connect attempt fails and the reconnect loop continues.
How it shows up
Connect failure with a timeout error; repeated "Reconnecting in N seconds".
Spec basis
NVMe/TCP transport TLS support; timeout is host policy.
Typical causes
tlshd user-space daemon not running or slow; key lookup failing; network latency.
How to detect / prove
dmesg and tlshd logs; /sys/module/nvme_tcp/parameters/tls_handshake_timeout.
In a trace
TCP connection established; TLS ClientHello; no completed handshake before the timeout; FIN.
Linux v7.2 source
tcp.c: tls_handshake_timeout (default 10 s) used as tmo = tls_handshake_timeout * HZ and args.ta_timeout_ms.
Test to provoke it
Stop tlshd and attempt a TLS connect. SAFE.
T-31Device needs a delay before CSTS.RDY is checked (quirk)Timer onlyLinux host / quirks
What happens
Some controllers misbehave if the host reads CSTS.RDY immediately after clearing CC.EN. Linux works around them with a fixed 2.3 s sleep. A new device with the same bug but without the quirk fails reset or initialisation.
How it shows up
Without the quirk: reset fails (Device not ready; aborting reset) or the controller hangs. With the quirk: every reset is 2.3 s slower.
Spec basis
Not a spec feature; a workaround for non-compliant behaviour.
Typical causes
Firmware that cannot handle register reads during reset.
How to detect / prove
Compare reset time with and without the quirk using the nvme.quirks=VID:DID:delay_before_chk_rdy module parameter (prefix the name with ^ to disable a built-in quirk), parsed in pci.c.
In a trace
MWr CC EN=0, then 2.3 s of silence, then CSTS polling.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme.h: NVME_QUIRK_DELAY_BEFORE_CHK_RDY, NVME_QUIRK_DELAY_AMOUNT 2300 ("found empirically" for PCI_DEVICE(0x1c58, 0x0003)); used in core.c:nvme_disable_ctrl(). pci.c ID table applies it to e.g. HGST and WDC SN200 adapters, Memblaze Pblaze4, Samsung PM1725/PM1725a, Seagate Nytro; core.c applies it by model string to "Samsung Portable SSD X5".
Test to provoke it
On a device that needs it, load nvme with quirks=VID:DID:^delay_before_chk_rdy and run nvme reset. CAUTION.
T-32Host stall mistaken for device timeoutDegradedLinux host / platform
What happens
The device completed on time, but the host could not process the completion: CPU soft lockup, interrupts disabled for seconds, VM paused or migrated, NUMA memory pressure. The NVMe timeout fires anyway.
How it shows up
NVMe timeout messages, often with "completion polled" (T-21), accompanied by soft-lockup or RCU-stall warnings, or a VM pause in the hypervisor log. The device's own logs show nothing.
Spec basis
None; host behaviour.
Typical causes
Long critical sections in other drivers; hypervisor steal time; firmware SMI storms (System Management Interrupts) on x86 platforms.
How to detect / prove
Correlate timeouts with watchdog: BUG: soft lockup, rcu: INFO: rcu_sched detected stalls, hypervisor steal time (top "st"), perf sched. An analyzer shows the CQE and MSI-X were sent on time.
In a trace
Normal device behaviour; the host's doorbell writes (CQ head) arrive late.
Linux v7.2 source
Same paths as T-21/T-22; the driver cannot tell host stalls from device stalls.
Test to provoke it
Pause a VM (virsh suspend) for longer than io_timeout during I/O and resume it. SAFE.

3.3.3 PCI core waits that NVMe depends on

Before the NVMe driver can read CAP, the PCI core must decide that the function is ready. Linux v7.2 implements these waits in drivers/pci/pci.c; their failure modes are cards in chapter 3.4:

  • pci_dev_wait() polls with exponential back-off (1, 2, 4, ... ms). Past PCI_RESET_WAIT (1000 ms) it logs not ready Nms after FLR; waiting, may try pcie_failed_link_retrain() on the upstream bridge, and gives up after the caller's timeout — PCIE_RESET_READY_POLL_MS = 60000 ms for FLR and D3hot→D0 — with not ready Nms after ...; giving up.
  • pcie_flr() waits for pending transactions, sets Initiate FLR, sleeps 100 ms (unless Immediate Readiness is set), then calls pci_dev_wait().
  • pci_bridge_wait_for_secondary_bus() handles waits after a bus reset or D3cold: 100 ms for ports at or below 5 GT/s; for faster ports it first waits for the Data Link Layer Link Active bit (pcie_wait_for_link_delay()) and then 100 ms.
  • pci_dev_d3_sleep() sleeps d3hot_delay (10 ms by default) after D3hot→D0.
PART IV

Silent Failures: Success That Isn't

The worst failures are the ones that never show up anywhere. The command is sent, the device accepts it, it posts a completion with status 0 (Success), and the analyzer, the kernel log and the SMART log all look clean. But the drive did not do the job: blocks that should be deallocated are still there, a flush did not reach the NAND, a write went to the wrong place, an erase left data behind. This part starts with a full worked chapter on the case you raised: "I wrote 10 GB, trimmed the last 5 GB, the drive said Success, and nothing changed." Then it lists every other kind of silent or semantic failure as an error card (D-01 to D-33), and ends with a chapter on how to catch them. The main idea of this part: a Success completion only means the controller accepted the command. To know the command did its job, you need a separate check that reads the result back.

IV.1 The deallocate that didn't: a full worked case

← 3.3 Host Timers in Linux v7.2: Timeouts, Retries, Resets and ReconnectsIV.2 Deallocate, Write Zeroes and allocation accounting: the error cards →
📌 The question this chapter answers

After writing 10 GiB to a namespace, the host deallocates (trims/discards) the last 5 GiB. The drive completes the command with Success, but it does not actually deallocate. The PCIe protocol analyzer trace looks normal, dmesg shows nothing, and nvme error-log is empty. How do you find out what really happened, and how do you prove it either way?

IV.1.1 Why this chapter exists

Deallocate is the clearest example of a command whose acceptance is visible but whose effect is not. Every step you can see on the wire happens normally: the Dataset Management (DSM) command, the fetch of its range list, the completion queue entry (CQE) and the MSI-X interrupt. The step that matters, the flash translation layer (FTL) dropping the logical-to-physical mapping entries, happens inside the controller and never crosses the PCIe link. So a trace can never prove that deallocation happened. It can only prove that it was requested correctly. Proof has to come from somewhere else: what a later read returns, an error that only appears when a feature is turned on, the namespace utilization counter, or a performance signature.

There is a second, less obvious point. In many cases the NVMe specification allows the drive to "not deallocate" without being wrong. Before calling a drive broken, you must work out which guarantee it actually made. The Identify Namespace fields DLFEAT and NSFEAT tell you that, and this chapter shows how to read them.

  • Trace a discard from fstrim/blkdiscard/file truncate through the Linux v7.2 block layer and nvme_setup_discard() down to the DSM command and its 16-byte range descriptors.
  • Know what Linux v7.2 sends in each case: DSM with the Attribute – Deallocate (AD) bit, or Write Zeroes with the Deallocate (DEAC) bit. Know the quirks that change this (NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES, NVME_QUIRK_DISABLE_WRITE_ZEROES).
  • Say exactly what the NVMe specification promises for deallocated blocks (DLFEAT read behaviour, DULBE, NUSE), and what it does not promise.
  • Explain why the analyzer trace, the kernel log and the error log all look normal.
  • Prove whether deallocation happened, using five independent kinds of evidence, and run the procedure as a CAUTION lab on a spare drive.
📭 What the spec does not say

The spec does not define the drive's internal mapping table, when garbage collection runs, or how fast deallocated space is reclaimed. Deallocation is described by its visible effects: what a read of a deallocated block returns (DLFEAT), whether a read can be made to fail (DULBE), and how the Namespace Utilization (NUSE) count behaves. If a drive gives none of these guarantees, "it didn't deallocate" may be impossible to see from outside, and may not even count as a failure.

IV.1.2 The story, the analogy, and where the analogy breaks

A validation engineer runs a simple script on a 1 TB drive formatted with 4096-byte logical blocks. The script writes a known pattern over LBAs 0 to 2,621,439 (10 GiB), then runs blkdiscard --offset 5GiB --length 5GiB /dev/nvme0n1. The command returns 0. The analyzer captured one DSM command and a Success completion. Then the engineer reads LBA 2,000,000 and gets the original pattern back, not zeroes. Is the drive broken? That depends on DLFEAT. If DLFEAT says reads of deallocated blocks return zeroes (read behaviour 001b), the drive has broken its promise: it either did not deallocate, or it deallocated and then returned stale data. If DLFEAT says 000b ("not reported"), old data is an allowed result and the read-back proves nothing.

🌍 Analogy

You tell a warehouse to "throw away everything on shelves 50 to 99". The clerk signs the form and hands it back. The signed form is the CQE. Whether the shelves are actually empty, you only find out by walking over and looking (a read), by asking for the inventory count (NUSE), or by noticing that the warehouse suddenly has room for new deliveries (better write performance).

🧩 Where the analogy breaks

In a real warehouse an empty shelf always looks empty. In an SSD, the drive may be allowed to show you the old content of a "thrown away" shelf (DLFEAT read behaviour 000b), so looking is not always proof. Also, the drive may legally throw things away later (in the background). The only thing it must get right immediately is what a read returns, and only when it has promised a value.

IV.1.3 The host path in Linux v7.2: who turns "free these blocks" into NVMe commands

There are three common ways the request starts. All of them end in the block layer as a REQ_OP_DISCARD (or REQ_OP_WRITE_ZEROES) request, which the NVMe driver turns into an NVMe command in nvme_setup_cmd().

Where it startsWhat happens before the NVMe driverEasy-to-miss trap
blkdiscard -o 5GiB -l 5GiB /dev/nvme0n1BLKDISCARD ioctl on the raw block device → blkdev_issue_discard() (in block/blk-lib.c; that file is not in the local source snapshot used for this atlas, so the name comes from general kernel knowledge) → discard bios.Fails with EOPNOTSUPP if the queue's discard_max_bytes is 0. This is the one case where you are told something.
fstrim -v /mntFITRIM ioctl → the filesystem walks its free-space map and issues discards for free extents only.The "N GiB trimmed" number is what the filesystem asked for, not what the drive did. Some filesystems report the whole free space every run.
File truncate / rm on a filesystem mounted with -o discardThe filesystem issues discards for freed extents, usually after the journal transaction commits (online discard).Without -o discard, a truncate sends nothing to the drive. The blocks are freed only in the filesystem's map until the next fstrim (often a weekly timer). File LBAs are also not "the last 5 GiB of the device".

Discard limits: how big pieces can be

drivers/nvme/host/core.c:nvme_update_disk_info() sets the queue limits from Identify data (v7.2, around lines 2123–2170):

  • Maximum discard size: if the controller reports DMRSL (Dataset Management Range Size Limit, from the I/O Command Set specific Identify Controller structure, read in nvme_init_non_mdts_limits()), then max_hw_discard_sectors is DMRSL converted to 512-byte sectors. Otherwise, if ONCS bit 2 (DSM supported) is set, it is UINT_MAX. If ONCS.DSM is clear, it is 0 and discard is disabled.
  • Maximum number of ranges: max_discard_segments = DMRL (Dataset Management Ranges Limit) if non-zero, else NVME_DSM_MAX_RANGES = 256.
  • Granularity: discard_granularity comes from NPDG/NPDGL and NPDA/NPDAL (preferred deallocate granularity and alignment) when the OPTPERF bits say they are valid, otherwise the logical block size. The block layer uses this to round discards, so a discard smaller than the granularity, or not aligned to it, may be trimmed or dropped on the host side before it is ever sent.

You can see all of this in sysfs: /sys/block/nvme0n1/queue/discard_max_bytes, discard_max_hw_bytes, discard_granularity, max_discard_segments, write_zeroes_max_bytes.

Building the DSM command: nvme_setup_discard()

drivers/nvme/host/core.c:nvme_setup_discard() (v7.2 line 840) does four things:

  1. It allocates a range buffer sized for all 256 ranges (sizeof(*range) * NVME_DSM_MAX_RANGES = 4096 bytes), even if only one range is used. The source comment says why: "Some devices do not consider the DSM 'Number of Ranges' field when determining how much data to DMA." This is a real device bug that the driver works around silently (see card D-32). If the allocation fails, it falls back to a per-controller discard_page.
  2. It fills one struct nvme_dsm_range per bio: cattr = 0 (context attributes), nlb = length in logical blocks, slba = starting LBA. Each descriptor is 16 bytes.
  3. It checks that the number of ranges built equals blk_rq_nr_discard_segments(). If not, it does WARN_ON_ONCE and fails the request with BLK_STS_IOERR. This is a host-side error you would see.
  4. It builds the 64-byte command: opcode nvme_cmd_dsm (09h), nr = segments − 1 (a 0's based count in CDW10), attributes = NVME_DSMGMT_AD (bit 2 of CDW11), and attaches the range buffer as a "special payload" (RQF_SPECIAL_PAYLOAD), so the PRP entries point at it.
/* Linux v7.2 drivers/nvme/host/core.c, nvme_setup_discard() (excerpt) */
	memset(cmnd, 0, sizeof(*cmnd));
	cmnd->dsm.opcode = nvme_cmd_dsm;
	cmnd->dsm.nsid = cpu_to_le32(ns->head->ns_id);
	cmnd->dsm.nr = cpu_to_le32(segments - 1);
	cmnd->dsm.attributes = cpu_to_le32(NVME_DSMGMT_AD);

	bvec_set_virt(&req->special_vec, range, alloc_size);
	req->rq_flags |= RQF_SPECIAL_PAYLOAD;

Note that Linux never sets IDR or IDW (Integral Dataset for Read/Write, bits 0 and 1). It sends only AD. Context attributes are always 0.

Write Zeroes and the DEAC bit: nvme_setup_write_zeroes()

REQ_OP_WRITE_ZEROES (from blkdev_issue_zeroout(), blkdiscard -z, fallocate zero-range, or filesystems) is handled by nvme_setup_write_zeroes() (v7.2 line 949):

  • If the controller has NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES, the function simply calls nvme_setup_discard(): a "write zeroes" request is sent as a DSM deallocate. The quirk's comment says the controller "deterministically returns 0's on reads to logical blocks that deallocate was called on". In drivers/nvme/host/pci.c v7.2 this quirk is set for Intel device IDs 0953h (750/P3500/P3600/P3700) and 0A53h (P3520). With this quirk and ONCS.DSM set, max_write_zeroes_sectors becomes UINT_MAX.
  • Otherwise it builds a real Write Zeroes command (opcode 08h) with SLBA and NLB. It sets NVME_WZ_DEAC (bit 9 of the control word, so CDW12 bit 25) only if the request does not have REQ_NOUNMAP and the namespace has NVME_NS_DEAC.
  • NVME_NS_DEAC is set in nvme_update_ns_info_block() (v7.2 around line 2483) only when (dlfeat & 0x7) == 0x1 (deallocated blocks read as zeroes) and dlfeat bit 3 is set (Write Zeroes supports the Deallocate bit). The comment explains: the DEAC bit itself does not require zeroes, "it must be a no-op if reads from deallocated data do not return zeroes".
  • If PI is enabled, it also sets PRACT so the controller generates protection information.

Write Zeroes can also be turned off: nvme_init_non_mdts_limits() sets max_zeroes_sectors to 0 if ONCS bit 3 (Write Zeroes) is clear or the controller has NVME_QUIRK_DISABLE_WRITE_ZEROES ("Broken Write Zeroes"). Many entries in the v7.2 nvme_id_table carry that quirk, including the Intel 600P/P3100 (F1A5h) and the QEMU emulated controller (Intel ID 5845h). When Write Zeroes is off, the block layer falls back to writing real zero-filled buffers, which is slower but cannot fail silently in the same way.

💡 Senior insight: the quirk table is a list of drives that lied

Each entry in nvme_id_table is a drive that once failed silently or behaved against the spec, and someone found out the hard way. NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES goes the other way: the drive guaranteed more than it said in Identify, so Linux trusts it more. When a new drive behaves strangely, read the quirk list first. If your drive's vendor already has entries, look at what they were for.

🔧 What Linux v7.2 does after the CQE

When the DSM completes with status 0, nvme_complete_rq() finishes the request with BLK_STS_OK and nvme_cleanup_cmd() frees the range buffer (or releases discard_page). Nothing checks the result. There is no read-back, no NUSE check, nothing. If the DSM fails, nvme_error_status() maps the status: Invalid Field / Invalid Opcode → BLK_STS_NOTSUPP, LBA Out of Range → BLK_STS_TARGET, others → BLK_STS_IOERR, and nvme_log_error() prints a rate-limited line such as nvme0n1: Dataset Management(0x9) @ LBA …, … blocks, … (sct 0x0 / sc 0x2). So you only get a log line when the drive refuses. A drive that accepts and then does nothing leaves no trace at all.

IV.1.4 What the specification actually promises

The table separates what can be checked in the local sources (Linux headers, libnvme types.h, the QEMU model) from what you should read in the NVM Command Set specification yourself. Field names are reliable. Exact normative wording is paraphrased, so verify in spec before quoting it in a bug report.

ItemWhereWhat it means for "did it deallocate?"Confidence
ONCS bit 2 (DSM) / bit 3 (Write Zeroes) / bit 11 (Write Zeroes Deallocate size limit, MAXWZD)Identify Controller, ONCSSays only that the commands are supported. Nothing about what deallocation does.verified (bit names in include/linux/nvme.h, libnvme enum nvme_id_ctrl_oncs)
DSM CDW11.AD (bit 2), CDW10.NR (0's based, up to 256 ranges), 16-byte range: Context Attributes, Length (NLB), Starting LBANVM Command Set, Dataset ManagementAD expresses the host's intent. It is generally understood to let the controller deallocate, without requiring it. Older base-spec text said the NVM subsystem "may" deallocate, and that a read of a deallocated LBA returns zeroes, ones, or the last data written. Newer specs tie read results to DLFEAT.Layout verified; "may" wording verify in spec
DLFEAT bits 2:0 read behaviour: 000b not reported, 001b reads return all 00h, 010b reads return all FFhIdentify Namespace, DLFEATThe key field. With 001b or 010b the drive promises a value, and a read-back can prove a failure. With 000b old data on read-back is allowed.verified (libnvme enum nvme_id_ns_dlfeat)
DLFEAT bit 3: Write Zeroes supports the Deallocate bit. Bit 4: Guard field of deallocated blocks with PI is the CRC of the returned data.Identify Namespace, DLFEATBit 3 together with RB = 001b is what makes Linux set DEAC. Bit 4 matters for PI namespaces: otherwise reading a deallocated block may raise a Guard error, or PI checks may be skipped for it verify in spec.verified (libnvme; Linux nvme_update_ns_info_block)
NSFEAT bit 2: DULBE supported. Feature 05h (Error Recovery) CDW11 bit 16: DULBE enable.Identify Namespace NSFEAT; Set Features FID 05hWhen supported and enabled, reads (and Compare/Verify) of deallocated or unwritten blocks fail with status 287h, "Deallocated or Unwritten Logical Block" (SCT 2h Media and Data Integrity). This turns deallocation into something you can observe as an error.Bits verified (libnvme NVME_FEAT_ERROR_RECOVERY_DULBE_SHIFT = 16; Linux NVME_SC_UNWRITTEN_BLOCK = 0x287); exact command coverage verify in spec
NSZE, NCAP, NUSE; NSFEAT bit 0 (THIN)Identify NamespaceNUSE = "current number of logical blocks allocated". If deallocation works and the drive tracks allocation, NUSE should go down by about the number of blocks deallocated (rounded to the internal granularity). Many drives report NUSE = NCAP all the time, and QEMU does exactly that (hw/nvme/ns.c: "no thin provisioning", nuse = ncap). In that case NUSE tells you nothing.Definition verified (libnvme comment); whether your drive tracks it is impl
NPDG/NPDA, NPDGL/NPDAL (preferred deallocate granularity / alignment)Identify Namespace / NVM CS Identify NamespaceDeallocating less than this, or misaligned, may have only partial effect inside the drive. It is a preference, not an error condition.verified (Linux comment cites NVM CS 1.1)
DMRL, DMRSL, WZDSLI/O Command Set specific Identify Controller (NVM)Limits on number of ranges, size of one range, and Write Zeroes size. What a controller does when a host exceeds them varies, see the QEMU note below.verified (Linux nvme_init_non_mdts_limits)
Get LBA Status (admin opcode 86h), OACS bit "GLSS"Admin commandMainly reports Potentially Unrecoverable LBAs (action types 10h/11h). libnvme also lists action type 02h "Return tracked allocated LBAs status", a newer addition. Do not assume Get LBA Status shows deallocation on a given drive. It does so only if action type 02h is supported, and its meaning should be verified in spec.Opcode verified; ATYPE 02h semantics version
Write ZeroesNVM Command SetUnlike DSM, Write Zeroes is a write: after it completes, reads must return zeroes (and PI as specified). With DEAC the controller may implement it by deallocating, but the read result is still mandatory. So Write Zeroes failing to zero is a clear spec violation, while DSM failing to deallocate often is not.derived from Linux DEAC comment + spec semantics; verify in spec
🏭 Implementation-dependent: the QEMU model skips ranges silently

The QEMU NVMe controller in the local snapshot (hw/nvme/ctrl.c:nvme_dsm_cb()) processes each range in turn. A range whose nlb is larger than n->dmrsl is skipped with only a trace event (pci_nvme_dsm_single_range_limit_exceeded, then goto next). A range that is out of bounds is also skipped (pci_nvme_err_invalid_lba_range, goto next). The command still completes with Success. QEMU also sets dlfeat = 0x9 (reads of deallocated blocks return zeroes, Write Zeroes supports DEAC) in nvme_ns_init(), and passes the discard to its block backend with blk_aio_pdiscard(). If that backend ignores discards (an option of the QEMU drive configuration, verify the default for your QEMU version), you get exactly the case in this chapter: DSM Success, DLFEAT promises zeroes, and a read-back returns old data. That makes QEMU a safe place to practise the proof procedure before using real hardware.

IV.1.5 Why the trace looks fine: what is and is not on the wire

Here is everything a PCIe protocol analyzer (or a PEX switch capture) sees for one deallocate. All of it is ordinary memory traffic. The only NVMe-specific content is in the payloads, which a good analyzer decodes.

Synthetic trace — DSM deallocate of LBA 1,310,720 + 1,310,720 blocks (4 KiB LBA format), I/O queue 3, CID 0x1047. The good drive and the lying drive produce the SAME trace.
#DirTLPDecoded contentWhat it proves
1H→DMWr 4 BSQ3 tail doorbell = 0x12Host submitted something
2D→HMRd 64 BFetch of SQ3 slot 0x11Device saw the doorbell
3H→DCplD 64 BSQE: OPC=09h (DSM), CID=0x1047, NSID=1, CDW10.NR=0 (1 range), CDW11=0000_0004h (AD), PRP1=0x0_7F3A_1000The command is a deallocate, not a hint
4D→HMRd 16 B (or up to 4096 B)Read of the range list at PRP1Device fetched the ranges. Length matters: 16 B for NR=0 is correct; 4096 B means it ignores NR (D-32)
5H→DCplDRange 0: CATTR=0, NLB=0x0014_0000, SLBA=0x0000_0000_0014_0000The exact LBAs requested. This is the only place they appear.
—(none)(none)FTL updates mapping (or doesn't)Invisible. Nothing on PCIe differs between "unmapped 1.3 M entries" and "did nothing".
6D→HMWr 16 BCQE: DW0=0, SQHD=0x12, SQID=3, CID=0x1047, P=1, Status=0 (Success)Command accepted and finished as far as the controller reports
7D→HMWr 4 BMSI-X vector 4Interrupt
8H→DMWr 4 BCQ3 head doorbellHost consumed the CQE

The trace answers three useful questions: (a) was the deallocate sent at all (this rules out the host-side traps in D-04), (b) did it carry the LBAs you expected (wrong LBAs point to host bugs such as partition offsets, dm-crypt, LVM or granularity rounding), and (c) was it accepted. It cannot answer (d): did the FTL change. Timing does not help either. A drive may complete a 5 GiB deallocate in a few microseconds because it only logs the request and does the unmapping later in the background, which is legal. A drive that does nothing completes just as fast.

🔍 Host-side trace without an analyzer

Linux tracepoints show the command but not the ranges: nvme_setup_cmd for a DSM prints nr=…, attributes=… via drivers/nvme/host/trace.c:nvme_trace_dsm(), and attributes=4 means AD. The LBAs live in the payload, so to see them use blktrace/blkparse (discard requests show RWBS containing D with sector and size), or a bpftrace probe on nvme_setup_discard.

IV.1.6 The discard path and where the evidence is: interactive figure

Click a step to follow one deallocate from the filesystem to the FTL, and then the probes you use afterwards to get evidence. Grey dashed arrows are inside the SSD and never appear on PCIe. Those are exactly the steps an analyzer cannot show you. synthetic

Software / host starts itDevice starts it (visible on PCIe)Inside the SSD (NOT visible on PCIe)PCIe link
  1. The filesystem or tool asks the block layer to discard

    blkdiscard (BLKDISCARD ioctl), fstrim (FITRIM) or a filesystem mounted with -o discard after a truncate creates discard bios. If the filesystem is not mounted with discard, a truncate stops here and nothing is ever sent.

    Where: kernel, block layer entryPCIe: No
    blkdiscard -o 5368709120 -l 5368709120 /dev/nvme0n1 → REQ_OP_DISCARD, sector 10485760, 10485760 sectors (512 B units)
  2. The block layer splits the discard; the driver builds the DSM command and range list

    The block layer cuts the request to discard_max_bytes, granularity and max_discard_segments. nvme_setup_discard() writes one 16-byte range per bio into a 4096-byte buffer and builds the 64-byte SQE with opcode 09h, NR = ranges − 1, and AD set.

    Where: host memory (SQ slot + range buffer)PCIe: No. The CPU writes its own RAM.
    SQE: OPC=09h CID=0x1047 NSID=1 CDW10=0 (NR=0 → 1 range) CDW11=4 (AD) Range[0]: CATTR=0 NLB=0x140000 SLBA=0x140000 (Large requests may become several ranges or several DSM commands: check with blktrace.)
  3. The driver rings the SQ tail doorbell

    A 4-byte posted write to the SQ3 tail doorbell. Only the new tail index crosses the link.

    Where: BAR0 + 1000h + (2·3)·(4<<DSTRD)PCIe: Yes, host → device · MWr 4 B
    With DSTRD=0: offset 0x1018, value 0x12
  4. The controller fetches the SQE

    A memory read of the 64-byte slot. An analyzer that decodes NVMe shows "Dataset Management, AD".

    Where: host memory, SQ3 slot 0x11PCIe: Yes · MRd 64 B + CplD
    First evidence point: the command IS a deallocate (CDW11 bit 2 = 1).
  5. The controller fetches the range list

    The controller reads the range descriptors from PRP1. This is the only place on the wire where the LBAs appear. Check that the read length matches 16 × (NR+1). A 4096-byte read when NR=0 shows the device ignores NR, the bug Linux allocates around.

    Where: host memory, range bufferPCIe: Yes · MRd 16 B (expected) + CplD
    Second evidence point: SLBA 0x140000, NLB 0x140000 → exactly LBAs 1,310,720 … 2,621,439
  6. Internal: the FTL unmaps the LBAs (or doesn't)

    A correct drive marks the mapping entries invalid (often lazily, in a journal or bitmap). A lying drive does nothing, or only part of it. The old NAND pages stay physically present until garbage collection either way. This is the step in question, and it is invisible.

    Where: controller SRAM/DRAM, FTL metadataPCIe: No. Nothing differs on the link.
    Good: 1,310,720 map entries → UNMAPPED (NUSE −1,310,720 if tracked) Bad: 0 map entries changed; same CQE, same timing
  7. The controller posts a Success CQE

    A 16-byte completion with status 0. This means the command was accepted and processed without an error the controller chose to report. It does not mean the blocks are now deallocated.

    Where: host memory, CQ3PCIe: Yes, device → host · MWr 16 B
    DW2: SQHD=0x12 SQID=3 DW3: CID=0x1047 P=1 Status=0x0000
  8. MSI-X interrupt; Linux completes the request

    The handler calls nvme_handle_cqe(), the request ends with BLK_STS_OK, the range buffer is freed, blkdiscard exits 0. No log line is printed.

    Where: CPU, nvme_irq → nvme_handle_cqePCIe: Yes · MWr 4 B to MSI-X address
    dmesg: (nothing) nvme error-log: (no new entry)
  9. Evidence queries: Identify Namespace, log pages, and Reads

    The host sends normal admin and I/O commands to measure the result: Identify Namespace (NUSE, DLFEAT, NSFEAT), Get Features 05h (DULBE), SMART log, Get LBA Status if supported, and Reads of sample LBAs from inside, at the edges of, and outside the range.

    Where: admin and I/O queuesPCIe: Yes · normal command traffic
    nvme id-ns /dev/nvme0n1 -H | grep -iE 'nuse|dlfeat|nsfeat' nvme read /dev/nvme0n1 -s 2000000 -c 0 -z 4096 -d out.bin
  10. Read-back data or a DULBE error comes back: this is the evidence

    The NAND→controller step is internal. The data (or error status) the controller returns is on the wire and in your buffer. Compare it with the DLFEAT promise: zeroes, FFh, an error 287h (DULBE enabled), or the old pattern.

    Where: host memory, read buffer / CQE statusPCIe: Yes · MWr data + CQE
    DLFEAT RB=001b and data = old pattern → PROVEN failure DLFEAT RB=000b and data = old pattern → allowed; use DULBE / NUSE instead

IV.1.7 How to prove it: the evidence ladder

Use the strongest evidence the drive supports. Each rung says what it proves, what it cannot prove, and when it applies. Rung 0 always comes first. Most reported cases of "the drive ignored my trim" turn out to be a discard that never left the host.

RungMethodProvesCannot prove / caveatsNeeds
0blktrace / tracepoint / analyzer: DSM with AD sent, ranges as expected, Success CQEThe host asked correctly and the device accepted itAnything about the FTLAlways possible
1Read-back of sample LBAs, compared with the DLFEAT read behaviourWith RB = 001b/010b: any old data = violation proven. All zeroes/FFh = consistent with deallocation (or with a Write Zeroes-like implementation).With RB = 000b old data is legal. Data matching the promised pattern does not prove the space was reclaimed internally.DLFEAT RB ≠ 000b
2DULBE: enable Feature 05h bit 16, then read sample LBAs with passthroughStatus 287h (Deallocated or Unwritten Logical Block) for blocks in the range = the controller's own metadata says they are deallocated. Success with old data = not deallocated.Only if NSFEAT bit 2 = 1. Affects every reader of the namespace (Linux returns BLK_STS_MEDIUM, so filesystems will see I/O errors).NSFEAT.DULBE
3NUSE before/after (Identify Namespace)A drop of about NLB (± granularity) = allocation accounting changedMany drives report NUSE = NCAP always. NUSE may update lazily. It counts the whole namespace, so other writers skew it.Drive that tracks NUSE (test: does NUSE drop after a Format? after a known-good DSM?)
4Get LBA Status with action type 02h (tracked allocated), where supportedPer-range allocation view from the controllerNewer feature. Action types 10h/11h report potentially unrecoverable LBAs, not deallocation. versionOACS.GLSS + ATYPE 02h support
5Performance / write amplification signatureAfter real deallocation of half the drive, sustained random writes over the other half run faster and with lower write amplification than a control run without deallocateIndirect and statistical. Needs a preconditioned drive and a control. Some drives reclaim slowly.Hours of drive time; vendor NAND-write counter helps
6SMART / vendor logs: host vs NAND writesData Units Written should not change from DSM. A vendor "NAND bytes written" counter lets you compute write amplification for rung 5.SMART has no "deallocated" counter. Vendor log formats are vendor- or OCP-specific impl.Vendor/OCP log support
7Vendor debug (FTL dump, internal trace)Direct view of the mapUnder NDA, firmware-specificVendor engagement
🐞 Decision procedure
  1. Did it leave the host? If there is no DSM/Write Zeroes in blktrace or the analyzer, the bug is host-side (D-04). Stop and fix the mount option, dm layer or discard_max_bytes.
  2. Were the ranges right? Decode the range list. If the LBAs are wrong, it is a partition/LVM/offset bug, not a drive bug.
  3. Was it rejected? A non-zero status shows in dmesg via nvme_log_error() and in nvme error-log. Not silent.
  4. What did the drive promise? nvme id-ns -H: DLFEAT RB, NSFEAT DULBE/THIN, NPDG/NPDA. nvme id-ctrl -H: ONCS, OACS.GLSS. NVM Identify Controller (nvme nvm-id-ctrl, if your nvme-cli has it): DMRL, DMRSL.
  5. Pick the strongest rung (1 > 2 > 3 > 4 > 5) the promises allow, and repeat with a known-good reference drive or the QEMU model as a control.
  6. Classify: promise broken (RB=001b, old data back) → D-01/D-03, a spec violation. Partial (edges or some ranges only) → D-02. No promise, accounting unchanged → "deallocate not effective", a quality or performance issue for the vendor, not a conformance failure.

IV.1.8 Worked example with numbers synthetic

Drive: 1.0 TB, one namespace, LBA format 4096 + 0 (lba_shift 12). Identify Namespace: NSZE = NCAP = 244,190,646. NSFEAT = 05h (THIN + DULBE). DLFEAT = 09h (RB 001b + Write Zeroes DEAC). NPDG = NPDA = 7 (0's based → 8 blocks = 32 KiB). NVM Identify Controller: DMRL = 0, DMRSL = 0.

LBAs written = 10 GiB / 4096 = 2,621,440 (LBA 0 … 2,621,439)
Range to deallocate = LBA 1,310,720 (0x140000), NLB = 1,310,720 (0x140000)
Granularity check: 1,310,720 mod 8 = 0 and 1,310,720 mod 8 = 0 → aligned
  • NUSE expected — before: ≥ 2,621,440 (plus any earlier data); after: before − 1,310,720, within one granule (8 blocks) per range edge.
  • DLFEAT 09h — reads of deallocated blocks must return 00h, and Linux will set DEAC on Write Zeroes for this namespace.

Observations on the "lying" drive: DSM completes in 11 µs with status 0. nvme id-ns NUSE before = 2,621,440, after = 2,621,440 (unchanged, even after 60 s idle). Reads of LBA 1,310,720, 2,000,000 and 2,621,439 return the original pattern. With DULBE enabled, reading LBA 2,000,000 returns Success, not 287h. Reads of LBA 2,621,440 (never written) do return 287h, which proves DULBE works and the drive tracks unwritten blocks. Conclusion: the drive promised zeroes (RB 001b) and returned old data. Its own allocation tracking (DULBE, NUSE) shows the range is still mapped. The deallocate was accepted and not performed. This is a conformance failure, card D-01, and the evidence package is: the trace (rungs 0), Identify before/after, read-back dumps, and the DULBE control read.

🧪 Lab IV.1 — Prove or disprove a deallocate on a spare drive CAUTION LAB
⚠️ Destroys data

Every write, discard and Set Features below acts on the whole namespace. Use a spare drive or a QEMU NVMe device with nothing mounted, and double-check the device name (nvme list, lsblk). Enabling DULBE makes reads of unwritten blocks fail for all users of the namespace. Turn it off again at the end. Never run this on a disk holding a filesystem you care about.

Objective. Write 10 GiB of verifiable data, deallocate the last 5 GiB, and collect at least three independent kinds of evidence about whether deallocation took effect.

Setup. Linux with nvme-cli, fio, util-linux (blkdiscard), optionally blktrace. Note the namespace's LBA size (nvme id-ns -H, "in use" LBA format). The example assumes 4096-byte LBAs; recompute LBA numbers for 512.

# 0. What does the drive promise?  (read-only)
DEV=/dev/nvme0n1; CTRL=/dev/nvme0
nvme id-ctrl $CTRL -H | grep -iE 'oncs|oacs|vwc'
nvme id-ns   $DEV  -H | grep -iE 'nsze|ncap|nuse|nsfeat|dlfeat|npdg|npda|in use'
cat /sys/block/nvme0n1/queue/{discard_max_bytes,discard_granularity,max_discard_segments,write_zeroes_max_bytes}
nvme get-feature $CTRL -f 0x05 -n 1 -H          # Error Recovery (TLER, DULBE)

# 1. Write 10 GiB of self-verifying data (header with offset + checksum per block)
fio --name=fill --filename=$DEV --rw=write --bs=128k --size=10G --direct=1 \
    --verify=crc32c --do_verify=0 --ioengine=libaio --iodepth=16
nvme id-ns $DEV | grep -i nuse            # record NUSE_1

# 2. Optional: start blktrace in another shell to capture the discard
#    blktrace -d $DEV -o - | blkparse -i - | grep ' D '

# 3. Deallocate the last 5 GiB (LBA 1310720, 1310720 blocks)
blkdiscard --offset 5GiB --length 5GiB $DEV ; echo "exit=$?"
sleep 30
nvme id-ns $DEV | grep -i nuse            # record NUSE_2

# 4. Read-back: inside, both edges, and just outside the range
for L in 1310719 1310720 2000000 2621439; do
  nvme read $DEV -s $L -c 0 -z 4096 -d /tmp/lba_$L.bin
  echo "LBA $L:"; od -A d -t x1 /tmp/lba_$L.bin | head -2
done

# 5. DULBE (only if NSFEAT bit 2 = 1): enable, read, disable
nvme set-feature $CTRL -f 0x05 -n 1 -v 0x10000
nvme read $DEV -s 2000000 -c 0 -z 4096 -d /dev/null   # expect status 0x287 if deallocated
nvme read $DEV -s 2621440 -c 0 -z 4096 -d /dev/null   # never written: control, expect 0x287
nvme set-feature $CTRL -f 0x05 -n 1 -v 0x0

# 6. fio verify of the first half must still pass (deallocate must not touch it)
fio --name=check --filename=$DEV --rw=read --bs=128k --size=5G --direct=1 \
    --verify=crc32c --verify_only --ioengine=libaio

Observations to record: DLFEAT RB, NSFEAT bits, NUSE_1 and NUSE_2, the first bytes of each read-back, DULBE statuses, the fio verify result for the first half, and (if captured) the discard line from blkparse with its sector and size.

Questions. (1) If LBA 1,310,719 (just outside) reads back as zeroes, what went wrong? (2) If NUSE does not change but read-backs are zero, is the drive failing? (3) Why is the never-written LBA a necessary control for step 5?

Solution

(1) The deallocate covered too much. Either the host rounded the range out (granularity or offset error, check blkparse) or the drive deallocated outside the range. Both are serious, see D-02. (2) Not necessarily. NUSE may be reported as NCAP on drives that do not track allocation, or update lazily. Zero read-back with RB=001b means the promise was kept. (3) It proves DULBE is really enabled and implemented. Without it, "Success" on LBA 2,000,000 could mean "DULBE is ignored" rather than "block still allocated".

Extension. Repeat with blkdiscard -z (Write Zeroes; with DLFEAT 09h Linux sets DEAC) and compare. Repeat in QEMU with a backend that ignores discards to reproduce a "lying" controller, and with one that honours them as a control.

IV.1.9 Common mistakes

  • Treating the Success CQE as proof. It shows acceptance. For DSM it is not even a promise of action.
  • Calling old data on read-back a failure when DLFEAT RB = 000b. That is allowed. Use DULBE or NUSE instead.
  • Testing through a filesystem. File extents are not device LBAs, discard may be deferred or not sent at all, and the filesystem may never read freed blocks again. Test on the raw namespace.
  • Ignoring granularity. Ranges not aligned to NPDA/NPDG may be partly deallocated by design. Test aligned and misaligned cases separately.
  • Measuring right after the CQE only. Background unmapping is legal for the internal space accounting. Check NUSE again after idle time. Read results must be correct immediately.
  • Leaving DULBE enabled. A filesystem reading never-written metadata areas will see I/O errors.

Quiz

  1. Which Identify Namespace field decides whether a read-back of old data after a deallocate is a spec violation?

    Answer

    DLFEAT bits 2:0 (read behaviour). 001b promises zeroes, 010b promises FFh, 000b promises nothing.

  2. Under what exact condition does Linux v7.2 set the DEAC bit in Write Zeroes?

    Answer

    When (dlfeat & 7) == 1 and dlfeat bit 3 is set (so NVME_NS_DEAC is set), and the request does not carry REQ_NOUNMAP. With NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES it sends a DSM instead of Write Zeroes at all.

  3. Where in a PCIe trace do the LBAs of a deallocate appear?

    Answer

    Only in the CplD that returns the range list to the device's MRd at PRP1. They are not in the SQE.

  4. Why does nvme_setup_discard() always allocate 4096 bytes for ranges?

    Answer

    Some devices ignore the NR field and DMA-read the maximum of 256 ranges. A small buffer would make them read past its end (card D-32).

  5. Which status code does a DULBE-enabled read of a deallocated LBA return, and what does Linux turn it into?

    Answer

    287h "Deallocated or Unwritten Logical Block" (SCT 2h). nvme_error_status() maps it to BLK_STS_MEDIUM.

Interview questions

  1. Beginner Why can't a protocol analyzer show whether a trim worked?
    Model answerThe analyzer sees the PCIe link. The DSM command, its range list and the completion are on the link, but the FTL change is inside the controller. A drive that unmaps and one that does nothing produce identical traffic.
  2. Intermediate A customer says "fstrim says 400 GiB trimmed but performance never recovers." Walk through your investigation.
    Model answerFirst confirm discards reach the device (blktrace for D requests, tracepoint nvme_setup_cmd with attributes=4, dm layers' discard passthrough, discard_max_bytes). Then check promises (DLFEAT, NSFEAT, NPDG), then do a raw-device controlled test: write, deallocate, read-back per DLFEAT, DULBE probe, NUSE delta. Finally a performance A/B with a control. fstrim's number is the request size, not the effect.
  3. Senior When is "deallocate not performed" a conformance bug and when is it a quality issue?
    Model answerConformance: when an observable promise is broken: DLFEAT RB 001b/010b with old data returned, DULBE enabled and supported but a deallocated block reads Success, Write Zeroes (with or without DEAC) not returning zeroes. Quality: with RB 000b and no DULBE/NUSE tracking, nothing observable is promised, so a no-op deallocate only costs space and performance. That is a vendor-qualification problem, and you prove it with performance and write-amplification A/B tests.
✅ Mastery gate
  • I can name the Linux v7.2 functions from discard request to SQE, and the limits that apply.
  • I can read DLFEAT/NSFEAT and state what a read-back can prove for this drive.
  • I can explain to a manager why a clean trace is not evidence.
  • I have run Lab IV.1 on QEMU or a spare drive and recorded three independent kinds of evidence.

Summary. A deallocate is visible on the wire only as a request and an acceptance. Whether it took effect is visible only through promised read behaviour (DLFEAT), errors you can turn on (DULBE), allocation accounting (NUSE, Get LBA Status 02h), or long-term performance. Rule out host-side drops first, then use the strongest evidence the drive supports. The next chapter turns each variant of this problem into an error card.

IV.2 Deallocate, Write Zeroes and allocation accounting: the error cards

← IV.1 The deallocate that didn't: a full worked caseIV.3 Durability and data-integrity failures: acknowledged but not true →

This chapter turns the worked case into cards you can look up. Each card says what the failure is, what is (and is not) reported, the spec basis, how to prove it, and how to provoke it. "Silent" means the command completes with Success and nothing appears in dmesg, nvme error-log or the SMART log. The cards share one rule: check the drive's promises first (DLFEAT, NSFEAT, ONCS, granularity), because they decide whether the observed behaviour is a spec violation or only a quality problem.

OperationLinux v7.2 sendsWhat must be true afterwardsCan a silent failure be proven by read-back?
blkdiscard, fstrim, online discardDSM 09h, CDW11.AD=1 (nvme_setup_discard)Only what DLFEAT/DULBE promiseYes if DLFEAT RB ≠ 000b or DULBE supported, otherwise only indirectly
blkdiscard -z, BLKZEROOUT, zero-rangeWrite Zeroes 08h (+DEAC if NVME_NS_DEAC), or DSM if NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES, or plain zero-filled Writes if Write Zeroes is disabledReads return zeroes (Write Zeroes is a write)Yes, always
Sanitize / Format (Part IV.4)Admin 84h / 80h via passthroughPer action (block erase, crypto erase, overwrite)Partly: read-back proves the logical view only, not the physical media
D-01Deallocate accepted but not performed SilentNVMe command
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The DSM SQE (OPC 09h, CDW11=4), the range-list fetch with SLBA/NLB, the Success CQE and MSI-X are all visible and look perfect. The failure itself is not. Partial clue: if the capture also holds the Identify Namespace data (DLFEAT RB=001b/010b) and a later Read of the range, a non-zero data payload in that Read proves it. Detail: Identical to a good deallocate: doorbell MWr, MRd of the 64-byte SQE (OPC 09h, CDW11 = 4), MRd of 16×(NR+1) bytes of ranges, MWr of a CQE with status 0, MSI-X. The DSM exchange itself never shows the failure; only the absence of a later effect does.
What happens
The host sends DSM with AD=1 for a range. The controller fetches the range list, completes with status 0, and leaves every LBA in the range mapped. Old data, space accounting and garbage-collection load stay as if nothing happened.
How it shows up
Nothing is reported: CQE status 0, no dmesg line, no Error Information log entry, no AER. blkdiscard/fstrim exit 0. Over time sustained write performance and endurance are worse than expected, because the drive keeps moving data the host no longer needs.
Spec basis
NVM Command Set: Dataset Management, Attribute – Deallocate. Identify Namespace DLFEAT (read behaviour bits 2:0), NSFEAT bit 2 (DULBE), NUSE; Feature 05h DULBE bit. Whether doing nothing is a violation depends on these promises. AD is widely described as advisory verify in spec.
Typical causes
Firmware treats AD as a hint and drops it under load or when the queue of unmap work is full. Ranges smaller than the internal mapping unit are ignored. Firmware feature disabled in a SKU. A bug where a multi-range DSM processes only range 0.
How to detect / prove

1) Confirm the DSM and its ranges on the wire or with blktrace (rule out D-04). 2) If DLFEAT RB = 001b/010b: read back sample LBAs; old data = proven. 3) If NSFEAT.DULBE: enable Feature 05h bit 16 and read; Success instead of status 287h = not deallocated (control: a never-written LBA must give 287h). 4) NUSE before/after (if the drive tracks it). 5) Performance A/B against a run without deallocate.

Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/core.c:nvme_setup_discard() builds the command; nvme_update_disk_info() sets discard limits; nvme_cleanup_cmd() frees the range buffer. No code path verifies the effect.
Test to provoke it
Lab IV.1 CAUTION: write a pattern, deallocate an aligned range, read back per DLFEAT, DULBE probe, NUSE delta. Vary the range size (1 granule, 1 GiB, whole namespace), number of ranges (1, 2, 256), and queue depth of concurrent writes. In QEMU, a backend that ignores discards reproduces it on purpose.
D-02Deallocate partially performed: ranges or edges silently skipped SilentNVMe command
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The range descriptors and the length of the range-list MRd are visible, so a device that fetched fewer than 16×(NR+1) bytes is caught. Which ranges took effect is not visible, except through later Read payloads at range edges. Detail: Decode every range descriptor in the range-list CplD. Confirm that NR+1 descriptors were fetched (the MRd length). If the device fetched fewer bytes than 16×(NR+1), it cannot have processed the later ranges. That is the one case where the trace does show a partial deallocate.
What happens
Only part of the request takes effect: the first range of a multi-range DSM, only fully-aligned granules (the unaligned head and tail are kept), or every range except the ones larger than an internal limit. The CQE is still Success.
How it shows up
Silent. Read-back gives zeroes in the middle of the range and old data near the edges, or in whole sub-ranges. NUSE drops by less than NLB.
Spec basis
NPDG/NPDA and NPDGL/NPDAL are preferred granularity and alignment. Unaligned deallocates are allowed, and how much is deallocated is implementation-dependent unless DLFEAT promises a read value for each deallocated LBA. DMRL/DMRSL are limits the host should honour. What a controller must do when they are exceeded: verify in spec.
Typical causes
Internal mapping unit larger than the LBA size (for example 4 KiB map on 512-byte LBAs). Range-count or range-size limits handled with a silent "skip". The QEMU model does exactly this: hw/nvme/ctrl.c:nvme_dsm_cb() skips a range with nlb > dmrsl or out-of-bounds LBAs, emits only a trace event, and completes Success.
How to detect / prove
Read back the first and last LBA of every range, plus one LBA either side of each. With DLFEAT RB = 001b, any non-zero LBA inside a range is a violation, whatever the alignment. Check NUSE delta against Σ NLB. For multi-range, use distinct patterns per range so you can tell which range was skipped.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_update_disk_info() (discard_granularity from NPDG/NPDA/NPDGL/NPDAL; max_discard_segments from DMRL or 256; max_hw_discard_sectors from DMRSL); core.c:nvme_init_non_mdts_limits() reads DMRL/DMRSL.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION With nvme dsm passthrough (bypasses the block layer's rounding): issue ranges that start or end off the NPDA boundary, 256-range DSMs, and a range just above DMRSL. Check every edge LBA. Run on QEMU first to see the skip behaviour in its trace events.
D-03Read after deallocate returns old data (DLFEAT promise broken) SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Visible If the capture includes the Identify Namespace payload (DLFEAT), the DSM, and the later Read, the Read data payload carrying the old pattern instead of the promised 00h/FFh is direct proof on the wire. Detail: The later Read's data MWr TLPs carry the old pattern. This is one of the few silent failures where the proof itself is visible on the wire, in the read data payload.
What happens
The namespace reports DLFEAT read behaviour 001b (zeroes) or 010b (FFh). After a successful deallocate, a Read returns the previous contents of the block. Either the FTL did not unmap it, or it unmapped and a cache (read buffer, write cache, read-ahead) still returns the old page.
How it shows up
Read completes Success with stale data. With PI enabled, the stale PI may still be valid for the old data, so even PI checks pass.
Spec basis
Identify Namespace DLFEAT bits 2:0. With 001b/010b the read result is defined. Returning old data is a conformance failure. Linux relies on this promise: nvme_update_ns_info_block() enables DEAC only when RB = 001b and DLFEAT bit 3 is set.
Typical causes
Deallocate not performed (D-01). Unmap applied to the map but not invalidated in an internal read cache. A race between a write still in the volatile cache and the deallocate (ordering bug). Unmap lost across a power cycle (the unmap journal was not persisted, so after power-on the old mapping comes back). The last case is the nastiest: data deleted for security reasons reappears.
How to detect / prove
Read-back right after the CQE, again after idle, and again after a clean power cycle (nvme subsystem-reset or a real power cycle). Any non-promised value at any point is proof. Collect: Identify Namespace (DLFEAT), trace of the DSM, and a hex dump of the read.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_update_ns_info_block() (DEAC condition), core.c:nvme_setup_write_zeroes(); quirk NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES in nvme.h and pci.c:nvme_id_table.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION Write pattern → deallocate → read immediately → idle 10 min → read → power cycle → read. Also overlap: issue a Write and a DSM to the same LBAs back to back at high queue depth, then read. The result must be either the new data or the promised value, consistent with the completion order.
D-04Discard never reaches the device (host-side silent drop) SilentHost / block layer
In a PEX trace
Visible Visible as an absence: no SQE with OPC 09h (or 08h) in a capture window long enough to cover the journal commit or fstrim. That proves the drive was never asked. Detail: Nothing: no OPC 09h SQE in the capture window. Note that discards may come long after the truncate (journal commit, periodic fstrim), so capture long enough.
What happens
The user believes the space was trimmed, but no DSM was ever sent. Common reasons: filesystem not mounted with -o discard and no fstrim run; dm-crypt without discard passthrough; LVM/MD layers or a virtual disk that do not forward discards; discard_max_bytes = 0 because ONCS.DSM is clear or the queue limit was changed; the discard was smaller than discard_granularity and rounded away.
How it shows up
Mostly silent. blkdiscard on a device without discard support does fail (EOPNOTSUPP), but filesystem truncate and many stacked layers just drop the request. The drive is blamed for keeping data it was never told about.
Spec basis
Not a device issue. Identify Controller ONCS bit 2 decides whether Linux enables discard at all.
Typical causes
Mount options, dm/LVM configuration, virtualization layers, misread fstrim output (its byte count is what it asked for), discards rounded away by granularity.
How to detect / prove
lsblk --discard on every layer of the stack (non-zero DISC-GRAN/DISC-MAX needed on each). blktrace -d /dev/nvme0n1 while truncating or trimming: look for discard requests (RWBS with D). Enable the nvme:nvme_setup_cmd tracepoint and look for DSM with attributes=4. Analyzer: no SQE with OPC 09h means nothing was sent.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_update_disk_info(): max_hw_discard_sectors = 0 when ONCS.DSM is clear and DMRSL is 0. core.c:nvme_setup_cmd() is the single point where REQ_OP_DISCARD becomes a DSM, so a probe there catches every discard that leaves the block layer.
Test to provoke it
SAFE on a scratch filesystem: mount without discard, write and delete a file, trace for 2 minutes and observe no DSM. Remount with discard and repeat. Repeat on dm-crypt with and without discard passthrough.
D-05Write Zeroes completes but blocks are not zero SilentNVMe command
In a PEX trace
Visible The Write Zeroes SQE (OPC 08h, DEAC in CDW12 bit 25) and Success CQE, followed by a Read whose data payload is non-zero, are both on the wire, which proves the failure. Detail: SQE OPC 08h with SLBA/NLB in CDW10-12 and DEAC in CDW12 bit 25. No data transfer for the command itself. The later Read data TLPs show the non-zero content.
What happens
Write Zeroes (08h), with or without DEAC, returns Success, but later reads return old data, garbage, or zeroes for only part of the range. Filesystems and databases that rely on zero-out for allocation (for example, unwritten extents converted to written, or BLKZEROOUT used to clear metadata) then see stale structures.
How it shows up
Silent at the NVMe level. Upper layers may later report corruption (filesystem check errors, database page checksum failures) far away from the cause.
Spec basis
NVM Command Set Write Zeroes: after completion the LBAs read as zeroes (and PI per PRACT/PRINFO). DEAC allows the controller to deallocate instead, but the zero read result is still required. Linux's comment in nvme_update_ns_info_block() says DEAC "must be a no-op if reads from deallocated data do not return zeroes".
Typical causes
Firmware implementing DEAC as "deallocate" on a drive whose deallocated read value is not zero. Size-limit handling (WZSL, or WZDSL when ONCS bit 11 applies) silently truncating long commands. Bugs the Linux quirk NVME_QUIRK_DISABLE_WRITE_ZEROES ("Broken Write Zeroes") exists for: in v7.2 it is set for many device IDs in pci.c:nvme_id_table, including the Intel 600P/P3100 and the QEMU 5845h controller.
How to detect / prove
Write a non-zero pattern, Write Zeroes the range (both DEAC=0 and DEAC=1 via nvme write-zeroes … --deac), read every block back, compare with zeroes. Test sizes around WZSL/WZDSL and MDTS. Any non-zero byte is proof, independent of DLFEAT.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_setup_write_zeroes(), core.c:nvme_init_non_mdts_limits() (WZSL, NVME_QUIRK_DISABLE_WRITE_ZEROES), core.c:nvme_update_disk_info() (max_write_zeroes_sectors, NVME_QUIRK_DEALLOCATE_ZEROES).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION On a spare namespace: pattern-fill 1 GiB; nvme write-zeroes in sizes 1 block, WZSL−1, WZSL, WZSL+1 (expect an error, not silent truncation), with and without --deac. Read back and compare. Also run blkdiscard -z and confirm with blktrace which command Linux actually used.
D-06Deallocate reaches too far: live data outside the range lost SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The range descriptors in the range-list CplD show whether the host asked for the right LBAs. Lost guard blocks show up only if the capture also holds the guard writes and a later read of the guards. Detail: Range descriptor values in the CplD. If they are right and guards are still lost, the device is at fault. If they are already wrong, the host is.
What happens
Blocks outside the requested range read back as zeroes/FFh or fail with 287h. The drive rounded the range out to its internal unit, mis-parsed the descriptor (for example, read NLB as 0's based, which adds one block), or the host sent the wrong range.
How it shows up
Silent until the data is read. Filesystems see corrupted files or metadata long after the trim. Host-side cases have happened in practice: a 2019 dm-devel thread reports fstrim discarding too many or the wrong blocks on Linux 5.1 with dm-crypt/LVM, which led to data loss.
Spec basis
Range descriptor: Length in logical blocks (a 1's based count, unlike CDW12 NLB in Read/Write, which is 0's based). Deallocating LBAs not listed in any range is never permitted.
Typical causes
Firmware treating Length as 0's based; rounding unaligned ranges outward instead of inward; host stacking layers translating offsets wrongly; a DSM processed against the wrong namespace (NSID bug).
How to detect / prove
Guard pattern: write distinct "guard" blocks right before and after each range, deallocate, and verify the guards with a checksumming read (fio --verify=crc32c over the guard areas). Decode the range list in the trace to tell host error from device error.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_setup_discard(): nlb = blk_rq_sectors(req) >> (lba_shift - 9) (single segment) or bi_size >> lba_shift per bio, written as a 1's based count.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION For N in {1, 7, 8, 9, 4095, 4096, 4097} blocks: guard–range–guard layout, deallocate the range via nvme dsm, verify guards. Repeat with 2- and 256-range DSMs and with ranges in a second namespace to catch NSID mix-ups.
D-07Allocation accounting wrong: NUSE frozen, DULBE not reported SilentIdentify / features
In a PEX trace
Visible The Identify Namespace data payload (NUSE at bytes 16–23) before and after, and the CQE status of a DULBE-enabled Read (287h expected, 0 observed) are on the wire, provided the test commands are captured. Detail: Identify Namespace data (4 KiB CplD/MWr to the host) shows NUSE at bytes 16–23. Compare captures before and after. The DULBE read's CQE carries SCT 2h / SC 87h when it works.
What happens
The namespace advertises thin provisioning (NSFEAT bit 0) or DULBE (NSFEAT bit 2), but NUSE never changes after writes or deallocates, or DULBE is enabled and reads of never-written/deallocated blocks still return Success. Management software that relies on NUSE for capacity planning or thin-provisioning alerts gets wrong numbers.
How it shows up
No error. NUSE stays equal to NCAP (or at its value from the factory), and Get Features 05h shows DULBE=1 while reads of unwritten LBAs succeed.
Spec basis
Identify Namespace NUSE ("current number of logical blocks allocated", ≤ NCAP), NSFEAT THIN/DULBE, Feature 05h DULBE bit 16. A drive without thin provisioning may legitimately report NUSE = NCAP. The QEMU model does so on purpose (hw/nvme/ns.c: "no thin provisioning").
Typical causes
NUSE computed once at format time; DULBE accepted by Set Features but not wired to the read path; DULBE checked for never-written blocks but not for deallocated ones (or the reverse).
How to detect / prove
After a Format (NUSE should drop to about 0 on a tracking drive), write N blocks (NUSE +N), deallocate them (NUSE −N). For DULBE: read a never-written LBA (expect 287h), write it (expect Success), deallocate it (expect 287h). The QEMU controller's nvme_check_dulbe() is a reference: it returns NVME_DULB when the block-status query says the range holds no data.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux does not use NUSE for block I/O. core.c:nvme_error_status() maps NVME_SC_UNWRITTEN_BLOCK (287h) to BLK_STS_MEDIUM; constants.c names it "Deallocated or Unwritten Logical Block".
Test to provoke it
CAUTION Format the test namespace, then run the NUSE/DULBE state table above: never-written → written → deallocated → written again, at both 1-block and 1 GiB scale. Disable DULBE afterwards.
🐞 Reading the cards together

D-01, D-02 and D-03 are the same mechanism seen at different strengths: nothing, part, or "something done but the read path not updated". D-04 is the false positive to rule out first. D-06 is the dangerous opposite of D-01: too much deallocated, which destroys data instead of wasting space. When a customer reports "trim doesn't work", check D-04 first, then D-01/D-02. When a customer reports "files corrupted after trim", check D-06 first.

Quiz

  1. A drive has DLFEAT = 00h and returns old data after a deallocate. Which card applies, and is it a spec violation?

    Answer

    Possibly D-01, but with RB = 000b old data is allowed, so the read-back is not proof and not a violation. Use DULBE (if NSFEAT bit 2) or NUSE, or a performance A/B, to decide.

  2. What is the one partial-deallocate case that a trace alone can prove?

    Answer

    The device fetched fewer than 16×(NR+1) bytes of range list, so it never saw the later ranges.

  3. Why is D-05 always provable by read-back but D-01 is not?

    Answer

    Write Zeroes defines the read result (zeroes) for every drive. DSM deallocate defines it only when DLFEAT says so.

IV.3 Durability and data-integrity failures: acknowledged but not true

← IV.2 Deallocate, Write Zeroes and allocation accounting: the error cards20 How AER Works, and What Linux v7.2 Does With It →

These failures break the most basic contract of a storage device: what I wrote and was told is safe, I will read back unchanged, from the place I wrote it. Every card here is silent at the moment it happens. The drive returned Success for the Write, the Flush, or the Read. The damage shows up later, often far from the cause: a database refuses to start, a filesystem check finds orphaned blocks, a checksum mismatch appears months later. Field studies show these are not theoretical:

  • Power faults: Zheng, Tucek, Qin and Lillibridge (FAST '13) cut power to 15 commodity SSDs from five vendors over more than 3,000 fault-injection cycles. 13 of the 15 showed failures such as bit corruption, shorn writes, unserializable writes, metadata corruption and total device failure. The extended ACM TOCS 2016 version reports 14 of 17 SSDs from six vendors failing, including devices sold as enterprise-class.
  • Corruption in production: Bairavasundaram et al. (FAST '08) analysed corruption across 1.53 million disk drives in NetApp systems over 41 months and found more than 400,000 checksum mismatches, which were not independent within a disk or a system. That study is about hard disks, but its taxonomy (checksum mismatches, lost writes, misdirected writes) applies to any block device. Krioukov et al. (FAST '08, "Parity Lost and Parity Regained") analysed how lost and misdirected writes defeat RAID parity schemes.
  • Drives that ignore flushes: Rajimwale et al. (DSN 2011, "Coerced Cache Eviction and Discreet-Mode Journaling") built a file-system technique specifically for disks that do not honour cache flushes correctly.
  • Flash in the field: Schroeder, Lagisetty and Merchant (FAST '16, Google) found that 20–63% of drives saw at least one uncorrectable error within four years, and that raw bit error rate does not predict uncorrectable errors well. Meza et al. (SIGMETRICS '15, Facebook) found that SSD failure rates do not rise steadily with wear but go through distinct periods. Maneas et al. (FAST '20, 1.4 million enterprise SSDs at NetApp) studied factors including firmware versions.

These studies count detected errors. The silent ones in this chapter are, by definition, under-counted. They are only found where an end-to-end checksum exists.

📘 The contracts involved
MechanismField / commandContract
Volatile write cacheIdentify Controller VWC bit 0 (present); Feature 06h WCE; Linux also reads NSFEAT bit 5 (NVME_NS_VWC_NOT_PRESENT) from the I/O-command-set-independent Identify NamespaceIf present and enabled, a Write completion does not mean the data is on non-volatile media.
FlushNVM opcode 00hOn completion, all data and metadata previously completed for the namespace is on non-volatile media. With no volatile cache present or enabled, Flush has no effect and completes Success.
Force Unit AccessRead/Write CDW12 bit 30 (Linux NVME_RW_FUA = control bit 14)That write is on non-volatile media before completion, regardless of cache.
AtomicityAWUN, AWUPF (Identify Controller), NAWUN, NAWUPF, NABSPF (Identify Namespace, when NSFEAT bit 1)Writes up to this size (and not crossing the boundary) are all-or-nothing in normal operation (AWUN) and across power fail (AWUPF).
Power-loss behaviourNo single field. Vendor "PLP" claims. Shutdown notification via CC.SHN and CSTS.SHSTWithout a volatile cache, or with power-loss protection, completed writes survive power loss. An orderly shutdown (SHN, wait for SHST = complete) must persist everything.
D-08Flush ignored / volatile write cache that lies SilentNVMe command
In a PEX trace
Partly visible Flush SQE (OPC 00h) and its CQE are visible, so you can show the host flushed. A Flush that always completes in microseconds after many MiB of writes is a clue, not proof. With VWC=0 you see no Flushes at all. The data loss needs a power-cut ledger test. Detail: Flush SQE (OPC 00h, NSID) and its Success CQE. How long the Flush took is the only clue: a Flush after many MiB of cached writes that completes in microseconds every time is suspicious, but not proof (the drive may have PLP). With VWC = 0 you will see no Flush commands at all.
What happens
Either (a) Flush completes Success before the cached data is on NAND, or (b) the drive reports VWC = 0 (no volatile cache) while it really buffers writes in DRAM or SRAM without power-loss protection. In case (b) Linux never sends a Flush at all, because the queue is set up without a write cache.
How it shows up
Nothing until an unexpected power loss. Then data that fsync()/fdatasync() reported as durable is missing or old, and journals replay inconsistent states.
Spec basis
NVM Command Set Flush; Identify Controller VWC; Set/Get Features 06h (Volatile Write Cache, WCE bit 0). A completed Flush is a durability promise for all previously completed writes.
Typical causes
Firmware completing Flush early for benchmark scores; a flush that only drains the controller's front-end buffer, not the FTL metadata; a missing VWC report; capacitors (PLP) degraded or absent on a SKU that shares firmware with a PLP model. A widely reported informal 2022 test (by Russ Bishop, covered in the press) claimed that two of four consumer NVMe SSDs lost flushed data on power cuts uncertain (not peer-reviewed; details not independently verified here).
How to detect / prove
Power-cut test with a host-side ledger (see Lab IV.3): write records, fsync/Flush, log the acknowledged sequence number to a different machine, cut power at random points, power up, check that every acknowledged record is present. Any acknowledged-but-missing record after Flush = proof. Check nvme id-ctrl -H VWC and nvme get-feature -f 6. On Linux, /sys/block/nvme0n1/queue/write_cache shows "write back" or "write through". "write through" on a drive you suspect caches is case (b).
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_update_ns_info_block() sets BLK_FEAT_WRITE_CACHE | BLK_FEAT_FUA only if ctrl->vwc & NVME_CTRL_VWC_PRESENT and not info->no_vwc; core.c:nvme_setup_flush() builds the Flush; core.c:nvme_ns_info_from_id_cs_indep() reads NVME_NS_VWC_NOT_PRESENT.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: controlled power interruption (programmable PSU or a PCIe slot power switch / interposer) during a write+fsync workload, hundreds of cycles, varied timing relative to the Flush completion. Compare with the same drive with WCE disabled (nvme set-feature -f 6 -v 0) as a control.
D-09FUA not honoured SilentNVMe command
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The FUA bit (Write CDW12 bit 30) and the completion latency are visible. FUA writes as fast as cached writes on a drive without PLP is a clue. Proof needs a power-cut test. Detail: Write SQE CDW12 bit 30 = 1. FUA writes that complete exactly as fast as non-FUA writes on a drive without PLP are a strong hint.
What happens
A Write with FUA = 1 completes while its data is still only in volatile cache. Filesystems and databases use FUA for journal commit blocks (Linux turns REQ_FUA into NVME_RW_FUA), so a lost FUA write loses the commit record of a transaction the application already saw as complete.
How it shows up
Only after power loss: the last committed transactions are missing even though the commit write completed.
Spec basis
Read/Write command Force Unit Access bit (CDW12 bit 30). Durable at completion, independent of the write cache.
Typical causes
Firmware treating FUA as a hint, or making the data durable but not the mapping-table update that points to it (after power-up the LBA maps to the old page).
How to detect / prove
Power-cut ledger test using only FUA writes and no Flush (use a small program that writes with O_DIRECT|O_DSYNC; Linux can then mark the write REQ_FUA when the queue advertises FUA. Confirm with the nvme_setup_cmd tracepoint that the control field has bit 14 set before trusting the test). Any acknowledged FUA write missing after power-up = proof.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_setup_rw(): if (req->cmd_flags & REQ_FUA) control |= NVME_RW_FUA;. FUA is advertised to the block layer only with a write cache (BLK_FEAT_FUA in nvme_update_ns_info_block()). Otherwise the block layer never sets it.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: as D-08 but with O_DSYNC writes. Compare the FUA write latency distribution with WCE on and off: if they are the same and the drive has no PLP, investigate.
D-10Acknowledged writes lost on power loss (PLP failure) SilentDevice internal
In a PEX trace
Not visible Not visible: the trace before the cut is normal, and after power-up you see only a normal (possibly slower) initialization. Use a power-cut ledger and the SMART Unsafe Shutdowns counter. Detail: Nothing before the cut. After power-up: normal initialization, possibly a longer time to CSTS.RDY = 1 (the drive rebuilding its map), then Reads returning old or wrong data.
What happens
On a drive that claims power-loss protection, or with the cache disabled, writes that completed before the power cut are missing or old after power-up. Also includes losing data written long before the cut, when an FTL metadata update in flight corrupts the mapping of older data. Zheng et al. classify this as metadata corruption.
How it shows up
Nothing at power-up in most cases. Sometimes the SMART log Unsafe Shutdowns counter increments (it should on every unexpected power loss), and sometimes Media and Data Integrity Errors rise if the drive notices. A drive that comes back with no namespace, read-only mode, or a Critical Warning bit set is the "total device failure" end of the range.
Spec basis
No single NVMe field promises PLP. Durability rests on Flush/FUA and the VWC report. SMART / Health log: Unsafe Shutdowns, Critical Warning bits (including "volatile memory backup device has failed"), Media and Data Integrity Errors.
Typical causes
Hold-up capacitors aged or out of tolerance; firmware not finishing the dump within the hold-up time; mapping table checkpoint corruption; power cut during an internal operation (GC, wear levelling) that moved live data.
How to detect / prove
Power-cut ledger test with verification of all previously written data, not just the latest records, because older data can be lost too. nvme smart-log before and after each cycle: Unsafe Shutdowns must increase by exactly one per cut (if it does not, the drive's power-loss detection itself is broken). Check the critical warning bits.
Linux v7.2 source
Linux has no visibility. On orderly shutdown it sets CC.SHN and waits for CSTS.SHST (see the shutdown timeout parameter shutdown_timeout in core.c). An unexpected power loss skips this entirely.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: hundreds to thousands of randomized power cuts (the FAST '13 study used more than 3,000 cycles across devices) with mixed sequential, random and flush-heavy workloads, including cuts during idle (background GC). Record temperature, as hold-up energy varies with it.
D-11Torn (shorn) write: atomicity broken SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Not visible Not visible: the write traffic is normal and the tear happens inside the drive at the cut. After power-up, Read payloads show mixed old/new blocks only if you read them back and know the expected content. Use record checksums. Detail: Normal Writes. The torn state is visible only in later Read payloads.
What happens
A write that should be atomic persists only partly: some logical blocks new, some old, or even part of one logical block new (a "shorn write" in Zheng et al.'s terms). Databases that rely on atomic 4 KiB or 16 KiB page writes (to skip double-write buffers) then see half-written pages.
How it shows up
After power loss (or after an error/reset during the write), reads return a mix. Page checksums in the application fail, but the drive reports nothing.
Spec basis
