AWUN/AWUPF (Identify Controller, 0's based in logical blocks), NAWUN/NAWUPF/NABSPF (Identify Namespace, valid when NSFEAT bit 1 is set). A write ≤ NAWUPF that does not cross an NABSPF boundary must be all-or-nothing even across power failure. A single logical block is always expected to be atomic.
Typical causes
NAND page programming interrupted; firmware splitting a host write across two internal units with separate mapping updates; advertising NAWUPF larger than really guaranteed.
How to detect / prove
Write records of exactly NAWUPF size, aligned to NABSPF, each carrying a sequence number and checksum in every logical block (so a mix is detectable). After a power cut every record must be entirely old or entirely new. Also test records of NAWUPF + 1 blocks to confirm the boundary (tearing is allowed there).
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_configure_atomic_write() uses NAWUPF (and NABSPF as the boundary) only when NSFEAT has the atomics bit. Otherwise it logs "AWUPF ignored, only NAWUPF accepted" and uses one logical block. nvme_update_disk_info() limits physical_block_size to the atomic size, because "Linux filesystems assume writing a single physical block is an atomic operation".
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: power-cut loop with atomic-size records. Also CAUTION: controller reset (nvme reset) or an FLR in the middle of large writes, then verify the records, because tearing can also follow resets, not only power loss.
D-12Unserializable persistence: write order not preserved across a power cut SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The trace proves the host side of the contract (Writes completed, Flush SQE/CQE, then the commit Write), which is half the evidence. The out-of-order persistence itself is not visible. Detail: Correct ordering on the host side: Writes completed, Flush SQE/CQE, then the commit Write. Capturing the trace proves the host kept its side of the contract.
What happens
After power loss, a later write (acknowledged and followed by Flush) is present, but an earlier one, also flushed before it, is missing. The persisted state matches no order in which the host could have seen the writes complete. Zheng et al. call these "unserializable writes".
How it shows up
Silent. Journaling and log-structured software break, because they assume "if the commit record is there, the data blocks before the flush are there too".
Spec basis
NVMe does not order independent commands, so writes in flight together may persist in any order. But Flush is a barrier: everything completed before it must be durable when it completes. A state with post-flush data present and pre-flush data missing violates the Flush contract.
Typical causes
Flush implemented per internal channel or die rather than globally. Mapping journal entries written out of order. The flush-ignoring behaviour of D-08 combined with a cache that evicts out of order.
How to detect / prove
Ledger with epochs: epoch N data → Flush → commit record N → Flush → epoch N+1. After a cut, if commit record N is present, every block of epoch N must verify. Note the distinction: missing data from an epoch whose Flush had not completed is legal.
Linux v7.2 source
Block layer flush machinery generates REQ_OP_FLUSH → core.c:nvme_setup_flush(). Linux does not wait for anything beyond the Flush CQE.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: power-cut loop with the epoch ledger. Run it with high queue depth and many concurrent streams so the drive's internal parallelism is busy.
D-13Lost write (dropped write) without any power event SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Visible If the Write and the later Read are both captured: the device fetched the new data (MRd/CplD of the write buffer), posted Success, and later returned the old version in the Read data payload. That is proof on the wire. Detail: Write SQE, data MRd/CplD (the device fetched the new data), Success CQE. The later Read returns the old payload. The trace proves the device received the new data, which pins the fault inside the device.
What happens
A Write completes Success, no power event or reset follows, and a later Read returns the previous content of the block. The write was dropped (never programmed, or programmed but the mapping not updated).
How it shows up
Silent. A per-block checksum stored with the block cannot catch it, because the old block is self-consistent. Only a checksum or version stored elsewhere (in the parent pointer, as in ZFS/Btrfs, or in an application index) catches it. This is the case Krioukov et al. analysed for RAID.
Spec basis
Write command semantics. With PI Type 1, the reference tag only checks the LBA, not the version, so PI does not detect lost writes either.
Typical causes
FTL mapping-update race (two writes to the same LBA in flight, the older one's mapping update applied last); write-cache bugs on overlapping writes; firmware error recovery that retries a failed program and silently gives up.
How to detect / prove
Write a version number into each block and keep the expected version in host memory (fio --verify with --verify_header-style headers, or a custom tool that stores per-LBA generation counters). Read back after overwrites. Old version = lost write. Stress overlapping writes to the same LBA at high queue depth.
Linux v7.2 source
No detection in the driver. Linux allows overlapping writes in flight (the block layer does not serialize them), so an application that issues them must not depend on order.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: fio --rw=randwrite --bs=4k --size=1G --iodepth=64 --verify=crc32c --verify_backlog=1024 with --norandommap (allows overwrites) plus a custom generation-counter checker. Long runs (hours), near-full drive to force GC.
D-14Misdirected write: data written to the wrong LBA SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The Write SQE SLBA shows where the host asked to write. The misdirection is visible only if you also capture a Read of the wrong destination returning that data (with an LBA header inside the payload). Detail: The Write SQE's SLBA shows what the host asked for. If the SLBA is already wrong, it is a host bug. If it is right and the data shows up elsewhere, it is the device.
What happens
The data for LBA X lands at LBA Y. Two corruptions result: X keeps old data (a lost write), and Y is overwritten with data that belongs elsewhere. Zheng et al. describe "flying writes" after power faults, data appearing at an unexpected location, which is the power-fault version of this.
How it shows up
Silent at the device. Detectable by checksums that embed the block's own address (a header containing the LBA, or PI reference tag with Type 1/2).
Spec basis
Write semantics; End-to-end Data Protection: with PI Type 1 the Reference Tag must match the lower bits of the LBA and the controller checks it when PRCHK includes the reference tag. A misdirected write then fails with Reference Tag Check Error (status 284h) on the read, or at write time if the controller re-checks.
Typical causes
FTL mapping corruption; namespace offset bugs (multi-namespace); bit flips in internal address paths without parity; on the host side, wrong SLBA from a stacking-driver bug.
How to detect / prove
Write blocks whose content includes their own LBA (fio verify headers include the offset). On read, check that the header offset equals the LBA read. A block containing another block's header = misdirected write, and the header tells you the intended destination.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_set_ref_tag() fills the reference tag for PI Type 1/2 (16-bit and 64-bit guard formats); core.c:nvme_error_status() maps NVME_SC_REFTAG_CHECK to BLK_STS_PROTECTION.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: full-drive fio write with --verify=crc32c (headers carry the offset), then a full read-verify, repeated after GC-heavy random overwrites and after power cuts (EXPERT). On a PI-formatted namespace, also confirm that a deliberately wrong SLBA via passthrough is caught.
D-15Stale data returned on read SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Visible With timestamps: the Write CQE MWr is earlier than the Read SQ doorbell MWr, and the Read data payload carries the old version. An analyzer capture gives exactly that ordering proof. Detail: Write CQE MWr timestamp earlier than the Read's SQ doorbell MWr timestamp, and the Read data payload carries the old version. An analyzer with timestamps gives exactly that proof.
What happens
A Read returns an older version of a block although a newer Write to that LBA has completed (no power event). Unlike D-13, the new data may be on media: a later Read, after a cache eviction or a reset, returns the correct new data. So the error comes and goes.
How it shows up
Silent, intermittent, and very hard to reproduce. Applications see "my write disappeared, then came back".
Spec basis
A Read that starts after a Write's completion must return that Write's data (or newer). NVMe does not order commands that are in flight at the same time, so a Read issued before the Write completed may legally return either version.
Typical causes
Read cache / read-ahead buffer not invalidated by a write to the same LBA; multi-queue race in firmware (write on one SQ, read on another); with multi-path/multi-controller subsystems, a second controller serving from a stale cache.
How to detect / prove
Write–complete–read loops with version numbers on the same LBAs from different queues (different CPUs) and, for multi-controller subsystems, via different controllers. Record the completion time of the write and the submission time of the read to show the read started after the write completed. That ordering is what turns "odd" into "proven".
Linux v7.2 source
Requests are spread over per-CPU hardware queues by blk-mq (block/blk-mq.c), so cross-queue read-after-write is normal Linux traffic.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: small hot set (for example 64 LBAs), one thread per CPU, each doing write(version)→wait for completion→read from another CPU→compare. Run with the drive both near-empty and full.
D-16Wrong data / bit flips returned without an error SilentData integrity
In a PEX trace
Partly visible The Read data payload with the wrong bytes is visible, and LCRC protected the hop, so the trace proves the device sent them. Recognising them as wrong needs the expected data (a captured earlier Write or a checksum). Detail: The Read data MWr payload already contains the wrong bytes, which proves the device sent them (PCIe LCRC protected the hop). If the trace shows correct data and the buffer is wrong, the fault is in host memory or an IOMMU/DMA mapping problem.
What happens
A Read completes Success but the data differs from what was written by a few bits or by a whole chunk. The drive's ECC either miscorrected (decoded to a wrong but valid codeword), or the corruption happened outside the ECC-protected path: controller SRAM/DRAM without parity, a firmware buffer handling bug, or the PCIe/host side (very unlikely to be undetected on PCIe because of LCRC, but possible in host memory).
How it shows up
Silent at the drive: no media error status, no SMART Media Errors increment. Only an end-to-end checksum catches it.
Spec basis
Read semantics. SMART/Health log Media and Data Integrity Errors counts only errors the controller detected. End-to-end Data Protection (PI guard CRC) covers the whole path from host buffer to media and back when PRCHK guard is enabled.
Typical causes
ECC miscorrection at high raw bit error rates; DRAM bit flips in the drive's buffer without ECC; firmware bugs in buffer management or in compression/encryption engines; host memory errors (rule out with ECC RAM and EDAC counters).
How to detect / prove
Checksummed reads: fio --verify=crc32c or sha256, a checksumming filesystem (ZFS/Btrfs scrub), PI with guard checking. Keep the bad buffer and compare it bit by bit with the expected one: a few scattered bit flips point to media/ECC, a block-sized chunk of other valid data points to D-14/D-15. Check host memory EDAC counters to rule out the host.
Linux v7.2 source
With a PI namespace and CONFIG_BLK_DEV_INTEGRITY, core.c:nvme_init_integrity() registers the profile so the block layer generates and verifies guard tags. Without it, Linux sets PRACT and the controller handles PI alone (core.c:nvme_setup_rw()), so host-to-controller transfer is not covered.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: long fio verify runs at high temperature, after many P/E cycles (endurance tests), and with data retention bakes (write, power off, store warm, read-verify). Compare the detected-error counters with the verify failures: every verify failure without a matching Media Errors increment is a silent corruption.
D-17Retries mask failing media DegradedDevice + host
In a PEX trace
Partly visible Device-internal retries are visible only as longer gaps between SQE fetch and data MWr. Host retries are visible as an error CQE (no DNR) followed by re-submission of the same SLBA/NLB. The media wear itself is not visible. Detail: Device-internal retries: only longer gaps between the Read SQE fetch and the data MWr. Host retries: the same command (same SLBA/NLB, new CID or same tag) re-submitted after an error CQE without DNR.
What happens
Data is returned correctly, but only after many internal read retries (read-level shifts, soft decoding) or after the host retried a failed command. Every layer reports success, while the media is wearing out and latency tails grow. Eventually it becomes an uncorrectable error. Schroeder et al. found uncorrectable errors in 20–63% of drives within four years, and that raw bit error rate is a poor predictor of them, so you cannot rely on a single "health" number.
How it shows up
Higher p99/p99.9 read latency on specific LBA ranges. SMART Percentage Used and Available Spare change slowly. On the host side, Linux retries a non-DNR error up to max_retries (default 5) times. A request that eventually succeeds produces no error to the application, and only the per-namespace command_retries_count in sysfs counts it.
Spec basis
CQE status Do Not Retry (DNR) bit and Command Retry Delay (CRD, with CRDT1–3 in Identify Controller). SMART/Health log Media and Data Integrity Errors, Available Spare, Percentage Used. Vendor or OCP logs may expose internal retry and ECC-correction counts impl.
Typical causes
Wear-out, read disturb, retention loss (data written long ago or at temperature extremes); host-visible retries also come from transient link or controller issues.
How to detect / prove
Latency heat map per LBA region (fio with --write_lat_log/--log_avg_msec, or bpftrace on block completion latency by sector). Read the per-path namespace counters in the diag sysfs group, …/diag/command_retries_count and …/diag/command_error_count (Linux v7.2 sysfs.c, nvme_ns_diag_attr_group; with native multipath they appear on the hidden path disk such as nvme0c0n1, not on the head nvme0n1). Track SMART over time. Compare Verify-command latency (opcode 0Ch, reads media without transfer) across regions.
Linux v7.2 source
core.c:nvme_decide_disposition() (RETRY unless DNR set, blk_noretry_request(), or retries >= nvme_max_retries); core.c:nvme_retry_req() (honours CRD via ctrl->crdt[], increments ns->retries); sysfs.c:command_retries_count_show(); module parameter nvme_core.max_retries.
Test to provoke it
SAFE to observe: periodic full-drive read scans with latency logging. CAUTION: use the kernel's NVMe fault injection (drivers/nvme/host/fault_inject.c, debugfs fault_inject) to inject retryable status codes and confirm that only the retry counter shows them.
🧪 Lab IV.3 — Power-cut durability ledger EXPERT LAB
⚠️ Hardware risk

Cutting power to a running SSD can corrupt or kill it, and switching slot power can harm the host if done wrongly. Use a spare drive, a dedicated test host, and a power-switching method designed for it (a programmable supply feeding a powered riser or interposer, or a controllable slot-power switch). Never cut the host's own power while the system disk is the device under test.

Objective. Prove or disprove D-08 to D-12 for one drive.

Setup. Device under test (DUT) on a separately powered riser. The test host writes; a second machine (the ledger) receives acknowledgements over the network, because the ledger must survive the cut.

loop forever:
    rec = {seq, epoch, lba, crc32c(payload), payload}
    write(rec) to LBA; on completion:
        if mode == FUA:   use O_DSYNC write              # D-09
        if mode == FLUSH: every K records: fdatasync()   # D-08 / D-12
        send(seq, lba, "durable") to ledger  ONLY after the durability point
    at random time T: ledger tells power controller: CUT
power on, wait for CSTS.RDY, read nvme smart-log (Unsafe Shutdowns must be +1)
verify:
    for every seq the ledger marked durable: record present, crc ok      # D-08/09/10
    every record either fully old or fully new (atomic-size records)    # D-11
    if commit(N) present then all of epoch N present                     # D-12
    no record found at an LBA other than its own                         # D-14

Observations. For each cycle: time from last durability point to cut, number of missing durable records, torn records, Unsafe Shutdowns delta, time to CSTS.RDY.

Solution / interpretation

Zero missing durable records over hundreds of cycles is a pass for that workload. One missing record after a completed Flush or FUA is a proven durability failure. Report it with the ledger extract, the power-cut timestamp and the SMART log. Torn records only above NAWUPF are allowed. Unsafe Shutdowns not incrementing is a separate bug in power-loss detection.

Extension. Repeat with WCE disabled (Feature 06h = 0) as a control, at high and low ambient temperature, and with the drive nearly full so GC runs during the cut.

PART V

PCIe Link and Transaction Errors

Every NVMe command travels as PCIe Transaction Layer Packets (TLPs), and every one of them can be damaged, refused, dropped or delayed on the way. PCIe has its own error system, Advanced Error Reporting (AER). AER is separate from NVMe status codes, and the NVMe driver often never hears about it. This part covers every AER status bit that Linux v7.2 defines, then the many link and fabric failures that set no AER bit at all: a link that trained narrow or slow, a recovery storm, a surprise link-down, Downstream Port Containment (DPC), lost interrupts, Input/Output Memory Management Unit (IOMMU) faults and bad Base Address Register (BAR) assignment. Card ids use the prefix L-.

20 How AER Works, and What Linux v7.2 Does With It

← IV.3 Durability and data-integrity failures: acknowledged but not true21 Correctable AER Errors: The Link Is Telling You Something →

20.1 Why this chapter exists

When an NVMe drive "disappears", the first evidence is usually a few lines from AER: or pcieport in the kernel log, not an NVMe status code. To read those lines you need four things: what each register holds, which layer of PCIe detected the problem, how severity is decided, and what Linux does next. Linux can do nothing, reset the link, or remove the device. This chapter explains all four and ends with a reference table of every AER status bit in include/uapi/linux/pci_regs.h. Chapters 21 and 22 give each bit its own card.

  • Name the AER registers (Uncorrectable Status/Mask/Severity, Correctable Status/Mask, Capabilities and Control, Header Log, Root Error Command/Status, Error Source ID) and their offsets inside the AER extended capability.
  • Explain correctable, non-fatal uncorrectable and fatal uncorrectable, and who decides which one applies.
  • Follow Linux v7.2 from the Root Port interrupt to pcie_do_recovery() to the NVMe driver's nvme_error_detected().
  • Find the per-device AER counters in sysfs and know why they can read zero on the device that caused the problem.

20.2 The AER capability in one picture

AER is a PCI Express extended capability (ID 0001h) in configuration space at offset 100h or above. The offsets below are relative to the start of that capability. They come from include/uapi/linux/pci_regs.h in Linux v7.2 verified.

OffsetRegister (Linux macro)What it holdsWho writes it
04hUncorrectable Error Status (PCI_ERR_UNCOR_STATUS)One RW1C bit per uncorrectable error type: PCI_ERR_UNC_*Hardware sets; software clears by writing 1
08hUncorrectable Error Mask (PCI_ERR_UNCOR_MASK)Same bit layout. A masked error is not logged in the Header Log, is not the First Error Pointer and sends no message.Firmware / OS
0ChUncorrectable Error Severity (PCI_ERR_UNCOR_SEVER)Same bit layout. 1 = Fatal (ERR_FATAL), 0 = Non-Fatal (ERR_NONFATAL).Firmware / OS
10hCorrectable Error Status (PCI_ERR_COR_STATUS)PCI_ERR_COR_* bitsHardware sets, RW1C
14hCorrectable Error Mask (PCI_ERR_COR_MASK)Same layout as 10hFirmware / OS
18hAdvanced Error Capabilities and Control (PCI_ERR_CAP)First Error Pointer (bits 4:0); ECRC Generation/Check Capable and Enable; TLP Prefix Log Present; Completion Timeout header-log capable; Flit-mode TLP log fieldsHardware / OS
1ChHeader Log (PCI_ERR_HEADER_LOG)Header of the TLP that caused the first logged errorHardware
2ChRoot Error Command (PCI_ERR_ROOT_COMMAND)Interrupt enables for received ERR_COR, ERR_NONFATAL and ERR_FATAL (Root Ports and RCECs only)OS
30hRoot Error Status (PCI_ERR_ROOT_STATUS)ERR_COR received, multiple ERR_COR, ERR_FATAL/NONFATAL received, multiple, first-uncorrectable-is-fatal, non-fatal received, fatal received, interrupt message numberHardware
34hError Source Identification (PCI_ERR_ROOT_ERR_SRC)Requester ID of the last ERR_COR source (low 16 bits) and the last ERR_FATAL/NONFATAL source (high 16 bits)Hardware
🌍 Analogy

Think of each PCIe device as a building with a fire panel. The status register is the panel of lamps, one per alarm type. The mask register is the set of lamps someone has taped over. The severity register decides whether a lamp calls the fire brigade (fatal) or only the caretaker (non-fatal). The Root Port is the central dispatch. It receives a short message ("building 03:00.0 has an alarm") and must then phone the building to find out which lamp is lit.

🧩 Where the analogy breaks

The message that reaches the Root Port (ERR_COR, ERR_NONFATAL, ERR_FATAL) carries only a Requester ID and a class, never the specific bit. Linux has to read the device's AER registers over the same link that may just have failed. On a fatal error Linux does not read the endpoint's uncorrectable registers at all. Section 20.5 shows the code.

20.3 Three classes of error, and who decides

  • Correctable. Hardware already fixed it. Examples are a bad Link CRC (LCRC) that a Data Link Layer replay fixed, or a receiver symbol error. The device sends ERR_COR. No data was lost, but each one costs bandwidth and latency. A rising rate is the most important early warning of a marginal link.
  • Uncorrectable, non-fatal. One transaction was lost or refused, but the link itself still works. The device sends ERR_NONFATAL. Examples are an Unsupported Request, a Completion Timeout, or a Poisoned TLP. The link is usable, so recovery may not need a reset.
  • Uncorrectable, fatal. The link or the device's protocol state can no longer be trusted. The device sends ERR_FATAL. Examples are a Data Link Protocol error, Surprise Down, a Malformed TLP or a Receiver Overflow. The link must be reset.

The Severity register decides fatal versus non-fatal for each uncorrectable bit. It has a spec-defined default, but firmware or the OS can change it. Before you reason from the log line, check the actual value with lspci -vvv ("UESvrt:"). The defaults are in the table in section 20.8.

📭 What the spec does not say

The PCIe Base Specification says when an error must be detected and signalled. It does not require the OS to do anything in particular. Whether a non-fatal Completion Timeout ends up as an NVMe I/O error, a controller reset or nothing visible depends entirely on the OS and the device. Everything in this part about "what happens next" describes Linux v7.2 behaviour impl.

20.4 Which layer detects what (as Linux reports it)

Every AER log line contains type=Physical Layer, Data Link Layer or Transaction Layer. Linux v7.2 does not read this from the hardware. It derives it from the status bits with the macro AER_GET_LAYER_ERROR in drivers/pci/pcie/aer.c verified:

/* drivers/pci/pcie/aer.c (v7.2), abbreviated */
#define AER_PHYSICAL_LAYER_ERROR_MASK(t) ((t == AER_CORRECTABLE) ? PCI_ERR_COR_RCVR : 0)
#define AER_DATA_LINK_LAYER_ERROR_MASK(t) ((t == AER_CORRECTABLE) ?  \
        (PCI_ERR_COR_BAD_TLP | PCI_ERR_COR_BAD_DLLP |                  \
         PCI_ERR_COR_REP_ROLL | PCI_ERR_COR_REP_TIMER) : PCI_ERR_UNC_DLP)
/* everything else is reported as "Transaction Layer" */

So Receiver Error is reported as Physical Layer. Bad TLP, Bad DLLP, REPLAY_NUM Rollover, Replay Timer Timeout and Data Link Protocol Error are reported as Data Link Layer. Every other bit is reported as Transaction Layer, including Surprise Down, even though the PCIe spec counts Surprise Down as a Data Link Layer error. When several bits are set, the line names only the first matching layer. The "agent" part of the line is derived the same way: Completer Abort gives Completer ID; Completion Timeout and Unsupported Request give Requester ID; Rollover and Replay Timer give Transmitter ID; everything else gives Receiver ID.

20.5 The Linux v7.2 AER path, step by step

The step-through below follows one error from the wire to the NVMe driver. The function names are the ones in drivers/pci/pcie/aer.c, err.c and drivers/nvme/host/pci.c.

Device / Root Port hardwarePCIe messageLinux PCI coreNVMe driver
  1. Device detects and signals

    The detecting device sets a bit in its AER status register. If the bit is unmasked it records the header of the offending TLP and sends an error message upstream. The detecting device can be the NVMe endpoint or the Root Port itself (for example, a Root Port that receives a bad TLP from the SSD). ERR_COR is used for correctable errors, and ERR_NONFATAL or ERR_FATAL for uncorrectable ones, chosen by the Severity register.

    Where: endpoint or port hardwarePCIe: Yes, device → Root Port · Message TLP
    e.g. endpoint 0000:03:00.0 sets UNCOR_STATUS bit 14 (Completion Timeout), severity 0 → ERR_NONFATAL (synthetic)
  2. Root Port interrupts the CPU

    The Root Port sets Root Error Status and Error Source ID, then raises its AER interrupt. In Linux v7.2 aer_irq() reads Root Error Status and Error Source ID, writes them back to clear them, and queues them in a kfifo. The threaded handler aer_isr() drains the fifo with aer_isr_one_error(), which handles the correctable part first and then the uncorrectable part.

    Where: drivers/pci/pcie/aer.cPCIe: MSI/MSI-X from Root Port
  3. Find the source and read its registers

    find_source_device() walks the hierarchy below the Root Port. aer_get_device_error_info() reads status and mask from each candidate. For correctable errors it reads the correctable registers. For uncorrectable errors it reads the uncorrectable registers only if the device is a Root Port, an RCEC or a Downstream Port, or the error is non-fatal. On a fatal error from an endpoint the link is assumed broken, so the endpoint's registers are not read. It then prints with aer_print_error(). All devices are printed before any is handled, "to not lose records by reset".

    Where: aer_process_err_devices()PCIe: CfgRd0/CfgRd1 to each candidate
  4. Broadcast error_detected()

    pci_aer_handle_error() clears a correctable error and calls the driver's optional cor_error_detected. For non-fatal errors it calls pcie_do_recovery(dev, pci_channel_io_normal, aer_root_reset), and for fatal errors the same with pci_channel_io_frozen. pcie_do_recovery() in err.c calls every driver below the bridge. The NVMe driver's nvme_error_detected() returns CAN_RECOVER for normal, disables the controller and returns NEED_RESET for frozen, and returns DISCONNECT for perm_failure.

    Where: drivers/pci/pcie/err.cPCIe: No (software)
  5. Reset the link if needed

    If the channel was frozen or any driver voted NEED_RESET, aer_root_reset() runs. It does a secondary bus reset of the Root or Downstream Port via pci_bus_error_reset(), or an FLR for an RCiEP, and logs "%s Port link has been reset (%d)". With DPC, dpc_reset_link() is used instead, because the hardware has already taken the link down.

    Where: aer_root_reset() / dpc_reset_link()PCIe: Yes, link goes down and retrains
  6. slot_reset and resume

    nvme_slot_reset() logs "restart after slot reset", restores config space and schedules the NVMe reset work. nvme_error_resume() waits for that work to finish. If everything voted RECOVERED, the PCI core logs "device recovery successful". Otherwise it calls error_detected again with perm_failure and logs "device recovery failed".

    Where: drivers/nvme/host/pci.cPCIe: Yes, config restore and controller re-init

20.6 What the log looks like

The format strings below are copied from aer.c v7.2. The bus type is "PCIe", or "CXL" for CXL devices. Linux adds the prefix AER: through dev_fmt. The example output is synthetic and abbreviated, built only from those format strings.

pcieport 0000:00:1d.0: AER: Uncorrectable (Non-Fatal) error message received from 0000:03:00.0
nvme 0000:03:00.0: AER: PCIe Bus Error: severity=Uncorrectable (Non-Fatal), type=Transaction Layer, (Requester ID)
nvme 0000:03:00.0: AER:   device [144d:a80a] error status/mask=00004000/00400000
nvme 0000:03:00.0: AER:    [14] CmpltTO                (First)
pcieport 0000:00:1d.0: AER: device recovery successful

Details to know when reading these lines:

  • error status/mask are the raw 32-bit values. The bit list that follows is status & ~mask. A set bit with no name in Linux's string table prints as Unknown Error Bit.
  • (First) marks the bit that the First Error Pointer (PCI_ERR_CAP_FEP) names. When several bits are set, this one caused the Header Log capture.
  • A TLP Header: line is printed only for the bits in AER_LOG_TLP_MASKS: Poisoned TLP, Poisoned TLP Egress Blocked, ECRC, UR, CA, Unexpected Completion, ACS Violation, MC Blocked, AtomicOp Egress Blocked, DMWr Blocked, TLP Translation Egress Blocked, TLP Prefix Blocked, Malformed TLP, IDE Check, Misrouted IDE and PCRC. It is also printed for Completion Timeout, but only if the device sets the "Completion Timeout Prefix/Header Log Capable" bit (PCI_ERR_CAP_COMP_TIME_LOG). See tlp_header_logged().
  • If the source cannot be found, the Root Port line ends with (no details found. If the status read returns zero, the line reads type=Inaccessible, (Unregistered Agent ID). Both mean "something sent an error message and Linux could not get the details". This is common when the device has already dropped off the bus.
  • Correctable errors are logged at KERN_WARNING and uncorrectable errors at KERN_ERR, so dmesg -l err hides every correctable error.

20.7 Rate limits, counters and the "it was the other end" trap

Linux v7.2 rate-limits AER printing per device. Correctable and non-fatal errors each get a ratelimit state initialised in pci_aer_init() with DEFAULT_RATELIMIT_INTERVAL and DEFAULT_RATELIMIT_BURST. Documentation/PCI/pcieaer-howto.rst gives these as 10 events per 5 seconds. Fatal errors are never rate-limited. The limits can be tuned in sysfs under /sys/bus/pci/devices/<BDF>/aer/: correctable_ratelimit_interval_ms, correctable_ratelimit_burst, nonfatal_ratelimit_interval_ms and nonfatal_ratelimit_burst. Counting is not rate-limited. pci_dev_aer_stats_incr() runs before the ratelimit check, so the counters are the reliable source.

# per-device counters (every AER-capable device)
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/aer_dev_correctable
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/aer_dev_nonfatal
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/aer_dev_fatal
# Root Port totals (messages received, including its own)
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:00:1d.0/aer_rootport_total_err_cor
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:00:1d.0/aer_rootport_total_err_nonfatal
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:00:1d.0/aer_rootport_total_err_fatal

Each aer_dev_* file prints one line per named bit (RxErr 0, BadTLP 3, ...) followed by TOTAL_ERR_COR, TOTAL_ERR_FATAL or TOTAL_ERR_NONFATAL. The correctable array has 16 entries (AER_MAX_TYPEOF_COR_ERRS).

⚠️ The "it was the other end" trap

The comment above struct aer_info in aer.c warns that counters show errors "as seen by this device". If the SSD's transmitter is bad, the Root Port's receiver sees the errors, so the Root Port's counters go up and the SSD's stay at 0. Always read both ends of the link: the SSD and its upstream port, which may be a switch Downstream Port rather than the Root Port.

🏭 Implementation-dependent: firmware-first

Linux handles AER only if firmware gives it control through ACPI _OSC (pcie_aer_is_native()). On many servers firmware keeps control ("firmware-first"). Errors then arrive as APEI/GHES records, go through aer_recover_queue() and are printed by pci_print_aer() in a different format (aer_status:, aer_mask:, aer_layer=, aer_agent=). Firmware may also hide, mask or count errors that Linux never sees. If dmesg shows no AER lines at all, first check that this is not the reason.

20.8 Reference table: every AER status bit in Linux v7.2

Name, bit and Linux string come from pci_regs.h and the string tables in aer.c verified. "Default severity" is the reset value of the Uncorrectable Error Severity bit as commonly documented for the PCIe Base Specification (Fatal = 1). The classic bits are widely documented. Values marked "verify" come from newer revisions and should be confirmed in the specification. "Default mask" is noted only where the bit is masked by default. The NVMe-context column is engineering experience derived, not spec text.

BitMacroLinux stringClassDefault severity / maskLinux layer / agentTypical NVMe-context causeCard
C0PCI_ERR_COR_RCVRRxErrCorrectable—Physical / ReceiverSignal integrity: bad riser, retimer, connector, poor equalization at Gen4/Gen5L-01

* Linux v7.2 reports Surprise Down as "Transaction Layer" because its layer macro only special-cases DLP among the uncorrectable bits. The PCIe spec classes it as a Data Link Layer error.

Bits not listed are reserved in Linux v7.2's tables: correctable 1–5 and 9–11, and uncorrectable 1–3 and 6–11. If hardware sets one, the log line reads Unknown Error Bit.

💡 Senior insight

The NVMe controller and the PCIe AER block are different pieces of logic, often from different IP vendors. AER errors usually do not appear in the NVMe Error Information log page (Log ID 01h), and NVMe status codes do not appear in AER. To diagnose a failure, line up three timelines: the AER lines in dmesg, the NVMe driver lines (nvme nvme0: ...), and the device's own logs (nvme error-log, telemetry). A Completion Timeout in AER that comes 50 ms before an NVMe "I/O timeout, aborting" is almost always the cause, not a coincidence.

Quiz

  1. The SSD logs nothing, but the Root Port's aer_dev_correctable shows thousands of BadTLP. Which side's transmitter is suspect?

    Answer

    The SSD's. The Root Port received bad TLPs, so the SSD's transmit path is the one to examine: its TX lanes, the connector, a retimer, or the Root Port's receiver equalization. Counters show what each device received.

  2. Why does Linux not read an endpoint's uncorrectable status after an ERR_FATAL?

    Answer

    aer_get_device_error_info() reads uncorrectable registers only for Root Ports, RCECs, Downstream Ports, or non-fatal errors. After a fatal error the link to the endpoint is assumed unreliable, so a config read could hang or return all-ones.

  3. You see [14] CmpltTO with no TLP Header line. Is the Header Log empty?

    Answer

    Not necessarily. Linux prints the header for Completion Timeout only if the device sets PCI_ERR_CAP_COMP_TIME_LOG. Otherwise it assumes no header was logged.

21 Correctable AER Errors: The Link Is Telling You Something

← 20 How AER Works, and What Linux v7.2 Does With It22 Uncorrectable AER Errors: Lost, Refused and Malformed Transactions →

21.1 Why correctable errors matter

A correctable error costs no data. The Data Link Layer replays the TLP and the command completes normally, so the NVMe driver never knows anything happened. But each replay costs link time, and a rate of correctable errors shows how much margin the link has. A Gen5 x4 SSD that logs a few Receiver Errors per day is healthy. One that logs a few hundred per second has lost real bandwidth and latency. It is also one temperature swing away from Replay Rollover, link Recovery and, eventually, a Surprise Down. This chapter covers the eight correctable bits and how to turn their counts into a verdict.

🧒 Kid analogy

You are passing notes in class. If a note gets smudged, your friend says "say again?" and you resend it. Nothing is lost, but if you have to resend every other note, the lesson is nearly over before your message gets through. Correctable errors are the "say again?" requests.

21.2 The replay mechanism in brief

Every TLP gets a 12-bit sequence number and a 32-bit Link CRC (LCRC) from the Data Link Layer. The receiver checks both. If they are good it returns an Ack DLLP. If they are bad it returns a Nak, discards the TLP and sets Bad TLP. The transmitter keeps every unacknowledged TLP in a replay buffer. On a Nak, or when its replay timer expires with no Ack/Nak (Replay Timer Timeout), it resends everything from the oldest unacknowledged TLP onward. A 2-bit REPLAY_NUM counter counts replays of the same TLP. When it rolls over (REPLAY_NUM Rollover) the transmitter sends the link into LTSSM Recovery to retrain. Physical-layer framing and symbol errors are counted as Receiver Error. In Gen6 Flit mode, Forward Error Correction (FEC) corrects most symbol errors before any of this happens, and the detailed rules differ version.

Synthetic trace — one LCRC error, Nak, replay (link-layer view)
t (ns)DirPacketSeqNote
0RC→EPMWr 64B (SQ tail doorbell)0x41normal
120EP→RCAck0x41
400EP→RCMRd 64B (fetch SQE)0x9a
1100RC→EPCplD 64B0x42LCRC bad at EP receiver (EP sets BadTLP)
1180EP→RCNak0x41"last good = 0x41"
1260RC→EPCplD 64B (replay)0x42RC replays from 0x42
1340EP→RCAck0x42recovered; SQE fetch ~250 ns late

21.3 How to judge a correctable-error rate

The PCIe spec defines a target bit error rate (BER) for the electrical link, but it does not say "N correctable errors per hour is a failure". Your platform qualification has to set that threshold impl. A practical method:

  1. Snapshot aer_dev_correctable on the SSD and on its upstream port. Run a fixed workload, for example 10 minutes of 128 KiB sequential read at QD32 with fio. Snapshot again.
  2. Compute errors per second. Compare across identical slots or systems. A slot that is far worse than its siblings points to the platform (riser, cable, retimer). A drive that is bad in every slot points to the drive.
  3. Correlate with temperature (nvme smart-log) and with link state (lspci -vvv LnkSta). A rising rate together with a speed drop (Chapter 23) means the link is retraining down.
L-01Receiver Error (RxErr) — physical-layer symbol or framing errors ReportedPCIe Physical
What happens
The receiver sees a bad symbol, disparity or block-encoding error, a framing error, or a lane-to-lane deskew problem. The TLP or DLLP that contained it is dropped and normally replayed.
How it shows up
Correctable Status bit 0 (PCI_ERR_COR_RCVR), ERR_COR to the Root Port. Linux v7.2 prints PCIe Bus Error: severity=Correctable, type=Physical Layer, (Receiver ID) and [ 0] RxErr at KERN_WARNING, rate-limited. The RxErr counter in aer_dev_correctable increments. The NVMe layer reports nothing.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification, AER Correctable Error Status register, Receiver Error Status. Physical Layer receiver error rules.
Typical causes
Marginal channel: long traces, riser or cable adapters, U.2/U.3 backplanes, missing or misconfigured retimers, poor TX/RX equalization presets at Gen4/Gen5, reference-clock problems (SSC mismatch with separate clocks), contamination or wear on M.2 connectors, thermal drift.
How to detect / prove
Read counters at both ends (cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/<BDF>/aer_dev_correctable). lspci -vvv shows CESta: RxErr+ and DevSta: CorrErr+. For per-lane detail, use the Lane Error Status register of the Secondary PCIe Extended Capability (setpci read-only) or the platform's lane-margining tool (Gen4+ Lane Margining at the Receiver).
In a trace
An analyzer flags disparity, invalid symbols or invalid sync headers on a specific lane, followed by a Nak or a replay. Interposers can add their own errors, so confirm with the device's own counters.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pcie/aer.c: aer_irq, aer_isr_one_error, aer_print_error, pci_dev_aer_stats_incr, pci_aer_handle_error (clears status and calls cor_error_detected if the driver has one; the NVMe driver does not).
Test to provoke it
SAFE: run the workload with the SSD in different slots, with and without an extender. CAUTION: use lane margining (time/voltage offset) to step toward the eye edge on Gen4+ platforms. EXPERT: insert a lossy channel or an attenuating fixture.
L-02Bad TLP — LCRC or sequence error, TLP replayed ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
A TLP arrives with a bad LCRC or an unexpected sequence number. The receiver Naks it and the transmitter replays it. The NVMe command is delayed by roughly one replay round trip.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 6 (PCI_ERR_COR_BAD_TLP), [ 6] BadTLP, reported by Linux as Data Link Layer / Receiver ID. Counter BadTLP.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification, Data Link Layer: LCRC, Sequence Number, Ack/Nak protocol; AER Bad TLP Status.
Typical causes
Same channel problems as L-01 when the error is not caught as a symbol error. Occasionally a controller bug that generates a wrong LCRC on certain payload sizes.
How to detect / prove
Counters at both ends. If BadTLP rises only during large DMA (writes to the SSD stress the RC→EP direction; reads stress EP→RC), the problem is direction-dependent: look at the transmitter on the far side.
In a trace
A Nak DLLP whose AckNak_Seq_Num is earlier than the TLP just sent, followed by a run of replayed TLPs with repeated sequence numbers. Analyzers mark these as "replayed".
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_DATA_LINK_LAYER_ERROR_MASK, aer_correctable_error_string ("BadTLP").
Test to provoke it
As L-01. An error-injecting interposer or jammer can corrupt the LCRC of chosen TLPs (EXPERT, needs lab equipment).
L-03Bad DLLP — corrupted Ack/Nak/flow-control DLLP ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
A DLLP (Ack, Nak, UpdateFC, PM) fails its 16-bit CRC and is discarded. A lost Ack is harmless because later Acks are cumulative, unless the next one is also late, in which case L-05 follows. A lost UpdateFC can briefly starve the transmitter of credits.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 7 (PCI_ERR_COR_BAD_DLLP), [ 7] BadDLLP, Data Link Layer.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification, DLLP CRC; AER Bad DLLP Status.
Typical causes
Channel quality, as L-01 and L-02. DLLPs are short, so a high BadDLLP count with little BadTLP points to burst errors.
How to detect / prove
Counters. Look for throughput dips that coincide with bursts of BadDLLP, which show credit starvation.
In a trace
A DLLP marked CRC-error by the analyzer, possibly followed by a replay timeout (L-05) on the far side.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "BadDLLP", data-link classification.
Test to provoke it
As L-01 and L-02.
L-04REPLAY_NUM Rollover — four replays failed, link forced into Recovery ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
The same TLP has been replayed until the 2-bit REPLAY_NUM counter rolls over. The transmitter starts LTSSM Recovery to retrain the link. No new TLPs move for the length of the retrain, typically microseconds but sometimes much longer. If retraining picks a lower speed, the link stays degraded (see L-25).
How it shows up
Correctable bit 8 (PCI_ERR_COR_REP_ROLL), [ 8] Rollover, Data Link / Transmitter ID. Because the agent is the transmitter, the device that logs it is the one whose packets were not getting through. If the port supports bandwidth notification, Link Status LBMS may also be set (Linux bwctrl tracks this).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: REPLAY_NUM, Recovery entry; AER REPLAY_NUM Rollover Status.
Typical causes
Severe signal integrity problems, a receiver CDR losing lock, or a lane that stopped working.
How to detect / prove
Counter Rollover. Check LnkSta speed and width before and after. On the analyzer, look for TS1/TS2 ordered sets mid-traffic (Recovery). Repeated Rollovers are the "recovery storm" pattern of L-26.
In a trace
The same sequence number replayed several times, then electrical idle or TS1 ordered sets, then traffic resuming. NVMe commands in flight show a latency gap the same length as the Recovery.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_AGENT_TRANSMITTER_MASK (Rollover, Timeout), string "Rollover". drivers/pci/pcie/bwctrl.c: pcie_bwnotif_irq records LBMS.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an interposer that Naks or corrupts every copy of one TLP; or margin the link until rollovers appear.
L-05Replay Timer Timeout — no Ack/Nak in time ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
The transmitter's replay timer expires before any Ack or Nak arrives for outstanding TLPs, so it replays them all. This is "timer only" at link level, but AER does report it.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 12 (PCI_ERR_COR_REP_TIMER), [12] Timeout, Data Link / Transmitter ID. Do not confuse it with Completion Timeout (uncorrectable bit 14, L-14).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: REPLAY_TIMER, its limit formula (depends on link width, max payload, and L0s exit latency); AER Replay Timer Timeout Status.
Typical causes
Lost or corrupted Acks (L-03). Receiver Ack-coalescing that is too slow. Long ASPM L0s/L1 exit latency on the receiver side so Acks leave late. A link partner stalled internally.
How to detect / prove
Counter Timeout. If it correlates with ASPM being enabled (it disappears with pcie_aspm=off as a diagnostic boot), suspect power-management exit latency (see L-37).
In a trace
A gap with no Ack after TLPs, then a replay of the same sequence numbers without any preceding Nak.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "Timeout", transmitter agent mask.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: enable aggressive ASPM (/sys/module/pcie_aspm/parameters/policy set to powersupersave) with bursty, low-QD I/O. Compare counts with ASPM off.
L-06Advisory Non-Fatal Error — an uncorrectable error handled as correctable ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The PCIe spec allows certain uncorrectable non-fatal errors to be signalled as "advisory" when the detecting agent is not the one that has to act. A typical case is a completer that returns UR to a request and also logs the error: it signals ERR_COR instead of ERR_NONFATAL, because the requester will handle the failed completion. The uncorrectable status bit (for example UR) is still set as well.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 13 (PCI_ERR_COR_ADV_NFAT), [13] NonFatalErr, plus the underlying uncorrectable bit in the same device's uncorrectable status, which Linux does not print on this path. Masked by default per spec, so many systems never show it.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Advisory Non-Fatal Error cases; AER Advisory Non-Fatal Error Status and Mask.
Typical causes
Config reads of absent functions during enumeration (UR, advisory). An endpoint receiving a request it does not support, for example an MMIO read to an unimplemented register.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv: look for CESta: AdvNonFatalErr+ together with UESta: UnsupReq+ on the same device. Read both registers with setpci (read-only).
In a trace
A request answered with a completion that has status UR or CA, then an ERR_COR message from the completer.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "NonFatalErr". Handled like every correctable error: cleared, no recovery.
Test to provoke it
SAFE (if unmasked): lspci scans of absent functions. CAUTION: unmasking the bit with setpci changes platform configuration.
L-07Corrected Internal Error — controller fixed its own internal fault ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The function detected and corrected an internal error, for example single-bit ECC in a buffer RAM of the PCIe or NVMe front end. What counts as an internal error is up to the device.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 14 (PCI_ERR_COR_INTERNAL), [14] CorrIntErr. Masked by default. Linux v7.2 unmasks internal errors only for CXL devices (pci_aer_unmask_internal_errors(), exported only to cxl_core; its comment says internal errors are "too device-specific to enable generally").
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Corrected Internal Error Status/Mask (optional).
Typical causes
SRAM soft errors, aging, temperature. A rising rate can come before an uncorrectable internal error (L-22) or an NVMe-level Persistent Internal Error event.
How to detect / prove
Unmask it in a lab with setpci (CAUTION), then watch the counter. Correlate with the NVMe SMART "Media and Data Integrity Errors" field and with vendor telemetry.
In a trace
Only an ERR_COR message; nothing else is visible.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: pci_aer_unmask_internal_errors, is_aer_internal_error (CXL RAS only).
Test to provoke it
Vendor-specific error injection only.
L-08Header Log Overflow — more errors than the log can hold ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
With multiple-header recording enabled, the device's header log is full, so the next error's TLP header is lost. Diagnosis loses information exactly when errors are arriving fastest.
How it shows up
Correctable bit 15 (PCI_ERR_COR_LOG_OVER), [15] HeaderOF. Masked by default.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Multiple Header Recording, Header Log Overflow Status.
Typical causes
A burst of UR or CA from a misbehaving DMA engine. Software that does not clear status fast enough.
How to detect / prove
Treat it as a sign that the logged header is not necessarily representative. Look at the first error, not the last.
In a trace
Many erroneous transactions in a short window.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "HeaderOF".
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: error injection while multiple-header recording is enabled.
🐞 Debugging connection

None of the eight correctable bits ever produce an NVMe error, but all of them show up in NVMe latency. If p99.99 latency has spikes at the microsecond-to-millisecond scale with no NVMe errors, compare the spike timestamps with AER counter deltas sampled every second. A link Recovery after a Rollover stalls every queue at once, which is the giveaway: all queues spike together, not just one.

🧪 Lab 21.1 — Measure a correctable-error rate on both ends of the link SAFE

Objective: get an errors-per-second figure for the SSD link and decide which end is receiving the errors.

Setup: Linux with AER native control (check for AER: enabled with IRQ or that the aer_dev_* files exist), fio, an NVMe SSD at <BDF> and its upstream port <UP> (find it with lspci -tv or readlink /sys/bus/pci/devices/<BDF>/..).

for d in <BDF> <UP>; do echo "== $d"; cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/$d/aer_dev_correctable; done > before.txt
fio --name=r --filename=/dev/nvme0n1 --rw=read --bs=128k --iodepth=32 --direct=1 \
    --runtime=600 --time_based --readonly
for d in <BDF> <UP>; do echo "== $d"; cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/$d/aer_dev_correctable; done > after.txt
diff before.txt after.txt

Observations: which counters moved, and on which device. Repeat with a write workload. (Note: a write workload changes data; use a scratch namespace.)

Solution

Reads move data EP→RC, so a bad SSD transmitter shows up on <UP>. Writes move data RC→EP, so a bad host transmitter shows up on the SSD. Acks and flow-control DLLPs travel the other way, so BadDLLP is seen on the opposite side from BadTLP in each case.

Extension: repeat at 40 °C and 70 °C drive temperature and plot the rate against temperature.

22 Uncorrectable AER Errors: Lost, Refused and Malformed Transactions

← 21 Correctable AER Errors: The Link Is Telling You Something23 Link Failures That Are Not AER Errors →

22.1 How to read this chapter

Each uncorrectable bit means that at least one transaction did not complete as intended. In NVMe terms, that transaction can be any of four things:

  • Host → SSD MMIO. Doorbell writes (posted MWr) and register reads (MRd, which needs a completion). A lost doorbell write is silent. A failed register read returns all-ones to the CPU, which Linux treats as "device gone" (CSTS == ~0).
  • SSD → host DMA reads. The controller fetches SQ entries, PRP lists, SGL segments and write data with MRd and waits for CplD. A missing CplD gives a Completion Timeout in the SSD. The NVMe command is then usually aborted by the device or hangs until the host times it out.
  • SSD → host DMA writes. Read data, CQ entries and MSI-X messages are posted MWr, so the SSD never learns whether they arrived. If the root complex or IOMMU refuses one, the error is logged at the root, and the SSD believes it completed the command.
  • Configuration requests at enumeration and recovery time.

The default severities below follow section 20.8. Always check the actual Severity register.

L-09Undefined (bit 0) set — legacy "Training Error" bit ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
Bit 0 of Uncorrectable Status was "Training Error" in early PCIe revisions. It is now undefined. A device that sets it is either very old or buggy.
How it shows up
[ 0] Undefined in the AER log (PCI_ERR_UNC_UND).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Uncorrectable Error Status bit 0 is Undefined in current revisions.
Typical causes
Legacy IP, or firmware that writes the wrong bit.
How to detect / prove
Read UESta with lspci -vvv. Report it to the vendor as a compliance issue.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: aer_uncorrectable_error_string[0] = "Undefined".
L-10Data Link Protocol Error (DLP) ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
The Data Link Layer received an Ack or Nak whose sequence number does not match any unacknowledged TLP, or another DL-protocol violation. The replay state can no longer be trusted. The default severity is Fatal.
How it shows up
[ 4] DLP, severity=Uncorrectable (Fatal), type=Data Link Layer. Linux v7.2 calls pcie_do_recovery(..., pci_channel_io_frozen, aer_root_reset), which brings the link down and up again. The NVMe driver logs frozen state error detected, reset controller then restart after slot reset, and in-flight I/O is retried or failed.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Data Link Protocol Error, AckNak_Seq_Num checks.
Typical causes
Data Link Layer bugs in the port or SSD IP. Retimer bugs. Interaction with link power-management entry and exit.
How to detect / prove
The AER log and the counter aer_dev_fatal DLP. An analyzer capture with a trigger on "Ack/Nak sequence out of range".
In a trace
An Ack DLLP acknowledging a sequence number the transmitter never sent, or one that is already acknowledged.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_DATA_LINK_LAYER_ERROR_MASK (DLP); err.c: pcie_do_recovery; nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_error_detected, nvme_slot_reset, nvme_error_resume.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser or interposer that injects bad AckNak_Seq_Num values. Software injection: aer-inject with CONFIG_PCIEAER_INJECT exercises the recovery path only, not the hardware.
L-11Surprise Down Error (SDES) ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
A Downstream Port (Root Port or switch DSP) that supports Surprise Down reporting sees its link go from DL_Active to DL_Inactive without being told to. To the host, the SSD has vanished. Every MMIO read returns all-ones, posted writes are dropped, and DMA in flight is lost.
How it shows up
Uncorrectable bit 5 on the Downstream Port, not on the SSD. Default severity Fatal. Linux prints [ 5] SDES as "Transaction Layer" (see 20.4). If DPC is enabled and the port is a hot-plug bridge, dpc_is_surprise_removal() treats it as an expected removal and quietly cleans up (dpc_handle_surprise_removal()). Otherwise DPC or AER recovery runs. The NVMe driver then sees CSTS == ~0 on its next register read or in nvme_timeout(), and logs controller is down; will reset: CSTS=0xffffffff, PCI_STATUS=0xffff or PCI_STATUS read failed, followed by the hint lines about power saving (C-01).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Surprise Down Error, Data Link Layer Link Active reporting, Hot-Plug surprise.
Typical causes
Physical removal. Power loss to the slot or a brown-out. An SSD firmware crash or watchdog reset that drops the link. An ASPM L1 or L1-substate exit failure (see L-37). Thermal shutdown. A loose M.2 or U.2 connector.
How to detect / prove
On the port: lspci -vvv -s <port> shows UESta: SDES+ and LnkSta DLActive-. On the SSD, config reads return ff. Afterwards, check the drive's nvme smart-log "unsafe shutdowns" counter: if it went up by one, the drive lost power or reset without a shutdown notification.
In a trace
Traffic stops abruptly. One side goes to electrical idle with no PM_Enter DLLPs and no Hot Reset or Disable training sets first. Then Detect/Polling attempts follow if the device is still powered.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pcie/dpc.c: dpc_is_surprise_removal, dpc_handle_surprise_removal, pci_clear_surpdn_errors; nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_should_reset, nvme_warn_reset, nvme_timeout (checks pci_dev_is_disconnected), nvme_remove (checks pci_device_is_present).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: surprise-remove a hot-plug-capable U.2/E3.S drive in a hot-plug bay under I/O. EXPERT: cut slot power with a controllable riser or power interposer. Never do this on a non-hot-plug M.2 slot.
L-12Poisoned TLP received ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
A TLP arrives with the EP (Error Poisoned) bit set. Its data payload is known to be bad. Most often the host memory controller found an uncorrectable ECC error while serving the SSD's DMA read of write data, and returned the CplD poisoned. The opposite case is the SSD sending poisoned read data because of an internal uncorrectable error.
How it shows up
Uncorrectable bit 12 at the receiver, default Non-fatal, with the TLP header logged. Linux prints [12] TLP and a TLP Header: line. Recovery runs with pci_channel_io_normal, and nvme_error_detected() returns CAN_RECOVER (no reset). What happens to the NVMe command is up to the controller. A well-behaved SSD fails the write with a data-transfer or internal-error status and does not commit poisoned data impl. A poor one may write it. When the host receives poisoned read data, platform RAS policy decides the outcome (machine check, data dropped, or poison propagated in memory) impl.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Data Poisoning, EP bit, Poisoned TLP Received Status. NVMe Base Specification: generic status codes such as Data Transfer Error and Internal Error are what a controller can return. Check which one your device uses.
Typical causes
Host DRAM uncorrectable errors. Errors in the SSD's internal SRAM or DRAM on the read path. Switch internal parity errors (some switches poison rather than drop).
How to detect / prove
The AER log with the header (decode it: a CplD toward the SSD means host memory; an MWr toward the host means SSD data). Correlate with EDAC or MCE logs (ras-mc-ctl --errors, journalctl -k | grep -i mce). Read back the affected LBA range and check it.
In a trace
A TLP with the EP bit = 1. Analyzers highlight it. The NVMe CQE that follows may or may not carry an error status. Check whether it does.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_LOG_TLP_MASKS includes PCI_ERR_UNC_POISON_TLP; pci_aer_handle_error → pcie_do_recovery(..., pci_channel_io_normal, ...); dpc.c reports whether DPC can trigger on poisoned TLPs ("PoisonedTLP" in the capability line).
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that sends CplD with EP=1 to the SSD's MRd, or an analyzer with injection. On servers, ACPI EINJ memory error injection into a buffer the SSD then DMA-reads (CAUTION: may cause machine checks).
L-13Flow Control Protocol Error (FCP) ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The flow-control credit protocol was violated, for example InitFC advertising illegal values or credits that overflow the counter field. Credit accounting is corrupt. The default severity is Fatal, and the link is reset.
How it shows up
[13] FCP, Fatal, frozen-channel recovery, NVMe controller reset (messages as in L-10).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Flow Control, Flow Control Protocol Error.
Typical causes
Port IP bugs. Very rare in shipping products. More likely on FPGA-based NVMe prototypes.
How to detect / prove
Analyzer capture of InitFC and UpdateFC DLLPs around link-up or the event.
In a trace
InitFC1/InitFC2 with illegal values, or UpdateFC that moves the credit limit backwards beyond the allowed range.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "FCP".
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that injects bad UpdateFC.
L-14Completion Timeout — a read request was never answered Timer onlyPCIe Transaction
What happens
A requester sent a non-posted request (MRd, CfgRd, ...) and no completion arrived within its Completion Timeout range. In NVMe there are two cases. (a) The SSD's DMA read of an SQE, PRP or SGL list, or write data is never answered by the host: an IOMMU silently dropping it, a root complex deadlock, or a switch problem. The SSD logs CmpltTO and usually ends the command with an error status, or hangs. (b) The host CPU's MMIO read of an SSD register is not answered because the controller's PCIe front end is hung. The root port logs CmpltTO, and the CPU receives all-ones (or, on some platforms, a machine check impl).
How it shows up
Uncorrectable bit 14 on the requester, default Non-fatal, agent "Requester ID". A header is logged only if the device advertises PCI_ERR_CAP_COMP_TIME_LOG. Downstream NVMe effects: case (a) gives a CQE error or an NVMe I/O timeout (I/O tag N (cid) opcode 0x.. (...) QID n timeout, aborting ...). Case (b) gives CSTS=0xffffffff → controller is down; will reset.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Completion Timeout mechanism, Device Control 2 "Completion Timeout Value" and "Completion Timeout Disable", Device Capabilities 2 "Completion Timeout Ranges Supported". The timeout value is configurable. A 50 µs–50 ms default range is commonly cited (verify in spec).
Typical causes
IOMMU faults where the platform drops the request (see L-36). DPC or containment upstream. Host memory power states that stall. P2P targets that never answer. A hung SSD front end.
How to detect / prove
See which device logged it: the SSD (case a) or the Root Port (case b). lspci -vvv DevCtl2 shows "Completion Timeout: 50us to 50ms" or similar, and whether it is disabled. Correlate with IOMMU fault logs and the NVMe timeout messages.
In a trace
An MRd from the SSD with a tag, and no CplD with that tag within the timeout. Later, possibly a late completion that triggers L-16 Unexpected Completion.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_AGENT_REQUESTER_MASK (CmpltTO, UR), tlp_header_logged; nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_timeout, nvme_warn_reset.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser acting as host memory that withholds CplD for chosen tags. CAUTION: shorten the SSD's Completion Timeout Value through Device Control 2 with setpci on a test system to make marginal latency visible.
L-15Completer Abort (CA) ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The completer understood the request but refused it because of an internal rule: an access violation, an unsupported access size to a register, or a device in a state where it cannot serve it. It returns a completion with status CA and logs bit 15. The requester sees an "unsuccessful completion".
How it shows up
[15] CmpltAbrt, agent "Completer ID", header logged, default Non-fatal. If the SSD aborts a host MMIO read, the CPU usually sees all-ones data. If the root complex aborts an SSD DMA read, the SSD's command fails.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Completion Status CA, Completer Abort Status.
Typical causes
Host side: MMIO accesses that cross register boundaries or use unsupported sizes (for example, 64-bit reads of registers some controllers accept only as 32-bit; Linux uses lo_hi_readq for CAP for this reason). Root complex: some platforms use CA for IOMMU-blocked requests impl.
How to detect / prove
Decode the logged header: address and requester. If the address is inside the SSD's BAR0, look at the access size and offset.
In a trace
A Cpl (no data) with Completion Status = CA, matching the tag of the request.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_AGENT_COMPLETER_MASK; nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_pci_reg_read64 uses lo_hi_readq.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: issue unusual-size MMIO reads to reserved BAR offsets from a test tool, on a disposable system only.
L-16Unexpected Completion — completion for a request nobody is waiting for ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
A completion arrives whose Requester ID and Tag do not match any outstanding request. The usual case is a completion that arrives after the requester already gave up (Completion Timeout). Another is a completion routed to the wrong function. The receiver drops it.
How it shows up
[16] UnxCmplt, header logged, default Non-fatal. Often seen right after an L-14 on the same device. Can be handled as advisory (L-06).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Unexpected Completion Status, completion matching rules.
Typical causes
A late completion from slow host memory or a switch. Tag reuse bugs, especially with 10-bit tags when one side does not support them properly. A completer returning the wrong Requester ID (for example a function-alias DMA bug; see the Marvell quirk in Chapter 27).
How to detect / prove
Decode the header's Tag and Requester ID. Check whether 10-Bit Tag Requester Enable is set (lspci -vvv DevCtl2 "10BitTagReq") and whether every hop supports it.
In a trace
A CplD with a tag that has no matching MRd, or whose MRd was older than the timeout.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "UnxCmplt"; probe.c "enabling Extended Tags" and "disabling Extended Tags" messages.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that delays a completion past the SSD's completion timeout.
L-17Receiver Overflow (RxOF) ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The receiver got more TLPs or data than the credits it advertised, so its buffer overflowed. Data is lost and credit state is broken. Default Fatal.
How it shows up
[17] RxOF, Fatal, link reset, NVMe controller reset.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Receiver Overflow, flow-control rules (optional checking).
Typical causes
Transmitter credit-accounting bugs, often after a link Recovery or power-state transition.
How to detect / prove
Analyzer: compare the credits advertised with the TLPs sent.
In a trace
The transmitter sends a TLP whose header or data credits exceed the last UpdateFC limit.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: string "RxOF".
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that ignores credits.
L-18Malformed TLP ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
A TLP breaks format rules. Examples: payload longer than the receiver's Max_Payload_Size, Length field not matching the actual payload, a request crossing a 4 KB boundary, illegal byte enables, an undefined Type, or TC/VC mapping violations. Default Fatal.
How it shows up
[18] MalfTLP with the header logged, Fatal, frozen recovery, NVMe reset. If it repeats at every large transfer, Linux keeps resetting (a reset loop, C-06).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: TLP formation rules, Malformed TLP, Max_Payload_Size rules.
Typical causes
MPS mismatch is the classic case: the SSD is configured for 512 B and sends 512-byte MWr to a Root Port set to 256 B (L-32). DMA engine bugs that cross 4 KB with an unaligned PRP offset. Firmware bugs in CQE or MSI-X write formation.
How to detect / prove
Decode the logged header. Compare its Length with the MPS of the receiving device (lspci -vvv DevCtl "MaxPayload"). Check that the whole path has a consistent MPS.
In a trace
The analyzer flags the TLP as malformed (oversize, 4 KB crossing, length mismatch).
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_LOG_TLP_MASKS includes MALF_TLP; probe.c: MPS configuration (pcie_bus_configure_settings path; messages "Max Payload Size set to ...", "can't set Max Payload Size to ...").
Test to provoke it
CAUTION/EXPERT: on a test system, set the Root Port's MPS lower than the SSD's with setpci, then run large reads. This deliberately creates a fatal error, so use a disposable system with no valuable data.
L-19ECRC Error — end-to-end CRC mismatch ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
When ECRC generation and checking are enabled, the receiver found the 32-bit end-to-end CRC (TLP Digest) wrong. LCRC protects each hop, but a TLP can be corrupted inside a switch or retimer buffer between hops. ECRC catches that.
How it shows up
[19] ECRC with the header, default Non-fatal. Without ECRC enabled, the same corruption is silent: the switch regenerates a valid LCRC around corrupted data.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: ECRC, TLP Digest (TD bit), AER ECRC Generation/Check Capable/Enable.
Typical causes
Switch or retimer internal memory errors.
How to detect / prove
Check whether ECRC is on at all. lspci -vvv AERCap shows "GenCap+ CGenEn- ChkCap+ ChkEn-". Linux v7.2 has an ecrc= boot policy, "bios" (default), "off" or "on" (ecrc_policy_str; pcie_set_ecrc_checking), passed as pci=ecrc=on.
In a trace
A TLP with TD=1 whose digest does not match. Analyzers can verify the ECRC independently.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: enable_ecrc_checking, disable_ecrc_checking, pcie_set_ecrc_checking, pcie_ecrc_get_policy.
Test to provoke it
SAFE to enable (pci=ecrc=on) on a test system. EXPERT: inject digest errors with an exerciser.
L-20Unsupported Request (UR) ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The completer does not support the request at all: an address no BAR or window claims, a disabled Memory Space Enable, a message type it does not know, or a config request to a function that does not exist. Non-posted requests get a Cpl with UR status. Posted ones (DMA writes, doorbells) are dropped.
How it shows up
[20] UnsupReq, agent "Requester ID", header logged, default Non-fatal. The kernel documentation example is exactly this line: severity=Uncorrectable (Fatal), type=Transaction Layer, (Requester ID) ... [20] UnsupReq (First), on a platform where severity was set to fatal. NVMe consequences: an SSD DMA write of read data or a CQE to an unmapped host address is dropped, and the command "completes" from the SSD's view while the host never sees the CQE, so it times out. A doorbell write while Memory Space is disabled is dropped silently.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Unsupported Request, completion status UR, address decoding.
Typical causes
DMA to a stale or unmapped IOVA when the IOMMU is in a mode that returns UR impl. PRP entries pointing at garbage (a host driver bug or a corrupted PRP list). MMIO after the BAR was moved or disabled by hot-plug or reset. Enumeration probing absent functions (expected, normally masked or advisory).
How to detect / prove
Decode the header address. Look for a matching IOMMU fault (L-36). Check lspci -vvv Control: "Mem+ BusMaster+" on the SSD.
In a trace
An SSD MWr to an address outside any host memory region, followed by an ERR_NONFATAL from the root. For non-posted requests, a Cpl with status UR.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: AER_AGENT_REQUESTER_MASK; Documentation/PCI/pcieaer-howto.rst example output.
Test to provoke it
SAFE-ish: setpci read of a non-existent function (normally masked). EXPERT: a deliberately wrong PRP in a user-space NVMe test driver (SPDK/vfio) with the IOMMU on. The IOMMU should block it, which proves the protection works.
L-21ACS Violation ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
A switch Downstream Port or root port with Access Control Services (ACS) enabled blocked a TLP that violated its policy. Typically this is peer-to-peer traffic (SSD → GPU or NIC BAR) that ACS Source Validation, P2P Request Redirect or Egress Control forbids, or a TLP with a spoofed Requester ID. See also the configuration-level card L-33.
How it shows up
[21] ACSViol with the header, logged at the port, default Non-fatal. The P2P DMA fails, and the NVMe command using a P2P buffer times out or returns an error.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Access Control Services, ACS Violation Status.
Typical causes
P2PDMA (NVMe CMB as a target, GPUDirect Storage) through a switch with ACS redirect enabled for IOMMU isolation. VFs using the wrong Requester ID.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv ACSCtl on each port. Linux logs Configured ACS to %#06x (pci_std_enable_acs path), and the pci=config_acs= parameter changes it.
In a trace
The P2P MWr or MRd enters the switch and never leaves toward the peer. The switch sends an error message upstream.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pci.c: pci_enable_acs, pci_acs_enabled, pci_acs_path_enabled, messages "Invalid ACS flags specified", "Configured ACS to %#06x".
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: attempt P2PDMA between two devices under one switch with ACS P2P redirect on.
L-22Uncorrectable Internal Error ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
The function detected an internal error it could not correct: a parity or ECC error in internal SRAM or an internal bus fault inside the PCIe or NVMe front end. Default Fatal, but masked by default, so most systems never see it as AER. The NVMe controller may instead set CSTS.CFS (C-01) or post a Persistent Internal Error asynchronous event (C-11).
How it shows up
If unmasked: [22] UncorrIntErr, Fatal recovery. If masked: nothing in AER. Look at the NVMe-level symptoms.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Uncorrectable Internal Error Status/Mask/Severity (optional).
Typical causes
SRAM soft errors, silicon defects, overheating.
How to detect / prove
Check UEMsk in lspci -vvv. For a lab, unmask and watch (CAUTION). Correlate with NVMe telemetry and the error log.
In a trace
Only an ERR_FATAL message, if unmasked. The device may stop responding afterwards.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: pci_aer_unmask_internal_errors (CXL only), string "UncorrIntErr".
Test to provoke it
Vendor-specific injection only.
L-23Egress-blocked family: MC Blocked, AtomicOp Egress Blocked, TLP Prefix Blocked, Poisoned TLP Egress Blocked, DMWr Request Egress Blocked, TLP Translation Egress Blocked ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
A port refused to forward a TLP out of an egress port because the next hop cannot accept that TLP type, or because policy says to block it. Bit 23 is a multicast TLP blocked. Bit 24 is an AtomicOp toward a port without AtomicOp routing or support. Bit 25 is an End-End TLP Prefix (such as PASID in some forms) toward a port that does not support prefixes. Bit 26 is a poisoned TLP blocked by policy. Bit 27 is a Deferrable Memory Write blocked. Bit 31 is translation-related egress blocking.
How it shows up
Bits 23–27 and 31, all in AER_LOG_TLP_MASKS (header logged). Linux strings: "BlockedTLP", "AtomicOpBlocked", "TLPBlockedErr", "PoisonTLPBlocked", "DMWrReqBlocked", "TLPXlatBlocked". The originator's request fails, usually as a Completion Timeout or UR from its point of view.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: AtomicOp routing/egress blocking, End-End TLP Prefix rules, Poisoned TLP Egress Blocking, Deferrable Memory Write. Default severity and mask values for bits 26, 27 and 31: verify in spec uncertain.
Typical causes
Rare for plain NVMe I/O. Possible with PASID or SVA-capable NVMe controllers, computational storage using AtomicOps toward host memory, and poisoned-TLP containment policies on server root ports.
How to detect / prove
Decode the logged header (prefix present? Fmt/Type = AtomicOp?). Check DevCap2 AtomicOpsCap and AtomicOp routing on each port, and End-End TLP Prefix support.
In a trace
The TLP enters a switch or root port and never leaves on the egress side, followed by an error message.
Linux v7.2 source
pci_regs.h: PCI_ERR_UNC_MCBTLP, _ATOMEG, _TLPPRE, _POISON_BLK, _DMWR_BLK, _XLAT_BLK; aer.c: strings and AER_LOG_TLP_MASKS.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser sending AtomicOps or prefixed TLPs through a port without support.
L-24IDE family: IDE Check Failed, Misrouted IDE TLP, PCRC Check Failed ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
With PCIe Integrity and Data Encryption (IDE) active, used for confidential computing and TDISP-style device assignment, a received TLP failed its integrity (MAC) check (bit 28), arrived on the wrong IDE stream or port (bit 29), or failed the optional plaintext CRC (PCRC) check (bit 30). IDE treats integrity failure as a security event. The spec defines how the IDE stream is affected (for example, the stream is torn down and must be re-keyed) uncertain — verify in spec.
How it shows up
"IDECheck", "MisIDETLP", "PCRC_CHECK", with the header logged. The NVMe device's DMA stops working until the stream is re-established.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: IDE (introduced in 6.0-era ECNs). Severity defaults: verify in spec.
Typical causes
Key-programming mismatches, stream ID misconfiguration, real tampering, or (more likely) firmware bugs in early IDE implementations.
How to detect / prove
The AER log plus the platform security or TSM logs. IDE-capable analyzers need the keys to decode traffic.
In a trace
Encrypted TLPs. Without keys only the headers are visible.
Linux v7.2 source
pci_regs.h: PCI_ERR_UNC_IDE_CHECK, _MISR_IDE, _PCRC_CHECK; aer.c strings.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: program mismatched keys on a test setup.
📌 Remember

Posted writes fail silently for the sender. When an SSD's MWr carrying a CQE or read data is dropped as UR or by the IOMMU, the SSD considers the command complete and the host waits forever. The only evidence is an error logged at the root complex (AER on the Root Port or an IOMMU fault), followed 30 seconds later by the NVMe timeout. Always check the root-side logs for an NVMe timeout that has no device-side explanation.

Quiz

  1. MalfTLP appears on the Root Port every time a large sequential read starts. What is the first register you compare?

    Answer

    Max_Payload_Size in Device Control on the SSD and on the Root Port (and any switch between them). The SSD is sending read data in MWr TLPs larger than the Root Port's MPS.

  2. Why can Completion Timeout be "timer only" even though AER reports it?

    Answer

    Nothing reported the missing completion. The requester infers the failure only because its own timer expired. AER records that inference, but the root cause (who dropped the request) has to be found elsewhere.

23 Link Failures That Are Not AER Errors

← 22 Uncorrectable AER Errors: Lost, Refused and Malformed Transactions24 Fabric Configuration, Interrupt and DMA-Translation Failures →

23.1 Why this chapter exists

The most common PCIe "failure" in the field sets no error bit at all. A link trains at Gen3 instead of Gen4, or at x2 instead of x4, and the SSD runs at half speed for years. Other failures are visible only as side effects: a link retraining hundreds of times a second, a device that disappeared, a Root Port that cut the link off on purpose (DPC), or a device that answers "retry" forever during enumeration. This chapter covers the link, enumeration and containment failures. Chapter 24 covers configuration, interrupt and DMA-translation failures.

23.2 Reading link state correctly

Three registers in the PCI Express Capability matter. Link Capabilities (LnkCap) holds the maximum speed and width this port supports. Link Status (LnkSta) holds the current negotiated speed and width, plus the DL_Active, Link Training, LBMS and LABS bits. Link Control 2 (LnkCtl2) holds the Target Link Speed, which can be lower than LnkCap. The negotiated link is limited by the lesser of the two ends and by every hop. Linux v7.2 computes this in pcie_bandwidth_available(), walking up from the device to find the slowest link on the path to the root.

lspci -vvv -s 0000:03:00.0 | grep -E 'LnkCap:|LnkSta:|LnkCtl2:'
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/max_link_speed      # from LnkCap
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/current_link_speed  # from LnkSta
