cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/max_link_width
cat /sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:03:00.0/current_link_width
# ...and the same for every upstream port up to the Root Port
⚠️ Power management can make LnkSta lie

Some devices drop to a lower speed when idle (autonomous speed change, or a platform policy) and go back up under load. Read LnkSta while a workload is running before you declare a link downtrained. Also note that LnkSta "downgraded" annotations in newer lspci versions only compare against LnkCap of the same device.

L-25Link trained at lower speed or narrower width than capable DegradedPCIe Physical
What happens
The LTSSM settled on a lower data rate (for example 8 GT/s instead of 16 GT/s) or fewer lanes (x2 instead of x4). Everything works and nothing is logged as an error, but sequential bandwidth is capped. A Gen4 x4 link carries roughly 7–7.5 GB/s of payload, while Gen3 x2 manages under 2 GB/s.
How it shows up
No AER bit and no NVMe status. Linux v7.2 prints an informational line once at enumeration, from pcie_report_downtraining() → __pcie_print_link_status(dev, false), only when available bandwidth is less than capable: "%u.%03u Gb/s available PCIe bandwidth, limited by %s x%d link at %s (capable of %u.%03u Gb/s with %s x%d link)". The at %s part names the limiting device, which may be an upstream switch port or Root Port and not the SSD. A downgrade after boot (retraining at runtime) does not print this line. bwctrl updates the cached speed in sysfs when the port supports Link Bandwidth Notification.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Link Capabilities (Max Link Speed, Maximum Link Width), Link Status (Current Link Speed, Negotiated Link Width), Link Control 2 (Target Link Speed), LTSSM Configuration and Recovery (speed change, equalization).
Typical causes
Width: a damaged lane or connector pin; M.2 slots wired x2 on the board; bifurcation set wrong in BIOS; a riser that carries only some lanes. Speed: equalization failure at Gen3+ that falls back to a lower rate; BIOS "Link Speed" set to GenN; a retimer or redriver not rated for the rate; a slot sharing lanes through a slower switch; Linux or firmware lowered Target Link Speed (a thermal cooling device through bwctrl, or the 2.5 GT/s retrain quirk, L-39).
How to detect / prove
Compare LnkCap with LnkSta at each hop under load. dmesg | grep 'limited by'. Prove the performance impact with fio sequential read at high queue depth (bandwidth close to the lower link's ceiling). Re-seat the drive or swap slots. Check LnkCtl2 Target Link Speed. Check BIOS settings.
In a trace
During training, TS1/TS2 ordered sets advertise the supported rates. Look for the speed-change attempt (Recovery.Speed) failing and reverting, or lanes that never leave Detect or are reported as PAD in Configuration (width).
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pci.c: __pcie_print_link_status, pcie_print_link_status (verbose variant, exported for drivers), pcie_bandwidth_available, pcie_bandwidth_capable; drivers/pci/probe.c: pcie_report_downtraining (only for Endpoints, Legacy Endpoints and Upstream Ports, function 0, not VFs); drivers/pci/pcie/bwctrl.c: pcie_bwnotif_irq, pcie_set_target_speed.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: limit speed in BIOS, then confirm the log line appears and fio bandwidth drops. CAUTION: write LnkCtl2 Target Link Speed with setpci on the Root Port and set Retrain Link. This changes the live link and may drop it.
Synthetic example — the "limited by" message for a Gen4 x4 SSD behind a Gen3 switch
DeviceLnkCapLnkStaNote
Root Port 0000:00:01.116 GT/s x168 GT/s x16link to switch
Switch USP 0000:01:00.08 GT/s x168 GT/s x16switch is Gen3
Switch DSP 0000:02:04.08 GT/s x48 GT/s x4limiting link
SSD 0000:03:00.016 GT/s x48 GT/s x4prints: 31.504 Gb/s available PCIe bandwidth, limited by 8.0 GT/s PCIe x4 link at 0000:02:04.0 (capable of 63.012 Gb/s with 16.0 GT/s PCIe x4 link)

The bandwidth values in this example come from the kernel's per-lane calculation for 128b/130b encoding (8 GT/s × 128/130 ≈ 7.877 Gb/s per lane). The exact string format is __pcie_print_link_status's. The device numbers are invented synthetic.

L-26Link flapping / recovery storm — only correctable errors, endless retraining DegradedPCIe Physical/Data Link
What happens
The link keeps entering LTSSM Recovery: after Replay Rollover, after receiver errors, or after an electrical-idle glitch. Each retrain stalls all traffic for microseconds to milliseconds. Data is never lost, so no uncorrectable error appears. The only symptoms are correctable counts (RxErr, BadTLP, BadDLLP, Rollover, Timeout), periodic latency spikes, and sometimes a speed or width that changes over time.
How it shows up
Rate-limited severity=Correctable lines (10 per 5 s per device by default, so the log hides the true rate; the aer_dev_correctable counters show it). LnkSta LBMS (Link Bandwidth Management Status) set by hardware when it changes speed or width "to correct unreliable link operation". Linux bwctrl records it (PCI_LINK_LBMS_SEEN). The NVMe layer shows no errors, only p99.9 latency spikes and lower throughput.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Recovery state, Link Bandwidth Management Status / Link Autonomous Bandwidth Status, Link Bandwidth Management Interrupt Enable.
Typical causes
Marginal signal integrity near the eye limit (temperature dependent). Spread-spectrum clocking mismatch. Failing retimers. ASPM exit problems (L-37).
How to detect / prove
Sample aer_dev_correctable on both ends every second and plot the deltas. Sample LnkSta. Run fio --percentile_list=99:99.9:99.99 latency. A storm shows as all queues stalling together. An analyzer with a trigger on TS1 ordered sets in L0 shows each Recovery entry.
In a trace
Repeated TS1/TS2 bursts between TLPs, each preceded by Naks or replays. Note the Recovery count per second.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: aer_ratelimit, pci_dev_aer_stats_incr; bwctrl.c: pcie_bwnotif_irq, pcie_reset_lbms; Documentation/PCI/pcieaer-howto.rst "AER Ratelimits".
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: thermal cycling of the riser or SSD under load. EXPERT: lane-margining at an offset that produces occasional errors.
L-27Surprise link down without Surprise Down reporting Timer onlyPCIe Data Link
What happens
The link drops (DL_Active goes to 0), but the Downstream Port does not support Surprise Down error reporting, or the error is masked, or there is no hot-plug or DPC service on that port. Nothing is signalled. The host finds out the next time it touches the device: MMIO reads return all-ones (after a Completion Timeout at the Root Port, if that is logged), doorbell writes vanish, and every outstanding NVMe command waits for its timer.
How it shows up
Up to io_timeout (30 s default in Linux v7.2) or admin_timeout (60 s) later: nvme_timeout() reads CSTS = 0xffffffff, nvme_should_reset() decides it must reset (CFS bit appears set in all-ones), and the driver logs controller is down; will reset: CSTS=0xffffffff, PCI_STATUS=0xffff plus Does your device have a faulty power saving mode enabled? and Try "nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us=0 pcie_aspm=off pcie_port_pm=off" and report a bug. The reset then fails (nvme_pci_enable sees CSTS == -1), and Disabling device after reset failure: -19 follows (C-17).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Data Link Layer Link Active Reporting Capable, Surprise Down Error Reporting Capable (both optional); a read to a non-responding device ends in a Completion Timeout at the requester.
Typical causes
SSD firmware hang or crash that takes down the PHY. Power-state exit failures (APST, L1.2). Power delivery dips. Connector problems.
How to detect / prove
Read the port's LnkSta (DLActive-) and LnkCap ("Surprise-" / "DLActive-" capability flags). Cross-check the time of the first NVMe timeout with the kernel timestamps of earlier traffic. An analyzer left armed on electrical idle catches the moment.
In a trace
Normal traffic, then sudden electrical idle on the SSD's TX lanes with no PM DLLPs first. Host MRd TLPs from the Root Port after that point get no completion.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_timeout, nvme_should_reset, nvme_warn_reset, nvme_pci_enable, nvme_reset_work; core.c: admin_timeout, nvme_io_timeout.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: cut SSD power with an interposer on a slot without Surprise Down reporting; or force a firmware crash with a vendor debug command.
L-28DPC containment event — the port cut the link on purpose ReportedPCIe Data Link
What happens
A Downstream Port with Downstream Port Containment (DPC) enabled saw an uncorrectable error. Linux v7.2 configures DPC to trigger only on ERR_FATAL, as the dpc_handler comment says. The port immediately disables its link, so no possibly-corrupt TLPs travel further upstream, and it interrupts the OS. Every request to the SSD fails until software clears the trigger and the link retrains.
How it shows up
dpc_process_error() logs one of: containment event, status:%#06x: unmasked uncorrectable error detected (the port's own error, followed by the AER details); containment event, status:%#06x, ERR_FATAL received from DDDD:BB:DD.F (an error message from the SSD); or containment event, status:%#06x: RP PIO error detected / software trigger detected. With RP extensions, RP PIO details follow, for example [18] Memory Request Completion Timeout. Then pcie_do_recovery(pdev, pci_channel_io_frozen, dpc_reset_link): the NVMe driver logs frozen state error detected, reset controller then restart after slot reset. If the link does not come back: Data Link Layer Link Active not cleared in 1000 msec, root port still busy, or device recovery failed.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Downstream Port Containment, DPC Status Trigger Reason, RP PIO extensions.
Typical causes
Any fatal error from the SSD (L-10, L-13, L-17, L-18, L-11). Completion Timeouts of host MMIO reads when RP PIO is enabled. Software trigger by firmware (EDR).
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -E 'DPC|containment'. lspci -vvv -s <port> shows the DPC capability and DpcSta. On probe, Linux prints enabled with IRQ and an error containment capabilities: Int Msg #%d, RPExt%c PoisonedTLP%c SwTrigger%c RP PIO Log %d, DL_ActiveErr%c line, which tells you what is enabled.
In a trace
The port transmits electrical idle and the link goes down with no Hot Reset. After software clears DPC, a fresh Detect → Polling → Configuration → L0 sequence follows.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pcie/dpc.c: dpc_irq, dpc_handler, dpc_process_error, dpc_process_rp_pio_error, dpc_reset_link, dpc_wait_rp_inactive, pci_dpc_recovered; err.c: pcie_do_recovery.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: software trigger via DPC Control (setpci) on a test port. EXPERT: inject a fatal error with an exerciser. The effect is a full SSD reset, and data in flight is lost.
L-29Hot-plug removal under I/O (surprise or orderly) ReportedPCIe / host
What happens
The SSD is removed or powered off in a hot-plug slot. In an orderly removal (attention button or echo 0 > /sys/bus/pci/slots/N/power) the OS unbinds the driver first. In a surprise removal the device is already gone when the driver is told. The PCI core marks it disconnected, and pending I/O must fail rather than hang.
How it shows up
The hot-plug driver (pciehp, not in the local source tree used here) logs slot link-down and presence-change events uncertain wording. Linux v7.2 NVMe: nvme_remove() sets the state to DELETING and, if !pci_device_is_present(), sets DEAD and calls nvme_dev_disable(dev, true) at once, so outstanding requests are cancelled and namespaces are marked dead (nvme_mark_namespaces_dead → blk_mark_disk_dead). Filesystems see I/O errors. In nvme_timeout(), if pci_dev_is_disconnected() is true, the controller goes straight to DELETING and is disabled. The source comment says this unblocks a deadlock between error recovery and removal.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Hot-Plug (Presence Detect, Data Link Layer State Changed, Attention Button); NVMe transport behaviour is up to the host.
Typical causes
Operator removal, drive failure that triggers presence-detect change, backplane faults.
How to detect / prove
dmesg around removal; lspci no longer lists the BDF; /sys/block/nvme* disappears. Check that no task is left in D state (ps -eo stat,pid,cmd | grep '^D'), which would mean an I/O path hang.
In a trace
Link down (electrical idle). For orderly removal, the host first stops traffic, writes CC.SHN, and waits for CSTS.SHST=10b.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_remove, nvme_timeout, nvme_dev_disable, nvme_pci_ctrl_is_dead; core.c: nvme_mark_namespaces_dead, nvme_remove_namespaces.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: orderly hot-remove through sysfs slot power with fio running on a scratch namespace, then check for hung tasks. EXPERT: physical surprise removal in a certified hot-plug bay.
L-30Configuration RRS (CRS) loop — device answers "retry" forever Timer onlyPCIe Transaction
What happens
After reset or power-up, a function may return Configuration Request Retry Status (RRS, formerly CRS) completions while it initializes. If its firmware never finishes booting, it keeps answering RRS. Enumeration or post-reset restore waits and then gives up. The device is missing from lspci, or present but unusable.
How it shows up
With Configuration RRS Software Visibility enabled on the Root Port (Linux enables it if supported: PCI_EXP_RTCAP_RRS_SV → PCI_EXP_RTCTL_RRS_SVE), a Vendor ID read returns a special value, which Linux recognises with pci_bus_rrs_vendor_id(). During enumeration, pci_bus_wait_rrs() doubles its delay each time and logs pci DDDD:BB:DD.F: not ready after %dms; waiting once past 1000 ms, then ready after %dms or not ready after %dms; giving up. After resets, pci_dev_wait() logs not ready %dms after %s; waiting / giving up (the timeout for reset waits is PCIE_RESET_READY_POLL_MS = 60000 ms). Past PCI_RESET_WAIT (1000 ms) it tries one link retrain through pcie_failed_link_retrain(). Without RRS visibility, the hardware retries silently and eventually returns all-ones.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Configuration Request Retry Status, Configuration RRS Software Visibility, the rule that a device must be ready for configuration requests after reset within the specified time (with Readiness Notifications as an optional speed-up).
Typical causes
SSD firmware stuck in its boot ROM (corrupt firmware image, failed update). Very slow boot after unsafe shutdown with a large mapping-table rebuild. Power sequencing problems.
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -E 'not ready|ready after'. A long "ready after" (several seconds) on every boot is a latent problem even when it works. Time the SSD's boot after power-on with an analyzer: count the RRS completions.
In a trace
CfgRd0 to Vendor ID, then Cpl with status RRS, repeated with growing gaps (Linux doubles the delay: 1, 2, 4, ... ms).
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/probe.c: pci_bus_wait_rrs, RRS SV enable around line 1304; drivers/pci/pci.c: pci_dev_wait, PCI_RESET_WAIT, PCIE_RESET_READY_POLL_MS; quirks.c: pcie_failed_link_retrain.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser as endpoint that answers RRS for N seconds; or a vendor command that holds the SSD in its boot loader.
L-31BAR not assigned / resource conflict — driver cannot map registers ReportedPCIe config / host
What happens
The SSD's BAR0 (registers and doorbells) has no address assigned, or has one that conflicts with another device or lies outside the upstream bridge window. The NVMe driver's probe fails, or MMIO goes to the wrong place.
How it shows up
Enumeration messages from probe.c such as %s: can't handle BAR larger than 4GB, %s: can't handle BAR above 4GB, initial BAR value %#010llx invalid, device has non-compliant BARs; disabling IO/MEM decoding, or devices behind bridge are unusable because %pR cannot be assigned for them. Resource-assignment failure messages come from setup-res.c, which is not in the local tree, so treat their wording as uncertain. NVMe probe then fails in nvme_dev_map() / pci_request_mem_regions or reads all-ones; nvme_pci_enable logs reading CSTS register failed at debug level only, and probe ends with probe failed (dev_err_probe).
Spec basis
PCI/PCIe configuration space: BARs, bridge memory windows. NVMe: BAR0/BAR1 hold the controller registers (the 64-bit MLBAR/MUBAR pair).
Typical causes
Too little 32-bit MMIO space with many devices. BIOS not reserving hot-plug windows. Thunderbolt or USB4 enclosures. SR-IOV VF BARs exhausting space. "Above 4G decoding" disabled.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vv -s <BDF> showing "Region 0: Memory at <unassigned>" or "[disabled]"; cat /proc/iomem; dmesg | grep -i 'BAR\|bridge window'. Try pci=realloc as a diagnostic boot option.
In a trace
Config writes to BARs during enumeration; later MMIO TLPs from the CPU are absent or go to a different device.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/probe.c: BAR sizing messages (lines ~264–320), "devices behind bridge are unusable..."; nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_dev_map, nvme_remap_bar, nvme_pci_enable.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: boot with many devices and BIOS "above 4G decoding" off, then inspect. CAUTION: hot-plug into a slot whose bridge window is too small.
L-39Link never reaches DL_Active — training fails or oscillates between speeds Timer onlyPCIe Physical
What happens
Training never completes. The LTSSM cycles between Detect, Polling, Configuration and Recovery, or keeps switching speed. The pcie_failed_link_retrain() comment in quirks.c describes a real case (ASMedia ASM2824 switches) where the link "continues switching between the two speeds indefinitely and the data link layer never reaches the active state, with link training reported repeatedly active ~84% of the time". The SSD never appears.
How it shows up
The SSD is not enumerated. Linux v7.2 may log broken device, retraining non-functional downstream link at 2.5GT/s, then removing 2.5GT/s downstream link speed restriction on success or retraining failed on failure. Also Data Link Layer Link Active not set in %d msec from pci_bridge_wait_for_secondary_bus() in pci.c (after pcie_wait_for_link_delay() fails).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: LTSSM, Data Link Layer Link Active, Link Bandwidth Management Status, Target Link Speed.
Typical causes
Speed-negotiation interoperability bugs. Bad equalization. A broken lane 0. Missing reference clock. PERST# timing.
How to detect / prove
Port LnkSta shows Link Training set or toggling, DLActive-, and LBMS+. The quirk uses exactly this: DLLLA clear plus LBMS seen. Try forcing 2.5 GT/s Target Link Speed to see whether the link comes up.
In a trace
Repeated TS1/TS2 at alternating rates. Never reaching L0, or reaching L0 briefly before Recovery.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/quirks.c: pcie_failed_link_retrain; bwctrl.c: pcie_set_target_speed; pci.c: pci_dev_wait.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that refuses speed change, or a degraded channel at the target rate.

24 Fabric Configuration, Interrupt and DMA-Translation Failures

← 23 Link Failures That Are Not AER Errors7.1 How a Protocol Checker Thinks →

24.1 Why this chapter exists

The failures in this chapter happen between the PCIe link and the NVMe controller logic. A payload-size setting or isolation policy can be inconsistent along the path. An interrupt can be routed to nowhere. A DMA address can be refused by the IOMMU. Or a power-saving link state may never wake up. On their own they rarely produce a clean error. Instead they cause timeouts, resets or silent loss of completions further up. Each card says which upstream symptom to look for.

L-32MPS / MRRS mismatch along the path ReportedPCIe config
What happens
Max_Payload_Size (MPS) must not exceed what each receiver on the path accepts. If the SSD's MPS is larger than the Root Port's or a switch's, its large MWr (read data to host) are Malformed at the receiver (L-18), which is fatal by default. Max_Read_Request_Size (MRRS) mismatches are legal but can hurt performance (too small) or fairness (too large). Some devices mishandle certain MRRS values, as the comment in pcie_write_mrrs() says: "issues with setting MRRS to 0 on a number of devices".
How it shows up
Fatal MalfTLP during the first large read, then a reset loop. Or, with consistent but small MPS/MRRS, a quiet bandwidth deficit (Degraded). Linux v7.2 messages: Max Payload Size set to %d (was %d, max %d), Upstream bridge's Max Payload Size set to %d ..., Max Payload Size %d, but upstream %s set to %d; if necessary, use "pci=pcie_bus_safe" and report a bug (with pci=pcie_bus_tune_off), can't set Max Payload Size to %d ..., Failed attempting to set the MPS, Failed attempting to set the MRRS, MRRS was unable to be configured with a safe value. If problems are experienced, try running with pci=pcie_bus_safe, and in non-default modes Max Payload Size set to %4d/%4d (was %4d), Max Read Rq %4d.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Device Capabilities Max_Payload_Size Supported, Device Control Max_Payload_Size and Max_Read_Request_Size, the rule that TLP payloads must not exceed the receiver's MPS.
Typical causes
Hot-plugged SSDs under a port configured for a different MPS. Firmware that programs the root ports differently from Linux's policy. P2P (pci=pcie_bus_peer2peer forces 128 B). Settings changed by user tools with setpci.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv | grep -E 'MaxPayload|MaxReadReq' on every device on the path. Each DevCtl line lists "MaxPayload N bytes, MaxReadReq M bytes". Each must be ≤ its partner's capability, and the payload size must be consistent across the path. Boot-option tests: pci=pcie_bus_safe, pcie_bus_perf, pcie_bus_tune_off, pcie_bus_peer2peer.
In a trace
MWr or CplD with a Length (DW) × 4 larger than the receiver's MPS. MRd sizes reflect the requester's MRRS. Completions are split per Read Completion Boundary.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/probe.c: pci_configure_mps, pcie_write_mps, pcie_write_mrrs, pcie_bus_configure_set, pcie_bus_configure_settings; drivers/pci/pci.c: pcie_bus_config and parsing of pcie_bus_*. Quirk: fixup_mpss_256 in quirks.c (limits MPSS to 256 for listed devices, including an ASMedia 0x0612 storage controller).
Test to provoke it
CAUTION/EXPERT: set mismatched MPS with setpci on a test machine and run a large read. Expect MalfTLP and a fatal reset.
L-33ACS misconfiguration — P2P blocked, redirected, or isolation silently missing SilentPCIe config
What happens
ACS has two failure directions. Too strict: P2P traffic (SSD ↔ GPU or NIC) is redirected upstream to the root complex, where it may be refused (UR or ACS Violation) or just run slowly through the IOMMU. Too loose: a switch without ACS lets an SSD DMA straight into a peer's BAR, bypassing the IOMMU. Isolation is broken with no error anywhere. A misprogrammed PRP that points at a peer BAR then corrupts that device silently.
How it shows up
Too strict: L-21 ACSViol, or P2P performance far below expectation. Too loose: nothing, apart from IOMMU groups that contain several devices (/sys/kernel/iommu_groups/*/devices).
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: ACS Source Validation, Translation Blocking, P2P Request Redirect, P2P Completion Redirect, Upstream Forwarding, P2P Egress Control, Direct Translated P2P.
Typical causes
Switches without an ACS capability. Firmware ACS settings that differ from what the OS expects. The pci=config_acs= override. VFIO setups.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv: ACSCap and ACSCtl on every downstream port. Linux logs Configured ACS to %#06x when it applies an override. Check IOMMU group membership.
In a trace
Too strict: P2P MWr going up to the root port and back down (doubling link traffic). Too loose: P2P TLPs turning around inside the switch without ever reaching the root.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pci.c: pci_request_acs, pci_std_enable_acs, pci_enable_acs, pci_acs_enabled, pci_acs_path_enabled, pci_acs_init.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: inventory ACS capabilities and IOMMU groups. CAUTION: P2PDMA tests with ACS on and off.
L-34ATS / PRI errors — translated-address or page-request failures ReportedPCIe Transaction
What happens
With Address Translation Services (ATS) the device caches IOVA→physical translations (in its ATC) and sends "translated" requests that the IOMMU does not check again. Failure modes: a stale ATC entry after the host unmapped and invalidated, where the device misses or acknowledges the invalidation too late, so DMA goes to freed memory (silent corruption). An Invalidate Completion that never arrives, so the IOMMU invalidation times out. With Page Request Interface (PRI), a page request that is never answered or that fails, so DMA waits forever.
How it shows up
Invalidation timeouts and page-request failures are reported by the IOMMU driver (Intel VT-d / AMD-Vi / Arm SMMU), not by AER. Exact message wording is uncertain here because those drivers are not in the local source tree. Stale-ATC DMA is silent. Most NVMe SSDs do not implement ATS or PRI, so this matters mainly for SVA/PASID-capable or computational storage devices impl.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: ATS (Translation Request/Completion, Invalidate Request/Completion), PRI (Page Request Message, PRG Response), PASID.
Typical causes
ATC invalidation bugs in device firmware. Invalidation queues saturated in the IOMMU. Page-request group bookkeeping bugs.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv: ATSCap/ATSCtl "Enable+", PRI capability, PASID capability. IOMMU fault logs. For stale-translation corruption: a read-back verify test (fio verify=crc32c) during heavy map/unmap churn (for example with the IOMMU in strict mode, iommu.strict=1).
In a trace
Translation Requests (MRd with AT=01b), translated requests (AT=10b), Invalidate Request messages, and missing or late Invalidate Completion messages.
Linux v7.2 source
ATS/PRI core (drivers/pci/ats.c) and IOMMU drivers are not in the local tree. Not verified here.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: an exerciser that delays Invalidate Completions; IOMMU stress with frequent unmaps during DMA.
L-35MSI / MSI-X misconfiguration — lost or misrouted interrupts Timer onlyPCIe / host IRQ
What happens
The SSD posts a CQE, then sends its interrupt as an MWr to the MSI or MSI-X address. If the vector is masked, has the wrong address or data, is blocked by interrupt remapping, or is never sent (a device bug), the CPU never runs the completion handler. The CQE sits in host memory, already written.
How it shows up
After io_timeout, Linux v7.2's nvme_timeout() first polls the CQ ("Did we miss an interrupt?" in the source). If the request was in fact completed, it logs I/O tag %d (%04x) QID %d timeout, completion polled and finishes the request with no reset. A single occurrence means a lost interrupt. Many mean systematic interrupt failure. Other signs: /proc/interrupts counts for the nvme0qN vectors that stay flat while I/O is issued; the MSI core's arch assigned 64-bit MSI address %#llx above device MSI address mask %#llx.
Spec basis
PCI/PCIe: MSI and MSI-X capability, MSI-X Table and Pending Bit Array, Function Mask, per-vector Mask. NVMe: the INTMS/INTMC registers mask pin-based and single-message MSI only (not MSI-X). The Create I/O CQ "IV" field selects the vector.
Typical causes
Devices whose MSI (not MSI-X) does not work at all. Linux has NVME_QUIRK_BROKEN_MSI for two SanDisk models (Chapter 27). Controllers that require all queues on vector 0 (NVME_QUIRK_SINGLE_VECTOR, Apple). Interrupt-remapping table errors. CPU hot-unplug with affinity mistakes. A device that sends the MWr before its CQE write is visible (an ordering bug), so the handler finds nothing and the next interrupt never comes. Coalescing settings too aggressive (Set Features Interrupt Coalescing).
How to detect / prove
Search dmesg for "completion polled". Compare /proc/interrupts deltas with I/O counts. lspci -vvv "MSI-X: Enable+ Count=N Masked-". Boot with nvme.use_threaded_interrupts=1 or use poll queues (nvme.poll_queues=N, with io_uring hipri) to see whether the problem disappears when interrupts are bypassed.
In a trace
Normal: CQE MWr then MSI-X MWr (to the 0xFEExxxxx range on x86). Bug: CQE MWr with no MSI-X MWr afterwards, or an MSI-X MWr whose address or data does not match the table entry programmed by the host.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_timeout (poll then "completion polled"), nvme_irq, nvme_irq_check, nvme_poll_irqdisable, nvme_setup_irqs (BROKEN_MSI clears PCI_IRQ_MSI; SINGLE_VECTOR uses one vector), nvme_pci_enable; drivers/pci/msi/msi.c: msi_verify_entries.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: mask an MSI-X vector through the table (with a debug tool) while I/O runs; expect "completion polled" after the timeout. SAFE: run with nvme.io_timeout lowered on a test box to shorten the experiment.
L-36IOMMU / DMAR fault — SSD DMA to an address the host did not map ReportedHost IOMMU
What happens
The SSD issues DMA (SQ fetch, PRP-list fetch, data read or write, CQE write, or the MSI address when not remapped) to an IOVA that has no valid mapping or no permission. The IOMMU blocks it. Blocked reads complete with an error (UR or CA, or a timeout at the device). Blocked writes are dropped. The NVMe command fails, or more often its CQE is lost and the host times out.
How it shows up
An IOMMU fault message naming the device's Requester ID and the faulting address. On Intel the message is commonly of the form DMAR: [DMA Read/Write] Request device [bb:dd.f] ... fault addr 0x... [fault reason 0xNN] ..., on AMD AMD-Vi: Event logged [IO_PAGE_FAULT device=... address=...]. These IOMMU driver strings were not verified against the local tree uncertain. The QEMU NVMe model shows what a careful device does when its own SQ or CQ DMA fails: nvme_process_sq() and nvme_post_cqes() trace pci_nvme_err_addr_read/_write and pci_nvme_err_cfs, then set CSTS to NVME_CSTS_FAILED. Real SSDs may instead set CFS, log an error, or carry on impl.
Spec basis
Platform IOMMU specifications (VT-d, AMD IOMMU, SMMU). NVMe: CSTS.CFS is the controller's way to say it can no longer operate. PCIe: completion statuses UR and CA.
Typical causes
Host driver bugs: use-after-unmap, wrong PRP list length, PRP offsets crossing into unmapped pages. Controller bugs: prefetching past the end of a PRP list, reading SQ entries beyond the queue size, DMA after a reset was requested (stale queue addresses). A device using the wrong Requester ID (see the DMA alias quirks in Chapter 27). NVME_QUIRK_DMA_ADDRESS_BITS_48: controllers that cannot DMA above 48 bits; Linux limits their DMA mask.
How to detect / prove
dmesg | grep -iE 'DMAR|AMD-Vi|iommu|smmu'. Match the fault address to an NVMe structure: run the nvme_setup_cmd tracepoint (/sys/kernel/tracing/events/nvme/) and look for the same IOVA in PRP1/PRP2. Rebooting with iommu=pt or the IOMMU off as a diagnostic can show whether faults turn into silent corruption, so verify the data.
In a trace
An SSD MRd or MWr to an address that does not match any PRP or queue base programmed in the preceding SQE or Create-queue commands.
Linux v7.2 source
nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_pci_alloc_dev sets dma_set_mask_and_coherent to 48 or 64 bits; drivers/pci/quirks.c: quirk_dma_func1_alias and related alias quirks; __pci_dev_specific_reset logs failed to stop IOMMU for a PCI reset: %d. QEMU: hw/nvme/ctrl.c nvme_process_sq, nvme_post_cqes.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: a user-space NVMe driver (vfio-based) that submits a command with a PRP pointing outside its mapped buffer. The IOMMU must fault, and the SSD's response (CFS, error status, or hang) is the result of the test.
L-37ASPM-related link drop or stall (L0s / L1 / L1 substates) Timer onlyPCIe Physical / PM
What happens
When idle, the link goes into L0s, L1 or L1.1/L1.2. On the next transaction it must wake up. If exit latency exceeds what the endpoint tolerates, or the exit fails (CLKREQ# handshake, reference clock not restored, a PHY bug), the link either stalls (replay timeouts, Recovery) or goes down entirely. Often this happens only after minutes or hours of idle, which makes it look random.
How it shows up
Mild cases: correctable Replay Timer Timeout and Receiver Errors after idle periods. Severe cases: SSD drops off, and the first I/O after idle times out with CSTS=0xffffffff and the driver hint Try "nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us=0 pcie_aspm=off pcie_port_pm=off" and report a bug. NVMe APST (controller power states) often combines with ASPM, and the quirk table (Chapter 27) has many "drops off the PCIe bus" entries for APST. Linux ASPM messages: ASPM: current common clock configuration is inconsistent, reconfiguring, ASPM: Could not configure common clock, ASPM: Link Capabilities L0s/L1 treated as unsupported to avoid device defect (from pcie_aspm_remove_cap() via quirks), can't disable ASPM; OS doesn't have ASPM control.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: ASPM L0s/L1, L1 PM Substates (L1.1, L1.2), LTR, exit latencies in Link Capabilities and Device Capabilities (acceptable latencies), Common Clock Configuration.
Typical causes
Platform firmware advertising L1.2 without proper CLKREQ# routing. Wrong T_POWER_ON or LTR values (Linux logs Invalid T_PwrOn scale). Device PHY bugs. Missing LTR restore after resume (no suspend buffer for LTR; ASPM issues possible after resume).
How to detect / prove
Diagnostic boots: pcie_aspm=off, or set /sys/module/pcie_aspm/parameters/policy to performance. Per-link control is under /sys/bus/pci/devices/<BDF>/link/ (for example l1_2_aspm; availability varies) version. lspci -vvv: LnkCtl "ASPM L1 Enabled", L1SubCtl1. An idle-then-burst test loop: sleep N seconds, issue a 4 KiB read, measure latency, raise N.
In a trace
PM_Active_State_Request_L1 DLLPs, electrical idle, then on wake an exit sequence (TS1s) that fails or takes too long. Analyzers need to handle electrical idle and CLKREQ# to capture this. Use a sideband probe on CLKREQ#.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/pci/pcie/aspm.c: pcie_aspm_remove_cap, common-clock configuration, policy parameter; quirks.c: quirk_disable_aspm_l0s, quirk_disable_aspm_l0s_l1 (including ASMedia 0x1080); nvme/host/pci.c: nvme_warn_reset.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: idle-burst loop with ASPM policy powersupersave versus performance, counting timeouts. CAUTION: run for hours; a drop may need a power cycle to recover.
L-38Poisoned data accepted and committed — poison "handled" by writing it SilentPCIe / NVMe data path
What happens
The SSD receives a poisoned CplD carrying write data (L-12). AER logs it at the SSD, but the controller's data path ignores the poison, writes the bad data to NAND and completes the Write with success. Later reads return the corrupt data with good status.
How it shows up
One AER Poisoned TLP line (non-fatal, no reset, and possibly rate-limited). NVMe CQE status Success. Nothing else.
Spec basis
PCIe says a receiver of a poisoned TLP must not use the data as if it were good (Data Poisoning rules). How an NVMe controller then fails the command is implementation-defined impl.
Typical causes
Firmware that does not propagate the poison indication from the PCIe core to the command engine.
How to detect / prove
Inject a poisoned completion for a known write (exerciser), then read back the LBA and check that either the write failed or the data is intact. Correlate field AER poison events with later application checksum errors.
In a trace
A CplD with EP=1 for the SSD's MRd of write data, followed by a successful CQE for that Write command. This is the smoking gun.
Linux v7.2 source
aer.c: pcie_do_recovery(..., pci_channel_io_normal, ...) for non-fatal; nvme_error_detected returns CAN_RECOVER, so the NVMe layer does nothing.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: exerciser or analyzer injection of EP on the CplD path; destructive to the target LBAs.
L-40Tag and ordering capability mismatch: Extended/10-bit tags, Relaxed Ordering errata DegradedPCIe config
What happens
Two related failures. (1) Tags: a requester using 8-bit (Extended) or 10-bit tags toward a completer or root that mishandles them gives wrong-tag completions (Unexpected Completion, Completion Timeout). Disabling them limits outstanding reads, so SSD read bandwidth drops at high queue depth. (2) Relaxed Ordering: some root complexes have errata with Relaxed Ordering (RO) TLPs. Linux disables RO for listed root ports and prints Disable Relaxed Ordering Attributes to avoid PCIe Completion erratum (the quirks.c comment cites a Flow Control credit issue on Broadwell/Haswell Xeon root complexes causing "performance problems").
How it shows up
probe.c messages enabling Extended Tags / disabling Extended Tags, Relaxed Ordering disabled because the Root Port didn't support it. Otherwise only lower bandwidth.
Spec basis
PCIe Base Specification: Extended Tag Field Enable, 10-Bit Tag Requester/Completer, Relaxed Ordering attribute, Enable Relaxed Ordering in Device Control.
Typical causes
Root complex errata; host-bridge flags (no_ext_tags); devices with limited completion buffers.
How to detect / prove
lspci -vvv DevCtl "ExtTag+ RlxdOrd+", DevCtl2 "10BitTagReq+". Compare fio random-read bandwidth at QD128 with and without those settings.
In a trace
Count of distinct outstanding MRd tags from the SSD. The RO bit in TLP Attr.
Linux v7.2 source
probe.c: pci_configure_extended_tags, Relaxed Ordering configuration; quirks.c: quirk_relaxedordering_disable.
Test to provoke it
SAFE: measure; CAUTION: toggle with setpci on a test system.
💡 Senior insight — the 30-second rule

Almost every card in this chapter ends in the same NVMe symptom: an I/O timeout 30 seconds later (Linux v7.2's default nvme_core.io_timeout). When you see an NVMe timeout, do not start with the SSD. Look backwards 30 seconds in the kernel log for AER, DPC, IOMMU, bwctrl or ASPM messages. If you find any, the timeout is a symptom. If you find none, check the "completion polled" message first (lost interrupt) before blaming the controller.

Interview questions

  1. Intermediate An SSD shows "completion polled" timeouts only after the system has been idle for an hour. Name two hypotheses and how to split them.
    Model answer

    (1) A lost MSI-X after a power-management transition (device bug, or vector masked across D3/L1.2 transitions). (2) ASPM or APST exit causing the device to post the CQE late or drop the interrupt. Split them by disabling APST (nvme_core.default_ps_max_latency_us=0) and ASPM (pcie_aspm=off) separately. Also check /proc/interrupts to see whether the vector fired at all.

  2. Senior Why might ECRC catch corruption that LCRC never sees?
    Model answer

    LCRC is per hop and regenerated by every switch. Corruption inside a switch's internal buffer happens after the ingress LCRC check and before the egress LCRC is computed, so the egress LCRC is valid over corrupt data. ECRC is computed end to end by the original requester and checked by the final receiver, so it covers the switch internals.

PART VII

Spec Violations You Can Only See in a Trace

Some failures never produce a status code, an AER bit or a log entry, yet they are plainly visible to anyone who looks at the wire: a doorbell value that points past the end of a queue, a completion that overtakes its own data, a command identifier used twice, a TLP bigger than the link allows. This part lists the rules that a protocol checker — a PCIe protocol analyzer, a PEX/interposer capture, or simply a script over an exported trace — can enforce mechanically. Each card names the rule, shows what the violation looks like in a trace, and explains why it matters even when "nothing failed".

7.1 How a Protocol Checker Thinks

← 24 Fabric Configuration, Interrupt and DMA-Translation Failures7.2 Queue and Doorbell Protocol Violations →

A protocol checker is a state machine that replays a captured trace and asks, for every packet: was this event legal given everything that happened before it? It does not need to know whether the drive returned the right data; it only needs the rules of the protocol and enough state to apply them. This makes it the perfect tool for a class of bugs that are invisible to the operating system: the host and the device can both "work" while one of them breaks a rule that the other happens to tolerate today and will not tolerate on the next firmware, the next switch, or the next CPU.

🧒 Kid analogy

A referee does not care who wins the football match. The referee only checks: was that pass offside? Did that player touch the ball with a hand? A protocol checker is a referee for the bus. It can blow the whistle even when the goal still counts.

🧩 Where the analogy breaks

A referee sees the whole pitch. A checker sees only what the probe captured: if the analyzer sits on the link between the root port and the SSD, it sees TLPs and DLLPs on that link, but it does not see what the CPU wrote into host memory unless that data later crosses the link (for example, when the controller fetches a submission queue entry). Many NVMe rules can therefore only be checked indirectly, by watching the controller read the memory.

7.1.1 What the wire actually shows for NVMe over PCIe

Every NVMe-over-PCIe interaction becomes a small set of TLP (Transaction Layer Packet) types. A checker decodes them back into NVMe events:

NVMe eventDirectionPCIe transaction on the wireWhat a decoder recovers
SQ tail doorbell / CQ head doorbellhost → deviceMWr (Memory Write), 4 bytes, to BAR0 offset 1000h + (2y)·(4<<CAP.DSTRD) for SQ y tail, 1000h + (2y+1)·(4<<CAP.DSTRD) for CQ y headqueue id, new tail/head value
Command fetchdevice → host, host → deviceMRd (Memory Read) of 64·n bytes from the SQ base, answered by CplD (Completion with Data)the 64-byte SQE: opcode, FUSE, PSDT, CID, NSID, PRP1/PRP2 or SGL1, CDW10–15
Read data to hostdevice → hostMWr TLPs to the PRP/SGL addresses, each ≤ MPSwhich CID it belongs to (by address match against decoded PRPs)
Write data from hostdevice → host, host → deviceMRd ≤ MRRS, answered by one or more CplDaddress, length, completer split pattern
Completion queue entrydevice → hostMWr of 16 bytes to CQ base + slot·16CID, SQID, SQHD, status, phase tag
Interruptdevice → hostMWr of the MSI-X message data to the MSI-X message addressvector number (by matching the MSI-X table, which the analyzer must have seen being programmed or be told)
Link healthbothDLLPs: Ack/Nak, UpdateFC, PM; Ordered sets; in Gen6 Flit mode: FLITs with CRC/FECreplays, NAKs, retrains, power state changes

A good analyzer does much of this decoding for you ("NVMe decode" or "transaction view"). A script over an exported CSV can do the rest, as Chapter 7.6 shows.

7.1.2 Three kinds of rule

  • Stateless rules look at one packet: TLP payload larger than MPS, read request larger than MRRS, request crossing a 4 KiB boundary, PRP2 with a non-zero offset, reserved bits set. Cheap, no false positives if the configuration (MPS, MRRS, page size) is known.
  • Stateful rules need history: CID reuse needs the set of outstanding commands; phase-tag checks need the CQ position and wrap count; Unexpected Completion needs the set of outstanding PCIe tags. The checker must start from a known state — ideally from controller enable (CC.EN 0→1) or queue creation — or it must "learn" state for a while and suppress findings until it is synchronised.
  • Ordering rules compare the timing of two related events: the CQE must not reach host memory before the data; the MSI-X must not arrive before the CQE it announces. These depend on where the probe sits: an analyzer on the SSD link sees the order in which the device sent the TLPs, which is what the PCIe ordering rules constrain; what the CPU observes also depends on the root complex.
💡 Senior insight

Most violations in this part are not reported by either side. The controller is allowed to ignore a bad doorbell; the host driver silently drops a CQE for an unknown CID with a log line; a PCIe receiver may or may not check the 4 KiB rule. "No error in dmesg" means only "nobody who checks was looking". The trace is the only witness — which is why a validation lab runs a checker on every capture, not only on failing ones.

7.1.3 What a real controller checks: evidence from the QEMU NVMe model

The QEMU emulated NVMe controller (hw/nvme/ctrl.c) is not a reference implementation of the specification, but it is an open, readable controller that encodes many of the rules in code, with comments citing the spec. It is the best public evidence of "what a controller is expected to check". The table summarises what it validates and — equally important — what it does not check. impl

Check (QEMU function)RuleReaction in QEMU
nvme_process_db: addr & 3doorbell writes must be 32-bit alignedguest-error trace pci_nvme_ub_db_wr_misaligned, write ignored, nothing reported to the host
nvme_process_db + nvme_check_cqidCQ head doorbell for a queue that does not existignored; if an AER is outstanding, posts an Error-type async event with info NVME_AER_INFO_ERR_INVALID_DB_REGISTER (0), log page Error Information
nvme_process_db: new_head >= cq->sizeCQ head value must be inside the queueignored; async event NVME_AER_INFO_ERR_INVALID_DB_VALUE (1) if an AER is outstanding
nvme_process_db + nvme_check_sqidSQ tail doorbell for a non-existent queueignored; Invalid Doorbell Register async event
nvme_process_db: new_tail >= sq->sizeSQ tail value must be inside the queueignored; Invalid Doorbell Write Value async event
not checkedSQ tail overrunning entries not yet fetched; SQ tail moving "backwards"; CQ head moving past the tailQEMU simply accepts the new value — the queue state silently becomes inconsistent
nvme_post_cqes: nvme_cq_fullnever post into a full CQ (one slot always empty)stops posting; resumes when the head doorbell frees space
nvme_map_prpPRP2 used as data pointer must have zero offset; PRP list entries must be page alignedstatus NVME_INVALID_PRP_OFFSET (13h) with DNR
nvme_map_sgl, nvme_map_sgl_datasegment descriptor length non-zero and a multiple of 16; Last Segment must end in data blocks; SGL must cover the transferstatus 0Dh / 0Eh / 0Fh / 11h with DNR (QEMU constant names in include/block/nvme.h)
nvme_admin_cmdSGLs shall not be used for Admin commands over PCIe; FUSE not supportedInvalid Field in Command
nvme_io_cmdFUSE bits set on an I/O command (fused ops unsupported in this model)Invalid Field in Command (no DNR)
nvme_check_mdtstransfer length ≤ page size << MDTSInvalid Field in Command with DNR
nvme_create_sqvalid CQID, unused SQID, 0 < QSIZE ≤ CAP.MQES, page-aligned PRP1, PC=1Invalid Queue Identifier / Completion Queue Invalid / Invalid Queue Size / PRP Offset Invalid / Invalid Field
not checkedCID unique among outstanding commands of an SQcommands proceed; nvme_abort would match the first request with that CID

Two lessons follow. First, the controller-side checks are almost all "ignore and maybe tell the host via an AER" — and the AER path only works if the host keeps an Asynchronous Event Request outstanding. Second, several host-side rules (CID uniqueness, monotonic SQ tail progress) are simply not policed by the device; the trace is the only place they can be caught.

7.1.4 How the cards in this part are organised

  • 7.2 queue and doorbell protocol (V-01 … V-12)
  • 7.3 ordering and interrupt rules (V-13 … V-16)
  • 7.4 command format: PRP, SGL, MDTS, FUSE, reserved fields (V-17 … V-25)
  • 7.5 PCIe Transaction, Data Link and Flit layer rules (V-26 … V-38)
  • 7.6 an executable checker over a synthetic CSV trace

The severity label on each card describes how the violation surfaces if nobody runs a checker: Reported if some register, status or log records it; Timer only if the only symptom is a timeout; Silent if nothing records it; Degraded if things keep working, slower.

7.2 Queue and Doorbell Protocol Violations

← 7.1 How a Protocol Checker Thinks7.3 Ordering and Interrupt Violations →

The NVMe queue model is a pair of circular buffers per queue pair with strict producer/consumer ownership: the host produces SQ entries and advances the SQ tail doorbell; the controller consumes them and reports its consumer position in the CQE's SQ Head Pointer (SQHD); the controller produces CQ entries, marking each new pass with an inverted Phase Tag; the host consumes them and returns slots by writing the CQ head doorbell. A queue with N entries holds at most N−1 items: "full" is (tail + 1) mod N == head, "empty" is head == tail. Every card below is a violation of this ownership contract. verified

Synthetic trace — a legal 4 KiB read on I/O queue 1 (queue size 4), for reference
t (ns)TLPDecodedChecker state after
0MWr host→dev, BAR0+1008h, 4 BSQ1 tail doorbell = 1SQ1 tail 1, 1 entry to fetch
310MRd dev→host, 64 B @ SQ1 basefetch slot 0—
620CplD host→dev, 64 BSQE: opc 02h Read, CID 0010h, PRP1 1000_0000h, NLB 7 (8×512 B)outstanding {(1,0010h)}
4900–530016 × MWr dev→host, 256 B eachread data to 1000_0000h … 1000_0FFFhCID 0010h: 4096/4096 B delivered
5400MWr dev→host, 16 B @ CQ1 base + 0CQE: CID 0010h, SQID 1, SQHD 1, status 0, P=1CQ1 tail 1, outstanding {}
5450MWr dev→host to MSI-X addressMSI-X vector 1—
6900MWr host→dev, BAR0+100Ch, 4 BCQ1 head doorbell = 1CQ1 head 1, empty
V-01SQ tail doorbell value beyond queue size ReportedNVMe queue
What happens
The host writes an SQ y Tail Doorbell with a value ≥ the number of entries of that SQ (for example tail = 70 on a 64-entry queue). The value cannot be a slot index.
How it shows up
The controller ignores the write. If an Asynchronous Event Request is outstanding it may post an Error-type async event "Invalid Doorbell Write Value" and log an entry in the Error Information log. If no AER is outstanding, nothing is reported — commands placed in the SQ are simply never fetched and later time out.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: SQ Tail Doorbell register semantics; asynchronous event information "Invalid Doorbell Write Value" (Error event type). QEMU quotes the rule from NVMe 1.3d in nvme_process_db: "If host software writes an invalid value to the Submission Queue Tail Doorbell or Completion Queue Head Doorbell register and an Asynchronous Event Request command is outstanding, then an asynchronous event is posted…".
Typical causes
Host driver modulo bug (wrap at 2n instead of queue size); queue resized by a Create I/O SQ with a different QSIZE than the driver uses (QSIZE is 0's based); memory corruption of the driver's tail variable; a user-space driver (SPDK-style) with a stale queue configuration after reset.
How to detect / prove
Checker rule: for each doorbell MWr, decode queue id from offset and compare the value against the QSIZE+1 seen in the Create I/O SQ command (or AQA.ASQS+1 for the admin queue). On Linux, look for nvme_async_event trace events and nvme error-log entries; Linux v7.2 records the result in ctrl->aen_result.
In a trace
MWr to BAR0 + 1000h + 2y·(4<<DSTRD) with data ≥ QSIZE+1; afterwards, no SQE fetch MRd from that SQ; possibly an AER CQE on the admin CQ with async event type Error.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_write_sq_db writes nvmeq->sq_tail, which nvme_sq_copy_cmd wraps at nvmeq->q_depth; drivers/nvme/host/core.c:nvme_complete_async_event handles NVME_AER_ERROR.
Test to provoke it
On QEMU (SAFE): use a user-space driver or nvme-cli is not enough — write the doorbell with a raw BAR mapping of an emulated device and watch the QEMU trace event pci_nvme_ub_db_wr_invalid_sqtail. On real hardware this is EXPERT: an unbound device, a vfio-based test harness, and an AER posted beforehand.
V-02CQ head doorbell value beyond queue size ReportedNVMe queue
What happens
The host writes a CQ y Head Doorbell with a value ≥ the CQ size. The controller cannot interpret how many entries were released.
How it shows up
Write ignored; optional Error async event "Invalid Doorbell Write Value". Because the head is not advanced, the controller eventually sees the CQ as full and stops posting completions: all I/O on every SQ that shares that CQ stalls and then times out.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CQ Head Doorbell semantics; same asynchronous event as V-01.
Typical causes
Host increments head past the end instead of wrapping; head computed in bytes instead of entries; CQ created with fewer entries than the driver assumes.
How to detect / prove
Same checker rule as V-01, against the CQ size from Create I/O CQ (QSIZE+1) or AQA.ACQS+1. Correlate: after the bad doorbell, CQE MWr count on that CQ stops at exactly size−1 outstanding entries.
In a trace
MWr to BAR0 + 1000h + (2y+1)·(4<<DSTRD) with data ≥ size; later, SQE fetches continue but CQE writes stop — the classic "device went quiet" picture.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_update_cq_head wraps cq_head at q_depth and flips cq_phase; nvme_ring_cq_doorbell writes it.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): raw doorbell write of size+2 on an idle CQ; observe pci_nvme_ub_db_wr_invalid_cqhead and, with an AER outstanding, an async event on the admin CQ.
V-03Doorbell write for a queue that does not exist ReportedNVMe queue
What happens
The host writes a doorbell offset belonging to a queue id that was never created, was already deleted, or exceeds the number of queues granted by Set Features Number of Queues.
How it shows up
Ignored by the controller; optional Error async event "Invalid Doorbell Register". QEMU's comment notes that the spec defines this code but "nowhere does it specify when to use it", and QEMU uses it for non-existent queues — so other controllers may report it differently or not at all. impl
Spec basis
NVMe Base: doorbell register layout (1000h + (2y)·(4<<CAP.DSTRD)); asynchronous event information "Invalid Doorbell Register".
Typical causes
Wrong CAP.DSTRD (doorbell stride) used by the host, so every doorbell lands on the wrong queue; queue deleted while another CPU still submits; use-after-teardown during reset; SR-IOV VF using PF queue numbering.
How to detect / prove
Checker keeps the set of live SQ/CQ ids from Create/Delete I/O SQ/CQ commands and their successful CQEs; any doorbell outside the set is flagged. A wrong stride shows as doorbells at offsets that are not multiples of 4<<DSTRD apart.
In a trace
Doorbell MWr after the Delete I/O SQ CQE for that queue, or to an offset beyond the highest created queue; no subsequent MRd from any SQ.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_create_queue sets up per-queue q_db pointers from dev->db_stride; teardown paths in nvme_dev_disable.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): write SQ tail doorbell of queue id conf_ioqpairs+1; QEMU trace pci_nvme_ub_db_wr_invalid_sq. A synthetic example appears as rule DB-NOQ in 7.6.
V-04Misaligned or partial doorbell write SilentNVMe register
What happens
A doorbell is written with a 1- or 2-byte access, or at an address that is not dword aligned, or a 64-bit write spans two doorbells (SQ tail and CQ head of the same pair).
How it shows up
Nothing is reported. QEMU logs a guest-error trace (pci_nvme_ub_db_wr_misaligned) and ignores the write; real controllers may ignore, latch only some bytes, or update both doorbells. impl
Spec basis
NVMe Base: controller registers are accessed with their natural width; doorbells are 32-bit registers. Accesses of other sizes are outside the defined behaviour.
Typical causes
Compiler merging two adjacent 32-bit stores into one 64-bit store on a write-combining mapping; a user-space driver using memcpy to MMIO; byte-wise BAR access tools.
How to detect / prove
Checker: doorbell MWr whose length ≠ 4 bytes, whose first/last byte enables are not 1111b, or whose address is not 4-byte aligned.
In a trace
MWr to the doorbell region with Length = 2 DW (both doorbells) or byte enables ≠ Fh.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_write_sq_db and nvme_ring_cq_doorbell use writel (32-bit).
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): 16-bit write to a doorbell; check QEMU trace output.
V-05SQ tail moves backwards or overruns unfetched entries SilentNVMe queue
What happens
The new SQ tail value implies either a negative number of new entries (tail moved "backwards", i.e. the host withdrew entries) or so many new entries that the SQ would hold ≥ N items — the host has overwritten SQEs the controller had not yet fetched.
How it shows up
Nothing reported: the value is in range, so V-01 does not fire. Modulo arithmetic hides the problem — a backwards move looks like "N−k new commands". Consequences range from duplicated commands (the controller re-executes stale SQEs from a previous pass, e.g. a stale Write re-writes old data) to lost commands that time out.
Spec basis
NVMe Base queue model: the host shall not submit to a full SQ (full = tail+1 == head); entries between head and tail are owned by the controller and must not be modified by the host. The host learns the head from SQHD in CQEs.
Typical causes
Two CPUs submitting to one SQ without a lock; driver uses CQ depth for flow control while CQ is shared by several SQs; SQHD ignored and SQ larger than outstanding-request budget assumed; a reset path that zeroes the driver's tail but not the controller's.
How to detect / prove
Checker: keep host tail T, controller head H (from the most recent SQHD and from observed fetches); for a new tail T', new = (T'−T) mod N; flag if new > (N−1) − ((T−H) mod N). Also flag if the data of a re-fetched slot is identical to an already-completed SQE (stale replay).
In a trace
Doorbell value sequence such as 5, 9, 7 on a 64-entry SQ with no wrap in between; the controller fetches 62 entries in one burst; CQEs with CIDs that already completed earlier.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_sq_copy_cmd under nvmeq->sq_lock; nvme_commit_rqs rings only when sq_tail != last_sq_tail. Linux sizes SQ and CQ identically and limits outstanding requests via blk-mq tags, so it does not need SQHD for flow control (it only passes it to trace_nvme_sq).
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): QEMU does not check this (see 7.1.3) — write tail values out of order and observe re-executed commands; a good negative test for your checker.
V-06CQ head released past the controller's tail SilentNVMe queue
What happens
The host writes a CQ head that releases more entries than the controller has posted. The controller now believes there is free space that actually contains its own future slots; its full/empty calculation is corrupted.
How it shows up
No status. Later the controller may overwrite CQEs the host has not yet consumed (because it thinks they were released), producing lost completions (timeouts) or, combined with phase-tag reuse, stale completions processed twice.
Spec basis
NVMe Base queue model: the host releases only entries it has consumed (entries with the current phase).
Typical causes
Host consumes entries by index without checking the phase tag (polling bug); host and controller disagree on CQ size; a missed wrap in the host.
How to detect / prove
Checker counts CQE MWr per CQ (device tail) and compares every CQ head doorbell: released = (H'−H) mod N must be ≤ (Tail−H) mod N. Rule CQDB-PAST-TAIL in 7.6.
In a trace
CQ head doorbell value that is ahead of the number of 16-byte CQE MWr TLPs written into that CQ.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_poll_cq consumes entries only while nvme_cqe_pending (phase match) and rings the head once, after the loop.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE) with a test harness: post one command, write CQ head = 3. QEMU will not complain; your checker should.
V-07CQ overflow: head not advancing, or controller posts into a full CQ Timer onlyNVMe queue
What happens
Two variants. (a) The host stops advancing the CQ head (interrupt lost, CPU stuck, driver bug); the CQ fills to N−1 entries and the controller must stop posting — I/O stalls. (b) The controller posts while the CQ is full, overwriting an entry the host has not consumed.
How it shows up
(a) Only the host's I/O timeout, often with Linux "completion polled" if the CQE was actually there (see V-15). (b) Nothing; the overwritten completion is lost and its command times out; the overwriting entry may carry the "wrong" phase from the host's point of view.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CQ full condition (head == tail+1); the controller does not post to a full CQ and the host is responsible for sizing CQs so that the commands that can be outstanding on its SQs fit. QEMU nvme_post_cqes breaks out of its loop when nvme_cq_full.
Typical causes
(a) Missing MSI-X (V-15), interrupt affinity to an offline CPU, softirq starvation. (b) Controller firmware not tracking the CQ head doorbell; several SQs mapped to one small CQ.
How to detect / prove
Checker: keep CQ tail (count of CQE writes) and head (doorbells); flag a CQE write when (tail+1) mod N == head. For (a), flag "CQ head unchanged for X ms while CQ non-empty".
In a trace
(a) N−1 CQE MWr, then silence on that CQ while SQ fetches continue to be possible; no CQ head doorbell. (b) a CQE MWr to the slot just before the current head.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_timeout polls the CQ first and prints "I/O tag %d (%04x) QID %d timeout, completion polled" when the CQE was present.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): issue N commands while never writing the CQ head; verify QEMU stops at N−1 CQEs. On hardware, the (b) variant needs an analyzer to prove.
V-08Command Identifier reused while still outstanding SilentNVMe command
What happens
The host submits a command whose CID equals that of a command still outstanding on the same SQ. Completions become ambiguous: the host cannot tell which of the two finished, and Abort (which names SQID+CID) becomes ambiguous too.
How it shows up
Usually nothing. A controller may detect it and complete the new command with Generic status 03h "Command ID Conflict" (Linux NVME_SC_CMDID_CONFLICT); many controllers do not check. QEMU does not check (its nvme_abort would match the first request with that CID). impl
Spec basis
NVMe Base: the Command Identifier together with the SQ Identifier uniquely identifies a command outstanding at the controller; Generic Command Status "Command ID Conflict".
Typical causes
Host reusing a tag before its completion was processed; driver completing a request on timeout and reusing the tag while the controller still owns the original; user-space drivers with fixed CIDs.
How to detect / prove
Checker keeps the outstanding set per SQ: add (SQID,CID) on SQE fetch, remove on CQE. Flag duplicates (rule CID-REUSE in 7.6). Linux encodes a 4-bit generation counter into the top bits of the CID, so a reused tag normally has a different CID — a duplicate in a Linux trace is strong evidence of a real bug.
In a trace
Two decoded SQEs on the same SQ with equal CID and no CQE for that CID between them.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/nvme.h:nvme_cid = nvme_cid_install_genctr(genctr) | rq->tag; nvme_find_rq checks the generation counter.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE) user-space harness: submit two long reads with the same CID; verify the checker flags it; on hardware, check whether the controller returns status 03h.
V-09CQE for an unknown or already-completed CID ReportedNVMe command
What happens
The controller writes a completion whose CID matches no outstanding command on that SQ — a duplicate completion, a corrupted CID, or a completion for a command the host already aborted/timed out.
How it shows up
Linux v7.2 logs "invalid id %d completed on queue %d" (or "request %#x genctr mismatch (got … expected …)") and drops the entry. Other drivers may complete the wrong request — a silent data-integrity hazard (the wrong buffer is declared ready).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: each command is completed exactly once by one CQE identifying it by SQID+CID.
Typical causes
Controller firmware posting a completion twice (retry path); CQE corrupted in flight (bit flip in DRAM or a poisoned TLP ignored); host timeout handling that freed the tag while the command was still executing.
How to detect / prove
Checker rule CQE-UNKNOWN. In Linux, grep dmesg for "invalid id" and "genctr mismatch". A trace distinguishes "duplicate" (CID seen in an earlier CQE) from "never submitted" (CID never fetched).
In a trace
A 16-byte CQE MWr whose DW3 CID has no matching earlier SQE fetch, or matches a CID already completed.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_handle_cqe; drivers/nvme/host/nvme.h:nvme_find_rq.
Test to provoke it
QEMU fault injection is not built in; use a modified QEMU or an FPGA/emulator that duplicates a CQE (EXPERT). In the synthetic example, CID 0x99 triggers it.
V-10Wrong SQID or non-advancing SQHD in a CQE SilentNVMe command
What happens
The CQE's SQ Identifier (DW2 bits 31:16) names a different SQ than the one the command came from, or SQ Head Pointer (DW2 bits 15:0) moves backwards, beyond the tail, or never advances although commands are being consumed.
How it shows up
With a shared CQ, a wrong SQID makes the host look up the CID in the wrong SQ — wrong request completed or "invalid id". A frozen SQHD makes a host that uses SQHD for flow control believe the SQ is full: throughput collapses to zero without any error. Linux, which does not use SQHD for flow control, is unaffected — so the same SSD "works on Linux, hangs on another OS".
Spec basis
NVMe Base: CQE DW2 SQID and SQHD semantics — SQHD indicates the current SQ head pointer for the SQ named by SQID.
Typical causes
Firmware filling SQHD from a stale variable; CQE template reused across queues; SQHD reported before the fetch actually advanced the head.
How to detect / prove
Checker: SQID must equal the SQ the CID was fetched from; SQHD must satisfy (SQHD − previous SQHD) mod N ≤ number of fetches since, and must never pass the host tail.
In a trace
CQE DW2 with SQID ≠ origin queue; or SQHD values like 3, 3, 3, 3 while 20 SQEs were fetched.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_handle_cqe passes cqe->sq_head to trace_nvme_sq only and uses cqe->sq_id in the "invalid id" message.
Test to provoke it
Use a driver that honours SQHD (e.g. a user-space test harness) and an SQ much smaller than the outstanding-command budget; a broken SQHD will deadlock it quickly.
V-11Phase tag not inverted on CQ wrap Timer onlyNVMe queue
What happens
After the controller wraps from the last CQ slot to slot 0 it must invert the Phase Tag (P, bit 0 of the status half of DW3). If it keeps the old value, the host — which expects the new phase — sees the new entry as "old" and does not consume it; if it inverts too early, the host consumes stale entries.
How it shows up
Missing inversion: completions invisible to the host → I/O timeout (Linux may report "completion polled" only if the phase matches; here it does not, so a plain timeout and abort/reset follow). Early inversion: stale CQEs processed twice → "invalid id" messages or double completion.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Phase Tag "identifies whether a completion queue entry is new"; it is inverted each pass through the CQ; host initialises the CQ memory to zero so the first pass uses P=1.
Typical causes
Firmware wrap logic off by one; CQ size mismatch between host and controller; controller reset without the host re-zeroing CQ memory.
How to detect / prove
Checker keeps tail and expected phase per CQ (start 1, invert at wrap) and compares every CQE (rule PHASE in 7.6). Note that a phase error is often the symptom of an earlier V-07(b) overflow; always report the first abnormal event.
In a trace
First CQE written to slot 0 after a wrap carries P=1 when the previous pass also used P=1.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_cqe_pending compares status & 1 with nvmeq->cq_phase; nvme_update_cq_head flips cq_phase at wrap.
Test to provoke it
Run enough I/O to wrap a small CQ many times (e.g. io_queue_depth=2 module parameter — the minimum Linux accepts — on a test system, SAFE) and verify no phase findings in the capture.
V-12Shadow doorbell buffer out of sync with the MMIO doorbell SilentNVMe queue
What happens
With the Doorbell Buffer Config admin command (used mainly by emulated/virtual controllers), the host writes doorbell values into a shadow buffer in host memory and only rings the real MMIO doorbell when the controller's EventIdx says so. If the host updates one but not the other, or ignores EventIdx, the controller works from a stale tail/head.
How it shows up
Stalls that recover when any later doorbell is rung; latency spikes; no error. QEMU's nvme_process_db comments that "various drivers including ones that run on Linux, are not updating Admin Queues" in the shadow buffer, so QEMU writes the admin values back itself. impl
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Doorbell Buffer Config command — Shadow Doorbell buffer and EventIdx buffer; QEMU quotes "the host shall also update the controller's corresponding doorbell property to match the value of that entry in the Shadow Doorbell buffer".
Typical causes
Missing memory barrier between the shadow write and the EventIdx read; admin queue excluded from shadowing in the driver; migration of a VM between hosts.
How to detect / prove
In a VM, the "trace" is QEMU's own tracing (pci_nvme_* events) or a vhost/vfio capture. Compare shadow value reads (DMA reads of the shadow buffer) with MMIO doorbells.
In a trace
Controller DMA reads of the shadow buffer returning values different from the last MMIO doorbell for that queue, followed by a period with no fetches.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_dbbuf_update_and_check_event, called from nvme_write_sq_db and nvme_ring_cq_doorbell.
Test to provoke it
QEMU (SAFE): enable shadow doorbells (they are used automatically when the controller supports the command) and stress small-queue workloads while tracing pci_nvme_mmio_doorbell_sq.

7.3 Ordering and Interrupt Violations

← 7.2 Queue and Doorbell Protocol Violations7.4 Command-Format Violations: PRP, SGL, MDTS, FUSE and Reserved Fields →

NVMe over PCIe relies on one PCIe ordering guarantee above all others: a posted Memory Write must not pass an earlier posted Memory Write in the same direction unless the later TLP has the Relaxed Ordering (RO) attribute set (and the path honours it). Data MWr TLPs, the CQE MWr and the MSI-X MWr are all posted writes from the device to the host, so if the device sends them in the order data → CQE → MSI-X without RO on the CQE and MSI-X, the host is guaranteed to observe them in that order. The producer–consumer correctness of every NVMe read rests on this. verified

V-13Completion posted before the data writes (ordering) SilentNVMe / PCIe ordering
What happens
For a Read, the controller writes the CQE (or sets RO on data or CQE TLPs in a way that lets the CQE overtake) before all data MWr TLPs for that command have been sent. The host sees "success", hands the buffer to the application, and the application reads stale bytes; the real data arrives microseconds later, after the application has already used the buffer.
How it shows up
Nothing in any log. Symptoms are intermittent data corruption that disappears when tracing is enabled (timing changes) and checksum failures at the application level, typically on the last TLP(s) of a transfer.
Spec basis
PCI Express Base: ordering rules for posted requests (a posted request must not pass another posted request unless RO is set) and the Relaxed Ordering attribute; NVMe Base: a completion indicates the command's data transfer is complete.
Typical causes
Controller DMA engine with several parallel channels and the CQE sent from a different channel; RO set on the CQE write; a switch or root complex that reorders RO TLPs (legally) combined with the device setting RO where it must not.
How to detect / prove
Checker: map each data MWr to its CID (address ∈ the command's PRP/SGL ranges); at each CQE, require delivered bytes == transfer length (rule CQE-BEFORE-DATA in 7.6). Also flag RO=1 on any CQE or MSI-X TLP. Prove at the application level with fio --verify=crc32c --verify_fatal=1 on reads of data written earlier.
In a trace
CQE MWr for CID X timestamped before the last data MWr whose address lies in CID X's PRP list; or attribute field RO=1 in the CQE TLP header.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_poll_cq issues dma_rmb() between the phase check and reading the rest of the CQE — this protects against CPU reordering of the CQE fields, not against a device that wrote the CQE too early. Relaxed ordering enable bit: include/uapi/linux/pci_regs.h:PCI_EXP_DEVCTL_RELAX_EN.
Test to provoke it
CAUTION: toggle Device Control "Enable Relaxed Ordering" with setpci on an idle test device and run a verify workload with an analyzer attached; never on a system with data you care about.
V-14MSI-X sent before the CQE it announces DegradedNVMe interrupt
What happens
The interrupt MWr reaches the host before the CQE MWr (or there is no new CQE at all). The interrupt handler polls the CQ, finds nothing with the expected phase, and returns.
How it shows up
Spurious interrupts: Linux nvme_irq returns IRQ_NONE; if this happens very often the kernel's spurious-interrupt detector can disable the IRQ line ("nobody cared"), after which completions are found only by the timeout handler — then V-07(a) and "completion polled" messages. Before that point: extra latency, because the real CQE waits for the next interrupt.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: interrupts are generated for new CQ entries; PCI Express Base posted-write ordering ensures the MSI-X MWr cannot pass the earlier CQE MWr if the device sends them in order without RO.
Typical causes
Interrupt sent from a different internal engine than the CQE; interrupt coalescing logic counting submissions instead of completions; RO set on the MSI-X write.
How to detect / prove
Checker rule MSIX-NO-CQE: each MSI-X for vector v must be preceded by at least one new CQE on a CQ mapped to v since the previous MSI-X. On Linux, compare per-vector counts in /proc/interrupts with completions; spurious counts show in /proc/irq/<n>/spurious.
In a trace
MWr to the MSI-X address with no preceding CQE MWr; or CQE MWr timestamped after the MSI-X MWr.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_irq (returns IRQ_NONE when nvme_poll_cq finds nothing), nvme_irq_check for threaded interrupts.
Test to provoke it
Device-side bug; to validate the checker, use the synthetic trace in 7.6 (line with MSI-X at t=1500 ns).
V-15CQE posted but no MSI-X ever sent Timer onlyNVMe interrupt
What happens
The controller writes a CQE but never sends the interrupt (vector masked in the MSI-X table, wrong vector programmed in Create I/O CQ, function-mask bit set, or firmware bug).
How it shows up
Linux v7.2: the request times out; nvme_timeout polls the CQ, finds the completion and prints "I/O tag … QID … timeout, completion polled" — the signature of a lost interrupt. Latency equals the timeout (default 30 s for I/O).
Spec basis
NVMe Base: Create I/O Completion Queue Interrupt Vector (IV) and Interrupts Enabled (IEN) fields; PCI MSI-X table and Pending Bit Array semantics (a masked vector sets its pending bit instead of sending).
Typical causes
MSI-X vector masked during CPU hotplug or IRQ migration and never unmasked; IV out of range of the MSI-X table size; interrupt remapping (IOMMU) misconfiguration so the write is blocked.
How to detect / prove
Checker: CQE written, no MSI-X for the vector within a window (e.g. 1 ms) while IEN=1 for that CQ. Host-side: "completion polled" in dmesg; read the MSI-X PBA through the BAR (read-only inspection) to see pending bits.
In a trace
CQE MWr followed by silence; next activity is the host's Abort or controller reset seconds later.
Linux v7.2 source
drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_timeout; vector assignment in nvme_create_queue / queue_irq_offset.
Test to provoke it
EXPERT: mask a vector in the MSI-X table of a test device during I/O; expect "completion polled" and recovery.
V-16DMA to a command's buffer after its completion SilentNVMe / DMA
What happens
The controller writes to (or reads from) host memory belonging to a command after it has posted that command's CQE — for example the tail of the data for a read, or a retry of a data read for a write. By then the host may have freed or reused the buffer.
How it shows up
Random memory corruption elsewhere in the system (the page has been reallocated), IOMMU faults if the mapping was torn down (DMAR/AMD-Vi fault messages in dmesg naming the device), or nothing at all.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: posting the completion transfers ownership of the data buffers back to the host; PRP/SGL entries are valid only while the command is outstanding.
Typical causes
Same as V-13 (parallel DMA channels); Abort handling that completes the command while a DMA is still queued; firmware retry after timeout.
How to detect / prove
Checker rule DMA-NOCMD: any data MWr/MRd whose address belongs to a command that has already completed (and no newer command owns that address). With an IOMMU in strict mode, a late DMA produces a translation fault — strong host-side evidence.
In a trace
CQE for CID X followed by MWr TLPs into CID X's buffer range.
Linux v7.2 source
Unmapping on completion in drivers/nvme/host/pci.c:nvme_pci_complete_rq / nvme_pci_complete_batch.
Test to provoke it
Boot a test host with iommu.strict=1 (or the platform equivalent) and run high-QD mixed I/O; any IOMMU fault naming the SSD is a finding to correlate with a trace.

7.4 Command-Format Violations: PRP, SGL, MDTS, FUSE and Reserved Fields

← 7.3 Ordering and Interrupt Violations7.5 PCIe Transaction, Data Link and Flit Layer Violations →

Once the checker decodes each 64-byte submission queue entry (SQE) from the CplD that answers the controller's fetch, it can validate the command format exactly as the controller should. The rules below are all visible in the SQE bytes and in the PRP-list / SGL-segment reads that follow. Byte layout reminders: CDW0 = opcode (bits 7:0), FUSE (bits 9:8), PSDT (bits 15:14), CID (bits 31:16); NSID at bytes 4–7; MPTR at bytes 16–23; DPTR (PRP1, PRP2 or SGL1) at bytes 24–39; CDW10–15 at bytes 40–63. verified

7.4.1 PRP rules a checker can enforce

RuleDetailQEMU evidence
PRP1 may have an offsetOffset into the first memory page; must be dword aligned (bits 1:0 = 0)nvme_map_prp: trans_len = page_size - (prp1 % page_size)
If the transfer fits in PRP1's page, PRP2 is unusedContent of PRP2 is then ignored (reserved)no read of PRP2 when len becomes 0
If the remainder fits in one more page, PRP2 is a data pointerMust have zero offset (page aligned)prp2 & (page_size-1) → NVME_INVALID_PRP_OFFSET | NVME_DNR
Otherwise PRP2 points to a PRP listThe list pointer may have an offset; entries are page alignedcomment "The first PRP list entry, pointed to by PRP2 may contain offset"; each entry checked for zero offset
List chainingWhen more entries are needed than fit in the rest of the list page, the last entry of that page points to the next list pageif (i == nents - 1 && len > n->page_size) → next list
Page size"Page" = CC.MPS memory page size (212+MPS), not the CPU page sizen->page_size
V-17PRP1 not dword aligned, or PRP list entry with an offset ReportedNVMe command
What happens
PRP1 has bits 1:0 set, or an entry inside a PRP list (other than as allowed for the list pointer) has a non-zero page offset.
How it shows up
A checking controller completes the command with Generic status 13h "PRP Offset Invalid" (Linux NVME_SC_PRP_INVALID_OFFSET), usually with DNR. A non-checking controller DMAs to the wrong address — silent corruption of unrelated memory.
Spec basis
NVMe Base: PRP Entry format (offset field; dword alignment), PRP List rules; Generic Command Status "PRP Offset Invalid".
Typical causes
Host builds PRPs from CPU page size (e.g. 16 KiB or 64 KiB pages on arm64/ppc64) while the controller is configured with CC.MPS = 4 KiB, or vice versa; byte-granular user buffers passed through a passthrough interface.
How to detect / prove
Checker: decode DPTR, fetch the PRP list via the MRd/CplD that follows, and test each entry against the rules in the table above. Host-side: nvme error-log shows status 13h with the CID and SQID.
In a trace
SQE CplD with PRP1 low bits ≠ 0; or a PRP list read (MRd from the address in PRP2) returning entries with non-zero low 12 bits.
Linux v7.2 source
